A Hilbert Bundles Description of Complex Brunn-Minkowski Theory
Tai Terje Huu Nguyen

Abstract
. We present an abstract and novel approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory (see [ 2 ] , [ 3 ] , [ 4 ] , [ 7 ] for some first references) in a general setting using a theory (or formalism) of Hilbert bundles. Our approach is based on and inspired by a new approach of Berndtsson to the variation of Bergman kernels in [ 2 ] that does not rely on the (classical use of the) deep Hamilton-Kohn regularity theory for the ∂ ¯ \overline{\partial} -Neumann operator associated with smoothly bounded strictly pseudoconvex domains (see the proof of Lemma 2.1 in [ 2 ] ). This is the first installment in a series of papers, and we establish in here the language and formalism, as well as some rudimentary results, that we use in subsequent papers.



Preface

It has been a great pleasure, and an honor, to work with both of you, and I hope our collaboration continues in the future.

Finally, my greatest gratitude goes to my parents and my family, to whom I owe everything I am today.

Tai Terje Huu Nguyen,

Trondheim, December 2022.

Introduction

The theme of the following thesis is two-fold. One of the folds concerns Hilbert bundles, or more specifically, trivial Hilbert bundles with smooth weighted hermitian metrics (
[
15
]
), while the other fold concerns complex Brunn-Minkowski theory (see for instance
[
2
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
7
]
). The complex Brunn-Minkowski theory is a theory introduced by Berndtsson, and deals with the positivity of curvatures of certain vector bundles of infinite rank associated with Bergman (type of) spaces (
[
4
]
). Parts of the theory may be viewed as complex analogues of the real-variables (functional version of the) Brunn-Minkowski inequality from convex analysis (for convex functions) (
[
4
]
,
[
7
]
). An aspect (of the theory) that we find to be particularly beautiful and interesting, is that rather deep results may be obtained by using relatively simple convexity arguments. One of our favourite examples here is a ”complex Brunn-Minkowski proof” of the Suita conjecture, and building on the same ideas, an extension theorem of Ohsawa-Takegoshi type with optimal estimates, first demonstrated by Berndtsson and Lempert in
[
13
]
. With the language and formalism that we shall develop, the optimal estimate in the extension theorem can be seen to follow from general monotonicity properties of (weighted) norms of holomorphic sections of certain trivial Hilbert quotient bundles. To explain this, we first take a dual point of view and consider sections of the dual bundle of the quotient bundle. By a quotient bundle curvature formula, which we shall detail (see
[
15
]
), if the initial Hilbert bundle is (semi)positive (in the sense of Griffiths), then so is the quotient bundle. By complex Brunn-Minkowski theory, this positivity now implies (or rather means) that all holomorphic sections of the dual bundle (of the quotient bundle) satisfy that their squared norm is logarithmically plurisubharmonic as a function of the base parameter. Choosing as base parameter the real part of the left half-plane in the complex plane this yields that the norms (squared) are logarithmically convex. In some special situations, one may verify that as a function of the base parameter, the sums of logarithms of the norms and some linear terms are bounded from above as the parameter tends to negative infinity. By the convexity from before, these sums must then be monotonically increasing functions of the base parameter. This latter monotonicity finally gives the optimal estimates, after turning back to the norms on the quotient bundle (times some exponential terms). Very interestingly (in our opinion), one finds that analogous arguments may be used to address also (the) strong openness (conjecture of Demailly) (
[
17
]
).

This thesis consists of a collection of 4 papers, each of which corresponds to its own chapter. In chapter 1, the first paper (
[
15
]
), we introduce what we call a ”Hilbert bundles approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory”. We develop and introduce here a formalism, or theory, of Hilbert bundles, more specifically, what we call trivial Hilbert bundles with smooth weighted hermitian metrics, and show how to use this formalism to give an abstract approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory in a general setting. Our approach is rooted in and inspired by a new approach to the variation of Bergman kernels in
[
2
]
(which might be said to be the starting point of complex Brunn-Minkowski theory) that does not rely on the classical use of the deep regularity properties of the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann operator associated with smoothly bounded strictly pseudoconvex domains. Instead, we use a smoothness theorem of Berndtsson for the Bergman projection map. We generalize this smoothness theorem to the setting of trivial Hilbert bundles with certain special weights, and discuss a few applications to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory, as well as some novel results akin to the variations of Bergman kernels. At this point we are only able to obtain these results presuming certain positivity properties of the curvatures of certain subbundles. These presumed positivity assumptions are addressed in a more concrete setting in the sequel paper and chapter 2 of the thesis.

Chapter 2, the second paper (
[
16
]
), is a continuation of chapter 1 and the first paper. We address here the presumed positivity assumptions in (some of) the main results from the first chapter in more concrete settings. In particular, we generalize the positivity of direct image bundles from
[
3
]
, in the special case of trivial fibrations, to the case of
(n,q)
-forms with
q\geq 0
(and not just
q=0
), and a more general class of Kähler manifolds. This class is the class of so-called quasi-complete Kähler manifolds, a notion which generalizes that of complete Kähler manifolds, and which is due to Xu Wang and Bo Yong Chen (
[
38
]
). To give a fairly general statement, we introduce a type of Hörmander estimate condition. The main idea here, similar to Berndtsson’s approach in
[
3
]
, is to use
L^{2}
-methods to control the second fundamental form term in a formula for the curvature of the subbundle (
[
15
]
), viewing it as the norm of the
L^{2}
(type of) minimal solution to a certain linear equation. The actual computations and end-results, however, differ from those that appear in
[
3
]
. We also give a novel plurisubharmonicity result for holomorphic sections of certain trivial Hilbert quotient bundles in the same setting, which we prove using similar ideas. Very briefly stated, and using the formalism and notation introduced in the first paper (
[
15
]
) and chapter 1, this result says that under certain conditions on the right-hand side of an equation of the form
\overline{\partial}(\cdot)=v
(that is, on
v
) that relates to an appropriate smooth weighted hermitian metric
h
,
-f(\left|\left|u_{m}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
admits plurisubharmonicity properties for all strictly increasing concave and twice differentiable functions
f
on
[0,\infty)
, where
u_{m}
denotes the
h
-minimal solution to the equation. It might be that this latter result is related to variations of so-called generalized Green functions, but the details are far from clear to us. We hope maybe to return to say more about this in a future paper. Finally, by considering special kinds of hermitian metrics on our Hilbert bundles, we prove versions of the above results where the metric on the line bundle may be possibly singular. The possibly non-smoothness of the line bundle metric is a main novelty, and we deal with it using a Hörmander type of theorem for singular weights, on quasi-complete Kähler manifolds. To obtain the latter, we use regularization of quasi-plurisubharmonic currents on compact manifolds, due to Demailly. The special form of the hermitian metrics that we consider plays a rather essential role here. We conclude the paper with a few comments on the theme of the sequel paper and chapter 3 of the thesis.

The first three chapters of the thesis are parts of a series of papers with the common title
A Hilbert bundles approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory
. Chapter 3, and the third paper, is the third installment in the series (and, of course, chapters 1 and 2 are respectively the first and second installments), and focuses on specific applications to two important topics in complex differential geometry and analysis of several variables:
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension and (strong) openness. Building on the ideas developed throughout the former two parts of the series, we give a complex Brunn-Minkowski proof of an Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem with sharp effective
L^{2}
-estimates, by generalizing the Berndtsson-Lempert method (
[
13
]
) to the global setting of complex manifolds, and the strong openness conjecture of Demailly (or the strong openess theorem of Guan-Zhou (
[
48
]
)). The crux of the argument in either case is a general monotonicity property, more specifically, a certain decresingness property, of (squared norms of) holomorphic sections of certain trivial Hilbert quotient bundles. This paper, and the third part of the series, is joint work with Xu Wang.

The final chapter of the thesis, and fourth paper, is also joint work with Xu Wang. We address a question by Ohsawa in a remark in his 2017-paper
[
35
]
on whether a complex Brunn-Minkowski proof, or rather a proof using the Berndtsson-Lempert method, of (either of) two Ohsawa-Takegoshi type of extension theorems (Theorems 0.1 and 4.1 in
[
35
]
) can be had. Using so-called weak geodesics from pluripotential theory to construct an appropriate weight function to be used in the Berndtsson-Lempert method, we prove a general Ohsawa-Takegoshi type of extension theorem that answers Ohsawa’s question in the affirmative (for both extension theorems). The paper has been published in Arkiv För Matematik. After its publication, the first (listed) author of the paper discovered that the main theorem of the paper may be used, in an interesting application of it, to give a new simple and short proof of a well-known first order Bergman kernel asymptotics. This proof is included in the version of the paper that appears in the thesis that follows.

Chapter 1
Paper 1

A HILBERT BUNDLES APPROACH TO COMPLEX BRUNN-MINKOWSKI THEORY, I

TAI TERJE HUU NGUYEN

1.1.
Introduction

In a milestone paper in 2006, entitled
Subharmonicity properties of the Bergman kernel and some other functions associated with pseudoconvex domains
(
[
2
]
), Berndtsson proved a rather remarkable result on plurisubharmonicity properties of Bergman kernels depending on a parameter, generalizing an earlier result of Maitani and Yamaguchi (
[
44
]
). This is in the literature often known as the
variation of Bergman kernels
, and might be said to be the starting point of the
complex Brunn-Minkowski theory
(see
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
7
]
for some first references). It is also the starting point of our story. The reader is directed to
[
2
]
, specifically to Theorem 1.1, for the most general statement(s). Here, we will focus on the following (special) situation (see, however, also sections 2 and 3 in
[
2
]
):

Let
U\times\Omega
be a domain in
\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, with
\Omega
bounded, and let
\phi
be a strictly plurisubharmonic function on
U\times\Omega
which is smooth up to the boundary. We use
t
to denote a generic point in
U
. Let
L^{2}_{t}
, respectively
A^{2}_{t}
, denote the weighted
L^{2}
-space, respectively the weighted Bergman space, on
\Omega
with weight function
e^{-\phi(t,\cdot)}
. Following
[
2
]
, we write
\phi^{t}
for the restriction of
\phi
to
\{t\}\times\Omega
. Thus,
L^{2}_{t}
and
A^{2}_{t}
are the (complex) Hilbert spaces

(1.1.1)

\displaystyle L^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left\{f\in L^{2}_{\text{loc}}(\Omega):\left|\left|f\right|\right|^{2}_{t}:=\int_{\Omega}\left|f\right|^{2}e^{-\phi^{t}}<\infty\right\}\quad\quad\text{ and }

(1.1.2)

\displaystyle A^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle=L^{2}_{t}\cap\mathscrsfs{O}(\Omega)

with respect to the weighted
L^{2}
-inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}
given by

(1.1.3)

\displaystyle(f,g)_{t}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Omega}f\bar{g}e^{-\phi^{t}}.

Integration is with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and
\mathscrsfs{O}(\Omega)
denotes the family of holomorphic functions on
\Omega
. Let
k^{t}
denote the Bergman kernel of
A^{2}_{t}
, and
K^{t}
the Bergman kernel of
A^{2}_{t}

on the diagonal
. Thus,
k^{t}
is the map

(1.1.4)

\displaystyle k^{t}:\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{C},(z,w)\mapsto k^{t}_{w}(z),

where
k^{t}_{z}
is the unique element in
A^{2}_{t}
satisfying that

(1.1.5)

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}f\overline{k^{t}_{z}}e^{-\phi^{t}}

for all
f\in A^{2}_{t}
, and
K^{t}
is the map

(1.1.6)

\displaystyle K^{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C},z\mapsto k^{t}_{z}(z).

The (version of the) variation of Bergman kernels (that will be important to us) asserts the following:

Theorem 1.1.1

(Variation of Bergman kernels (special case),
[
2
]
)
.

With the above notation and set-up, suppose that
\Omega
is pseudoconvex. Then for each
z\in\Omega
, the map
U\ni t\mapsto\log K^{t}(z)
is plurisubharmonic.

Let us comment briefly on the proof of the theorem; we denote the map
t\mapsto\log K^{t}(z)
in Theorem
1.1.1
by
\log K(z)
, and the map
t\mapsto K^{t}(z)
by
K(z)
. The proof may be split into three key steps:

(1)

Show that
K(z)
is smooth on
U
.

(2)

Compute
i\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}_{t}\log K(z)
; we use
t
-subscripts to emphasize that we are considering the
\partial
and
\overline{\partial}
operators on
U
(as opposed to
\Omega
).

(3)

Verify that
i\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}_{t}\log K(z)\geq 0
.

We shall in this paper mainly be concerned with steps (1) and (2), at least to begin with, and here merely mention that in step (3), Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates for (solutions of) the
\overline{\partial}
-equation (see
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
) are utilized. The pseudoconvexity of
\Omega
, and the (strict) plurisubharmonicity of
\phi
, are then assumptions of natural relevance and importance. Interestingly, however, and as we shall see, as far as steps (1) and (2) are concerned, the assumptions on
\Omega
and
\phi
may be (much) weaker.

In
[
2
]
, the smoothness of the Bergman kernel
K(z)
in step (1) is established by use of the deep Hamilton-Kohn regularity theory for the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann operator associated with smoothly bounded strictly pseudoconvex domains, under the additional assumption that
\Omega
be smoothly bounded and strictly pseudoconvex; see (the proof of) Lemma 2.1 in
[
2
]
. We will refer to this (way of showing that
K(z)
is smooth) as
the classical approach
to the variation of Bergman kernels. In addition to the classical approach, there is recently an alternative and novel approach owing to Berndtsson, more pertinent in the following paper, and that we now turn to. In this new approach, the role of the deep regularity properties of the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann operator in the classical approach is instead played by a smoothness theorem that is taught to us by Berndtsson. Said theorem admits an integral part in the present paper, and we have entitled it
Berndtsson’s regularity theorem
. For the statement of it, recall that the Bergman projection associated with
A^{2}_{t}
is the canonical orthogonal projection map
L^{2}_{t}\to A^{2}_{t}
with respect to the (complete) inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}
. Note that in our current situation,
L^{2}:=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
L^{2}_{t}
are equal as vector spaces for each
t\in U
, so this map is also the canonical orthogonal projection map
L^{2}\to A^{2}
with respect to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}
;
A^{2}
being the usual Bergman space
L^{2}\cap\mathscrsfs{O}(\Omega)
on
\Omega
. The point here is of course that
L^{2}
and
A^{2}
do not depend on (the) (parameter)
t
. Let
P^{t}
denote the Bergman projection associated with
A^{2}_{t}
, and define the map
P
by

(1.1.7)

\displaystyle P:U\times L^{2}\to A^{2},(t,f)\mapsto P^{t}f.

Using the above notation, Berndtsson’s regularity theorem may now be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1.2

(Berndtsson’s regularity theorem)
.

Let
U\times\Omega
be a domain in
\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and let
\phi
be a real-valued function on
U\times\Omega
. Assume that
\Omega
is bounded, that
\phi
is smooth up to the boundary, and let for each
t\in U
,
A^{2}_{t}=A^{2}(\Omega,\phi^{t})
,
P^{t}
, and
P
be defined as above. Then
P
is smooth (in the (real) Fréchet sense; see
[
45
]
).

Note that we in Theorem
1.1.2
are not assuming that
\Omega
be pseudoconvex, nor even that
\phi
be plurisubharmonic. The situation in there is therefore already notably different from that in the classical approach. Indeed, for instance, since
\Omega
is only assumed to be bounded, the deep regularity properties of the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann operator also never come into play. That we are allowed weaker assumptions on
\Omega
and
\phi
, suggests that some natural generalizations are plausible, and becomes the starting point for the theme of the present paper. We shall say a little bit more about this theme in a moment, but first we use Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
) to carry out step (1) above. We do this by using Theorem
1.1.2
to prove the following smoothness lemma for the Bergman kernel of
A^{2}_{t}
:

Lemma 1.1.3
.

Let
z,w\in\Omega
. Then under the assumptions in Theorem
1.1.2
, we have that
k^{t}_{z}(w)
is smooth in
t
.

Proof.

We shall give two proofs of the lemma. The first mimics in parts the proof of Lemma 2.1 in
[
2
]
. Said proof uses the specific setting that we are currently in. The second proof works also in a more general setting, and we shall meet it again in section
1.3
. The second proof also gives a slightly more general statement (in that we need not fix
w
to begin with), and we include it here to serve as a nice introduction to what to come.

First proof

It follows by definition of
P^{t}
that for all
g\in L^{2}_{t}
,

(1.1.8)

\displaystyle P^{t}g(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}g\overline{k^{t}_{z}}e^{-\phi^{t}}.

Let
w:=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
and
\chi_{j}
be a rotation-invariant smooth real-valued non-negative function on
\mathbb{C}
supported in a small disc
\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{j}}(w_{j})
centred around
w_{j}
; the notation here means that the disc has center
w_{j}
and radius
\epsilon_{j}
. Assume also for simplicity, by way of normalization, that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{j}}(w_{j})}\chi_{j}\equiv 1
. Let
\epsilon:=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})
and
\Delta^{n}_{\epsilon}(w):=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon_{j}}(w_{j})
. That is,
\Delta^{n}_{\epsilon}(w)
is the polydisc centred at
w
with polyradius
\epsilon
. Define
\chi:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\chi_{j}
as a function on
\Omega
supported in
\Delta^{n}_{\epsilon}(w)
, and consider
g:=e^{\phi^{t}}\chi
. By using polar coordinates, the rotation-invariance of each
\chi_{j}
, and the mean-value property for
k^{t}_{z}
, we then get from (
1.1.8
) that

(1.1.9)

\displaystyle P^{t}g(z)

\displaystyle=c\overline{k^{t}_{z}(w)},

where
c=1
by the normalization of each of the
\chi_{j}
’s. Hence, the assertion follows by Berndtsson’ regularity theorem, Theorem
1.1.2
(and by composition of smooth functions; see the upcoming remark below).

Second proof

We show that
k^{t}_{z}
is smooth in
t
as a map
U\to A^{2}
. Since evaluation at
w
is smooth (see
[
45
]
), this gives the assertion. Let
f\in A^{2}_{t}
, and denote by
\xi_{z}
the evaluation at
z
as a continuous linear functional on
A^{2}_{t}
. By the Riesz representation theorem there exists a unique (Riesz representative)
R^{t}(\xi_{z})\in A^{2}_{t}
(of
\xi_{z}
with respect to the
(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}
-metric) such that for all
f\in A^{2}_{t}
,

(1.1.10)

\displaystyle\xi_{z}(f)=f(z)=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f\overline{R^{t}(\xi_{z})}e^{-\phi^{t}}.

It follows that
R^{t}(\xi_{z})=k^{t}_{z}
, so it suffices to show that
R^{t}(\xi_{z})
is smooth in
t
. Let us denote the usual metric on
L^{2}
by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}}
, and take
R^{L^{2}}(\xi_{z})
to be the Riesz representative of
\xi_{z}
with respect to this metric. Then for all
f\in A^{2}\simeq A^{2}_{t}
(here
\simeq
means vector space isomorphism), we have that

(1.1.11)

\displaystyle\xi_{z}(f)=f(z)=\int_{\Omega}f\overline{R^{L^{2}}(\xi_{z})}=\int_{\Omega}f\overline{e^{\phi^{t}}R^{L^{2}}(\xi_{z})}e^{-\phi^{t}}=\int_{\Omega}f\overline{P^{t}(e^{\phi^{t}}R^{L^{2}}(\xi_{z}))}e^{-\phi^{t}},

where the last equality follows by orthogonality.
Thus by (
1.1.10
),
R^{t}(\xi_{z})=P^{t}(e^{\phi^{t}}R^{L^{2}}(\xi_{z}))
, so the assertion follows (as in the first proof) by Berndtsson’s regularity theorem, Theorem
1.1.2
(and by composition of smooth functions; see the upcoming remark).
∎

Remark
.

In the two previous proofs we have used the fact that if
u\in L^{2}
, then
t\mapsto e^{\pm\phi^{t}}u
is smooth on
U
. This is also used in the proof of Theorem
1.1.2
below, for the smoothness of
F
(see the proof below). This fact is not obvious (at least not to us), but certainly believable. It can be shown by use of the mean-value theorem and the uniform continuity of continuous functions on compact sets; recall that in our setting,
\overline{\Omega}
is compact (and
\phi
is smooth up to the boundary).

It clearly follows from Lemma
1.1.3
that
K(z)
is smooth. We have therefore carried out step (1) above using Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
), and the latter gives a new approach to the variation of Bergman kernels different from the classical approach. Let us also briefly discuss the proof of Berndtsson’s regularity (Theorem
1.1.2
); we shall give a more detailed proof of a generalized version of it later (see Theorem
1.1.4
and also Theorem
1.3.8
). The main tool used in the proof is the implicit mapping theorem (see
[
45
]
).

Proof of Theorem
1.1.2
.

Fix
(t_{0},f_{0})\in U\times L^{2}
, and assume for simplicity that
\phi^{t_{0}}=0
. It suffices to show that
P
is smooth near
(t_{0},f_{0})
. Consider the smooth map (see the previous remark)

(1.1.12)

\displaystyle F:U\times L^{2}\times A^{2}\to A^{2},(t,f,g)\mapsto P_{A^{2}}(e^{-\phi^{t}}(f-g)),

where
P_{A^{2}}
denotes the Bergman projection associated with
A^{2}
. It can be shown that we have
F(t,f,g)=0
if and only if
g=P^{t}f
, and that the partial derivative of
F
in the
g
-direction (the
A^{2}
-direction) at the point
(t_{0},f_{0},g_{0})
, for any
g_{0}
, is invertible (it is equal to
-\textbf{1}_{A^{2}}
, where we let
\textbf{1}_{X}
denote the identity map on a set
X
). Hence, by the implicit mapping theorem, there exist an open neighbourhood
O
of
(t_{0},f_{0})
and a unique smooth
A^{2}
-valued function
G
on
O
, such that
F(t,f,g)=0
if and only if
g=G(t,f)
, for all
(t,f)\in O
. By comparison we have
P=G
on
O
, so
P
is smooth(!).
∎

We now observe that only elementary tools are used in the proof (of Theorem
1.1.2
), the implicit mapping theorem being the main one. Looking at the classical approach, in which the deep regularity properties of the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann operator are rather important, we find this to be quite remarkable. This aspect of (the proof of) the theorem has moreover the noteworthy effects of making the new approach to the variation of Bergman kernels (much) less rigid, and (in our opinion) somewhat ”more accessible”, than the classical approach. For example, we see that as far as steps (1) and (2) above (in the 3-steps proof of Theorem
1.1.1
) are concerned (leaving aside step (3) for the time being), we may completely dispose of the pseudoconvexity and plurisubharmonicity assumptions on
\Omega
and
\phi
. Thus, if we are only interested in the smoothness of the Bergman kernel
K(z)
(and, not yet, its (logarithmic) plurisubharmonicity), we may consider a more general setting in which the assumptions in Theorem 1.1 are (much) weaker. We also see that the deep regularity properties of the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann operator have actually no play in the smoothness of
K(z)
, something which is not at all visible from the classical approach. These observations suggest some further natural generalizations, and we will in this paper explore such a generalization where we replace
L^{2}
and
A^{2}
above with more general Hilbert spaces. This naturally leads to the abstract and (more) general setting of (what we call)
(trivial) Hilbert bundles
, and we introduce and develop a theory (or formalism) for these. We then use this theory to generalize the new approach to the variation of Bergman kernels discussed above to the abstract setting. In particular, we shall give a generalization of Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
) to the setting of (trivial) Hilbert bundles. Using the latter as a key, we discuss an abstract and novel approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory in this (more) general setting; what we call
a Hilbert bundles approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory
, and which is precisely the theme of the present paper. To obtain the aforementioned generalization of Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
), whom we also entitle
Berndtsson’s regularity theorem
, and which is one of the main results in the paper (aside from the theory of Hilbert bundles and the abstract approach itself), we introduce two special kinds of (what we call)
hermitian metrics on (trivial) Hilbert bundles
. This allows us to adapt the original proof of Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
) to the (more) general setting and obtain a more general version using an analogous argument as before. We also show how to use this (more general) Berndtsson’s regularity theorem to prove a generalization, or a natural analogue, of Lemma
1.1.3
in the (more) general setting. Finally, we discuss steps (2) and (3) (in the proof of Theorem
1.1.1
) from above in the (more) general context of (trivial) Hilbert bundles, as well as some novel results, akin to Theorem
1.1.1
, or a variant of it.

With the terminology and formalism that we shall be developing throughout the rest of the paper, the aforementioned generalization of Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
) may be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1.4

(Berndtsson’s regularity theorem, Hilbert bundles version)
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over an
m
-dimensional complex manifold, and let
h
be a smooth weighted hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
induced by a hermitian metric
h^{0}
of zero variation. Let
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
be a subbundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and assume that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
is closed in
\mathscrsfs{H}
with respect to
h
and
h_{0}
. Then the total orthogonal projection map
P=P(h)
associated with
h
(of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
in
\mathscrsfs{H}
) is smooth.

This concludes our introduction. In the remainder of the paper, and starting with the next section, we detail our aforementioned Hilbert bundles approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory.

1.1.1.
Acknowledgements

It is a pleasure to thank Bo Berndtsson for being a great source of inspiration and for teaching us the implicit mapping theorem argument, and Xu Wang for invaluable discussions.

1.2.
Hilbert bundles and hermitian metrics on Hilbert bundles

1.2.1.
Hilbert bundles

In this section we begin our Hilbert bundles approach to complex Brunn-Minkowski theory, and we commence by discussing a few generalities on (what we call)
Hilbert bundles
and
hermitian metrics
(on Hilbert bundles). An immediate natural generalization of what we discussed in the introduction is to replace the families
\{L^{2}_{t}\}_{t}
and
\{A^{2}_{t}\}_{t}
with more general families of Hilbert spaces, say
\{\mathscrsfs{H}_{t}\}_{t}
and
\{(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{t}\}_{t}
. We may also let
t
vary over a more general index set
\mathscrsfs{B}
. We shall call a family of Hilbert spaces such as
\{\mathscrsfs{H}_{t}\}_{t}
, which depends on a parameter (
t
), a
(general) Hilbert bundle
. While this precisely defines what we shall mean by a (general) Hilbert bundle, to make our definition resemble somewhat more that of a vector bundle, we will employ the following formulation:

Definition 1.2.1
.

A
(general) Hilbert bundle
is an ordered triple
(\mathscrsfs{H},\pi,\mathscrsfs{B})
consisting of two (non-empty) sets
\mathscrsfs{H}
and
\mathscrsfs{B}
, and a surjective map
\pi:\mathscrsfs{H}\to\mathscrsfs{B}
, with the property that
\pi^{-1}(b)
is a (complex) Hilbert space for each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
.

In particular, all finitely-ranked vector bundles, and all infinitely-ranked vector bundles (see
[
55
]
,
[
56
]
) whose fibers are Hilbert spaces, classify as Hilbert bundles according to our definition. Given a Hilbert bundle
(\mathscrsfs{H},\pi,\mathscrsfs{B})
, we will refer to
\mathscrsfs{H},\mathscrsfs{B}
, and
\pi
as respectively
the total space, the base space
, and
the projection map
, of the (Hilbert) bundle. Also, given a Hilbert bundle
(\mathscrsfs{H},\pi,\mathscrsfs{B})
, and
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
, we will refer to the Hilbert space
\pi^{-1}(b)
as
the fiber of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
b
, and generically denote it by
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
. Finally, we will typically refer to a Hilbert bundle by referring to its total space, and to a Hilbert bundle whose base space is
\mathscrsfs{B}
as a Hilbert bundle
over

\mathscrsfs{B}
.

Of particular interest to us will be what we call
trivial
Hilbert bundles. One way to define these is to first define what one might call
Hilbert bundle isomorphisms
, meant to be ”the Hilbert bundles analogues” of vector bundle isomorphisms, and then mimic the definition of trivial vector bundles (see
[
57
]
). For simplicity, however, we have settled for the following definition that shall suffice for all our purposes:

Definition 1.2.2
.

Let
H
be a (complex) Hilbert space (or more generally, a Hilbert manifold; see
[
55
]
,
[
56
]
) and
\mathscrsfs{B}
a (non-empty) set. The
trivial Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
with typical fiber
H
is (the Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
given by) the triple
(\mathscrsfs{H},\pi,\mathscrsfs{B})
, where
\mathscrsfs{H}=\mathscrsfs{B}\times H
and
\pi:\mathscrsfs{H}\to\mathscrsfs{B}
is the natural projection map
(b,u)\mapsto b
.

Let us reserve the symbol
B
throughout the paper to denote a fixed
m
-dimensional complex manifold; this to avoid having to repeatedly write the phrase ”over an
m
-dimensional complex manifold”. We shall mostly focus on trivial Hilbert bundles
over
B
with Hilbert spaces (contra more general Hilbert manifolds) as their typical fibers. If
\mathscrsfs{H}
is a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
with typical fiber
H
, we identify for simplicity
H
with
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and also say that
H
is a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
.

Example 1.2.1
.

Let
L^{2}
,
A^{2}
, and
U
be defined as in the introduction (that is,
U\times\Omega
is a domain in
\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
U
open and
\Omega
bounded, and
L^{2}
and
A^{2}
are respectively the
L^{2}
-space and Bergman space on
\Omega
). Then
L^{2}
and
A^{2}
are trivial Hilbert bundles over
U
. Since we are dealing with domains in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we like to think of this example as being in
the local setting
. There is a natural
global analogue
of it in the setting of complex manifold as follows: Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional complex manifold, and let
L
be a hermitian holomorphic line bundle over
X
with metric
e^{-\phi}
. For two smooth
L
-valued sections
u
and
v
of the canonical bundle of
X
, here denoted
K_{X}
, define
h^{0}
by

(1.2.1)

\displaystyle h^{0}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}u\wedge\overline{v}e^{-\phi},

and let
H
be the completion of the space of smooth
L
-valued sections
u
of
K_{X}
such that
h^{0}(u,u):=\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{0}}<\infty
, with respect to
h^{0}
. Let
H_{0}
denote the subspace of
H
consisting of holomorphic sections, and equip both
H
and
H_{0}
with
h^{0}
as a metric (or Hilbert inner product). Then
H
and
H_{0}
are trivial Hilbert bundles over
B
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a (general) Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
, and let
\mathscrsfs{U}\subseteq\mathscrsfs{B}
be non-empty. Adopting terminology from the theory of vector bundles, we define
a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
\mathscrsfs{U}
to be a map
u:\mathscrsfs{U}\to\mathscrsfs{H}
such that

(1.2.2)

\displaystyle u(b):=u^{b}\in\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}

for all
b\in\mathscrsfs{U}
. In the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
is trivial over
B
with typical fiber
H
, we may, and will, identify (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
with
H
-valued maps defined on subsets of
B
, in the natural way. When the occasion calls for it, we use
\Gamma(\mathscrsfs{U};\mathscrsfs{H})
to denote the family of all local sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
\mathscrsfs{U}
; in the global case that
\mathscrsfs{U}=\mathscrsfs{B}
, we simply write
\Gamma(\mathscrsfs{H})
.

We shall also need the notions of
(Hilbert) subbundles
,
(Hilbert) dual bundles
, and
(Hilbert) quotient bundles
in our theory, which are analogous to the corresponding notions from the theory of vector bundles. For these, we use the following definition:

Definition 1.2.3
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a (general) Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
(some non-empty set, but we shall stop pointing this out). Then:

(i)

A
(Hilbert) subbundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
is a Hilbert bundle
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathscrsfs{H}
over
\mathscrsfs{B}
such that
(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
is a closed subspace of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
for each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
.

(ii)

The
(Hilbert) dual bundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, here generically denoted
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
, is the Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
such that
(\mathscrsfs{H}^{*})_{b}=(\mathscrsfs{H}_{b})^{*}
, the dual space of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
, for each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
.

(iii)

If
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
is a (Hilbert) subbundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, we refer to
\mathscrsfs{H}/\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
as a
(Hilbert) quotient bundle
and define it to be the Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
such that
(\mathscrsfs{H}/\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}/(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
for each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
. We will use the notation
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
to denote that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
is a subbundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and in the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
and
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
is fixed, or understood from the context, we will frequently denote the quotient bundle
\mathscrsfs{H}/\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
by
\mathscrsfs{Q}
(for ”quotient”). In the case of trivial Hilbert bundles, we shall typically use the symbols
H,H_{0}
, and
Q
respectively in place of
\mathscrsfs{H},\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
and
\mathscrsfs{Q}
.

Example 1.2.2
.

Let
L^{2},A^{2},H,
and
H_{0}
be as in Example
1.2.1
. Then
A^{2}\leq L^{2}
and
H_{0}\leq H
, as trivial Hilbert bundles.

1.2.2.
Hermitian metrics on Hilbert bundles

Next, we equip our Hilbert bundles with natural hermitian structures by introducing on these what we call
hermitian metrics
. In principle, the definition of a hermitian metric on a Hilbert bundle is straight-forward. There is, however, a slight subtlety that appears when we also take a ”dual point of view”. To explain this, suppose that
h=\{h_{b}\}_{b\in\mathscrsfs{B}}
is collection of complete inner products on the fibers of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, indexed of course such that
h_{b}
is a complete inner product on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
. Consider now the dual bundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
,
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
, and its fiber over
b
,
(\mathscrsfs{H}^{*})_{b}=(\mathscrsfs{H}_{b})^{*}:=\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}^{*}
. The fiber
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}^{*}
is by definition the dual space of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
, and thus consists of complex-linear continuous functionals on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
. The point to stress here is that the continuity in this context is with respect to the fixed inner product on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
with which it is equipped from the outset (being a Hilbert space). Let us denote this inner product by
g_{b}
, as it in general may differ from
h_{b}
. In the situation at hand, we may also consider the space of complex-linear functionals on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
which are continuous with respect to
h_{b}
, and an inconvenience arises if this latter space and
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}^{*}
do not coincide as vector spaces. We shall say that
h
satisfies
the duality condition (of continuity)
if the two former vector spaces
do coincide
. Thus,
h
satisfies the duality condition of continuity if it is the case that any complex-linear functional on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
is continuous with respect to
g_{b}
if and only if it is continuous with respect to
h_{b}
. In particular, if
g_{b}
and
h_{b}
are equivalent, this is of course the case. To avoid the aforementioned inconvenience, we incorporate the duality condition directly as part of our definition of hermitian metrics on Hilbert bundles. The latter is as follows:

Definition 1.2.4
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a (general) Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
. A
hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
is an assignment
h:=\{h_{b}\}_{b\in\mathscrsfs{B}}
to each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
a complete inner product
h_{b}
on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
which satisfies the duality condition (of continuity).

If
\mathscrsfs{H}
is a Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
, and
h
is a hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
, then we define for each pair of (local) sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
,
h(u,v)
to be the (local) function on
\mathscrsfs{B}
with values in
\mathbb{C}
given by

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle h(u,v)(b)

\displaystyle:=h_{b}(u^{b},v^{b})

(for all
b
in the intersection of the domain of
u
and
v
). We also put

(1.2.4)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{h}^{2}:=(\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{h})^{2}:=h(u,u),

and refer to
\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{h}
as
the
h
-norm of
u
, or the
norm of
u
with respect to
h
. The hermitian metric
h
is completely determined by specifying
h(u,v)
for all pairs of local sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
.

1.2.3.
Adjoints, minimal lifts, and total orthogonal projections

Suppose that
h
is a hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and let
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
. Then
h
obviously induces a natural hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, simply by restriction. Similarly,
h
induces a natural hermitian metric on the dual bundle
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
, and also on the quotient bundle
\mathscrsfs{Q}
under the additional natural assumption that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
be what we call
closed in
\mathscrsfs{H}
with respect to
h
. By the latter is meant the following: We shall say that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
is
closed in
\mathscrsfs{H}
with respect to
h
, or
h
-closed in
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and denote this by writing

(1.2.5)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0},

if
(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
is closed in
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
with respect to
h_{b}
for each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
.

To discuss the naturally induced hermitian metrics on
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
and
\mathscrsfs{Q}
, we introduce next what we call
adjoint operators
and
minimal lifting operators
. Let us begin with
adjoint operators
. Let
u^{*}
be a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
,
u
be a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and the intersection of the domains of
u^{*}
and
u
be denoted by
\mathscrsfs{U}
. We define

(1.2.6)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u\right\rangle=u^{*}(u)

to be the complex-valued function on
\mathscrsfs{U}
given by

(1.2.7)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u\right\rangle(b):=(u^{*}(b))u^{b}:=\left\langle u^{*}(b),u^{b}\right\rangle.

Let
D_{u^{*}}
denote the domain of definition of
u^{*}
. By the Riesz representation theorem (and the duality condition satisfied by
h
(!)), there exists a unique local section
\sharp u^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
D_{u^{*}}
such that

(1.2.8)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u\right\rangle

\displaystyle=h(u,\sharp u^{*})

for all local sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We give special names to the (local) section
\sharp u^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and the operator that sends
u^{*}
to
\sharp u^{*}
:

Definition 1.2.5
.

With the above notation and set-up, we define
\sharp u^{*}
(or
\sharp^{h}u^{*}
if we want to emphasize
h
) to be
the adjoint of
u^{*}
(with respect to
h
)
. We also define the
adjoint operator (for
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
) with respect to
h
to be the complex-antilinear operator that sends any local section
u^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
to its adjoint (with respect to
h
). We will generically denote the latter by
\sharp
(or
\sharp^{h}
if we want to emphasize
h
).

Note that the adjoint operator
\sharp
in Definition
1.2.5
has an obvious inverse operator. We will generically denote this inverse operator by
\sharp^{-1}
(or
(\sharp^{h})^{-1}
if
h
needs to emphasized), and its value at a ”point”
u
(a local section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
) by
\sharp^{-1}u
(or
(\sharp^{h})^{-1}u
if
h
needs to emphasized). The adjoint operator
\sharp
may be used to define a natural hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
induced from
h
. We will denote this natural hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
induced by
h
, by
h^{-1}
, and refer to it as
the dual (hermitian) (metric) of
h
. We define the dual metric
h^{-1}
by letting

(1.2.9)

\displaystyle h^{-1}(u^{*},v^{*}):=h(\sharp v^{*},\sharp u^{*})

for all local sections
u^{*}
and
v^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
.

We next consider the induced metric on the quotient bundle. For this, we shall always assume that

(1.2.10)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}.

It then follows that for each
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
, we have an orthogonal direct sum decomposition

(1.2.11)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}=(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}\oplus(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}^{\perp},

with respect to
h_{b}
, where

(1.2.12)

\displaystyle(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\left\{a_{b}\in\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}:h_{b}(a_{0,b},a_{b})\equiv 0\;\forall\;a_{0,b}\in(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}\right\}

is (what we may call) the
h_{b}
-orthogonal complement of
(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
in
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
. We shall usually write all these decompositions, as
b
varies, as a single decomposition

(1.2.13)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{H}

\displaystyle=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\oplus\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{\perp}.

Let
u
be a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
\mathscrsfs{U}
. The above decomposition induces a decomposition of
u
of the form

(1.2.14)

\displaystyle u=u_{0}\oplus u_{0}^{\perp},

where
u_{0}
is a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
over
\mathscrsfs{U}
, and where

(1.2.15)

\displaystyle h(v_{0},u_{0}^{\perp})\equiv 0

for all local sections
v_{0}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. In general, if
v
is any local section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
satisfying

(1.2.16)

\displaystyle h(a_{0},v)\equiv 0

for all local sections
a_{0}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, we shall say that
v
is a local section of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{\perp}_{0}
, and also write
v\perp\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
; if we need to emphasize
h
we may write
\perp_{h}
for
\perp
. Let
[u]
be a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{Q}
over
\mathscrsfs{U}
. By a
representative of
[u]
, we shall mean a (local) section
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
\mathscrsfs{U}
such that for all
b\in\mathscrsfs{U}
,

(1.2.17)

\displaystyle[u^{b}]=[u]^{b},

where
[a_{b}]
denotes the equivalence class of
a_{b}\in\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
in
\mathscrsfs{Q}_{b}=(\mathscrsfs{H}_{b})/(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
. It follows that
[u]
admits a canonical representative, namely
u_{0}^{\perp}
where
u=u_{0}\oplus u_{0}^{\perp}
is
any
representative of
[u]
. This suggests the following ”quotient analogue” to Definition
1.2.5
:

Definition 1.2.6
.

With the above notation and set-up, we define
the minimal lift(ing) of
[u]
(with respect to
h
)
to be the canonical representative
u_{0}^{\perp}
of
[u]
, where
u=u_{0}\oplus u^{\perp}_{0}
is
any
representative of
[u]
, and denote it by
[u]^{\uparrow_{\perp}}
or
m_{l}[u]
. We also define
the minimal lift(ing) operator for
\mathscrsfs{Q}
(with respect to
h
)
to be the complex-linear operator that sends any local section of
\mathscrsfs{Q}
to its minimal lift(ing) (with respect to
h
), and denote it by
m_{l}
. (If we want to emphasize
h
, we may write
\perp^{h}
instead of just
\perp
, and
m_{l}^{h}
instead of just
m_{l}
.)

Similar to the case for the adjoint operator, there is an inverse operator of
m_{l}
on its image. We will denote this inverse operator by
m_{l}^{-1}
, and its value at a ”point”
u
by
u_{\downarrow}
or
m_{l}^{-1}u
. (As before, we may replace
\downarrow
with
\downarrow^{h}
, and
m_{l}^{-1}
with
(m_{l}^{h})^{-1}
if we want to emphasize
h
.) Using the minimal lifting operator in Definition
1.2.6
, we may now define a natural hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{Q}
induced from
h
. We generically denote this metric by
h^{\mathscrsfs{Q}}
, and we define it by letting

(1.2.18)

\displaystyle h^{\mathscrsfs{Q}}([u],[v])

\displaystyle:=h([u]^{\uparrow_{\perp}},[v]^{\uparrow_{\perp}})

for all (local) sections
[u]
and
[v]
of
\mathscrsfs{Q}
. We shall refer to the metric
h^{\mathscrsfs{Q}}
as
the quotient (hermitian) metric (on
\mathscrsfs{Q}
) induced by
h
.

We conclude this section by defining the ”Hilbert bundles analogue” of the map
P
in Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
) from the introduction, and with a preliminary result that we shall later show relates to Lemma
1.1.3
. We begin with the definition, which is the following:

Definition 1.2.7
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a (general) Hilbert bundle over
\mathscrsfs{B}
, and suppose that
h
is a hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Assume that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
with
\overline{(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. Then we define
the total orthogonal projection map
P=P(h)
associated with
h
(of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
in
\mathscrsfs{H}
)
to be the complex-linear operator that sends any local section
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
to the local section
Pu
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
defined by

(1.2.19)

\displaystyle(Pu)(b)

\displaystyle:=P^{b}u^{b},

where
P^{b}
denotes the canonical orthogonal projection
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}\to(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
with respect to
h_{b}
.

Example 1.2.3
.

Let
L^{2}_{t},A^{2}_{t}
, and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}
be defined as in the introduction. Then
\mathscrsfs{H}:=\{L^{2}_{t}\}_{t\in U}
and
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}:=\{A^{2}_{t}\}_{t\in U}
are (trivial) Hilbert bundles over
U
with
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
, and we may view the collection
h:=\{(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}\}_{t\in U}
as a (smooth) hermitian metric on these such that
\overline{\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. The total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
in
\mathscrsfs{H}
is precisely the map
P
that appears in Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.1.2
).

We next give the preliminary result relating to Lemma
1.1.3
; we shall say more about the relation later (see Corollary
1.3.9
). To prepare for the result, let
\mathscrsfs{H},\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
,
h
, and
P
be as in Definition
1.2.7
. In this situation, there are (at least) two dual bundles that we may consider, namely
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
and
(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})^{*}:=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
. Let
\sharp
denote the adjoint operator for
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
, and
\sharp_{0}
the adjoint operator for
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
, both with respect to
h
(to be more precise, the first adjoint operator is with respect to
h
, and the second is with respect to the restriction of
h
as a hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
). We may ask for the relationship between the two adjoint operators, if any, and have then the following quite useful result:

Proposition 1.2.1
.

With the above notation and set-up, we have

(1.2.20)

\displaystyle\sharp_{0}=P\sharp.

It might be good to comment on the precise meaning of the identity in Proposition
1.2.1
. By definition,
\sharp_{0}
acts on (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
, while
\sharp
acts on (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
. The identity means the following: If
u^{*}
is a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
, then
by the Hahn-Banach theorem,
u^{*}
extends to a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
. The
\sharp
-operator can act on such an extension, and the identity means that if we first let
\sharp
act on such an extension and then apply
P
to the result, we get
\sharp_{0}u^{*}
. For this to make sense,
P
acting on
\sharp
acting on such an extension needs to be independent of the choice of extension of
u^{*}
. This is indeed the case. To see this, we let
\xi^{*}
and
\eta^{*}
be two extensions of
u^{*}
as (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
, and verify that
P\sharp\xi^{*}=P\sharp\eta^{*}
. It suffices of course (due to the projection) to show that for all (local) sections
u_{0}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
,

(1.2.21)

\displaystyle h(u_{0},P\sharp\xi^{*}-P\sharp\eta^{*})=0.

But this follows by definition of
\sharp
, orthogonality, and linearity. Indeed, we get

(1.2.22)

\displaystyle h(u_{0},P\sharp\xi^{*}-P\sharp\eta^{*})

\displaystyle=h(u_{0},P(\sharp\xi^{*}-\sharp\eta^{*}))=\left\langle\xi^{*}-\eta^{*},u_{0}\right\rangle=\left\langle u^{*}-u^{*},u_{0}\right\rangle=0,

with the two last equalities following from the fact that
\xi^{*}
and
\eta^{*}
are both extensions of
u^{*}
. Conversely, if
u^{*}
is a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
, and for each
b
in a subset of the domain of definition of
u^{*}
,
u^{*}(b)
happens to also be defined on
(H_{0})_{b}
, then we may naturally view
u^{*}
(restricted to this subset) as a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
. The identity then means that
\sharp_{0}
acting on
u^{*}
viewed as such a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
, is equal to
P\sharp u^{*}
. Our discussion actually also gives the proof of the proposition:

Proof of Proposition
1.2.1
.

Let
u^{*}
be a local section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
, and let
\xi^{*}
be any extension of
u^{*}
to a local section of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
. Let
u_{0}
be a local section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. Then, by definition of
\sharp_{0}
,

(1.2.23)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=h(u_{0},\sharp_{0}u^{*}).

On the other hand, since
\xi^{*}
is an extension of
u^{*}
, we also have, by definition of
\sharp
,

(1.2.24)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\xi^{*},u_{0}\right\rangle=h(u_{0},\sharp\xi^{*}),

By orthogonality the latter is equal to
h(u_{0},P\sharp\xi^{*})
, so by comparison it follows that we have
\sharp_{0}u^{*}=P\sharp\xi^{*}
(we must not believe that
h(u_{0},\sharp\xi^{*})=h(u_{0},\sharp_{0}u^{*})
, which does hold true, gives that
\sharp_{0}u^{*}=\sharp\xi^{*}
(!)).
∎

1.3.
Special hermitian metrics and Berndtsson’s regularity theorem

1.3.1.
Hermitian metrics of zero variation

In the previous section we looked at some generalities of Hilbert bundles and hermitian metrics. Most of what we discussed in there applies to general Hilbert bundles. In this section we shall mostly restrict our attention to trivial Hilbert bundles. Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
. Then the Hilbert bundle
H
admits a natural smooth and holomorphic structure. Let us start by briefly recapitulating this. Let
U\subseteq B
be open,
u\in\Gamma(U;H)
, and
b\in U
. We consider what it means for
u
to be smooth at
b
. Since smoothness is a local property, we may, by passing to local holomorphic coordinates near
b
, restrict to the case that
b
is point in some open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. We may then view
u
as an
H
-valued map on this open subset, and we say that
u
is
smooth at
b
if it is smooth in the real-Fréchet sense (see
[
45
]
) at
b
when viewed as such a map. We say that
u
is
smooth (on
U
)
if it is smooth at each point in
U
. Let us denote by
u^{\prime}(b)
the real-Fréchet derivative of
u
at
b
, viewed as such a map. The following definition asserts what it means for
u
to be
holomorphic
at
b
:

Definition 1.3.1
.

Using the above notation, we say that
u
is
holomorphic at
b
if
u^{\prime}(b)
(which apriori is only real-linear) is complex-linear. We also say that
u
is
holomorphic (on
U
)
if it is holomorphic at each point in
U
.

Let us denote the subfamily of
\Gamma(U;H)
consisting of smooth, respectively holomorphic, sections, by
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
, respectively
\mathscrsfs{O}(U;H)
; in the global case that
U=B
, we simply write
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(H)
, respectively
\mathscrsfs{O}(H)
. Let us also write
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U)
, respectively
\mathscrsfs{O}(U)
, for
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{C})
, respectively
\mathscrsfs{O}(U;\mathbb{C})
. Then
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
is a
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U)
-module, and
\mathscrsfs{O}(U;H)
is an
\mathscrsfs{O}(U)
-module. The smooth structure of
H
gives rise to a notion of
smooth hermitian metrics
on
H
:

Definition 1.3.2
.

Suppose that
h
is a hermitian metric on
H
. Then we shall say that
h
is
smooth
if
h(u,v)
is a smooth complex-valued function for all smooth local sections
u
and
v
of
H
.

In general, if
\mathscrsfs{H}
is a general Hilbert bundle over some complex, possibly infinite-dimensional manifold
\mathscrsfs{B}
, and there is an appropriate notion of smooth/holomorphic (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
(for example, the families of such smooth/holomorphic sections should have natural module structures), we shall say that
\mathscrsfs{H}

admits a smooth/holomorphic structure
. In the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
admits a smooth structure, and
h
is a hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
, we may define
h
to be smooth in exactly the same way as in Definition
1.3.2
. Namely, by saying that
h
is smooth if
h(u,v)
is smooth for all smooth (local) sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Now, suppose that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
. If
\mathscrsfs{H}
admits a smooth/holomorphic structure, then so does
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. Indeed, given a (local) section
u^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, we may view it as a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and then simply define
u^{*}
to be smooth/holomorphic as a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
if it is smooth/holomorphic when viewed as a (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. It is natural to ask whether the smooth/holomorphic structure of
\mathscrsfs{H}
also induces a smooth/holomorphic structure on the dual bundle
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
and or the quotient bundle
\mathscrsfs{Q}:=\mathscrsfs{H}/\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. This seems to be a rather hard question in general, and we will say just a little bit about the trivial case. Of course, since in this setting both the dual bundle and quotient bundle are trivial Hilbert bundles, they automatically admit smooth and holomorphic structures. The point is whether and or how these are induced from those of
\mathscrsfs{H}
.

Our discussion shall be by means of what we call
hermitian metrics of zero variations
, whom we introduce next. Let
h^{0}
denote the fixed inner product on
H
(as a Hilbert space). Then
h^{0}
induces a natural hermitian metric
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
on
H
(as a trivial Hilbert bundle) defined simply by letting, for all local sections
u
and
v
of
H
, and all
b
in the intersection of the domains of definition of
u
and
v
,

(1.3.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{0}(u,v)(b)

\displaystyle:=h^{0}(u^{b},v^{b}).

In particular, if
u
and
v
are elements of
H
(viewed as constant sections), then of course we get

(1.3.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{0}(u,v)(b)=\mathfrak{h}^{0}(u,v)(b^{\prime})

for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
. As a short-hand form, we shall express this by writing

(1.3.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{0}_{b}=\mathfrak{h}^{0}_{b^{\prime}}

for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
. We think of this as
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
not varying with respect to the base parameter, and hence being of
zero variation
. This motivates the following definition:

Definition 1.3.3
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
. We shall say that a hermitian metric
h
on
H
is of, or has,
zero variation
, if
h_{b}=h_{b^{\prime}}
for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
.

Despite their simplicity, hermitian metrics of zero variation turn out to be very important objects in our theory. Among other things, they play an essential role in the statement and the proof of Berndtsson’s regularity theorem below (Theorem
1.3.8
). Another aspect that is worth mentioning has to do with the existence of
Chern connections
; we discuss this more closely in section
1.4
, when these objects are introduced properly. Using calculus (see
[
45
]
), we get the following simple, but useful, characterization of hermitian metrics of zero variation:

Lemma 1.3.1
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and let
h
be a hermitian metric on
H
. Then
h
is of zero variation if and only if
h
is smooth and satisfies

(1.3.4)

\displaystyle\partial h(u,v)

\displaystyle=h(\partial u,v)+h(u,\overline{\partial}v)

for all smooth local sections
u
and
v
of
H
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
h
is of zero variation. Then, by calculus (see
[
45
]
),
h
is smooth, and we have

(1.3.5)

\displaystyle h(u,v)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=h(u^{\prime},v)+h(u,v^{\prime}).

The identity (
1.3.4
) thus follows by definition of partial derivatives (see
[
45
]
) and the sesquilinearity of
h
. Conversely, suppose that
h
is smooth and that (
1.3.4
) holds. Note that
h
is of zero variation if and only if
\partial h(u,v)=0
for all
u,v\in H
(viewed as constant sections). This is of course a special case of (
1.3.4
), so
h
is of zero variation.
∎

Suppose that
E
is a finitely-ranked holomorphic vector bundle over
U
, and that
u
is a smooth (local) section of
E
over
U
. Let
\overline{\partial}=\overline{\partial}_{t}
, where we choose generic local holomorphic coordinates
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
on
U
; recall also Example
1.2.1
. Then
u
is holomorphic if and only if
u
satisfies the
Cauchy-Riemann equation

\overline{\partial}u=0
. Not too surprisingly, the same characterization holds also in the possibly infinitely-ranked case that
u\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
:

Corollary 1.3.2
.

Let
u\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
. Then, using the above notation,
u\in\mathscrsfs{O}(U;H)
if and only if
\overline{\partial}u=0
.

Proof.

Let
h
be any hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
, and let
v\in H
(viewed as a constant section, but we shall stop pointing this out). From the proof of Lemma
1.3.1
,

(1.3.6)

\displaystyle h(u,v)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=h(u^{\prime},v),

from which it follows that
u^{\prime}
is complex-linear if and only if
h(u,v)^{\prime}
is complex-linear for all
v\in H
. The latter is equivalent to
\overline{\partial}h(u,v)=0
for all
v\in H
, which by Lemma
1.3.1
is the same as
h(\overline{\partial}u,v)=0
for all
v\in H
. The latter again is equivalent to
\overline{\partial}u=0
, which proves the assertion.
∎

We shall use the following notation: Let
\mathbb{K},\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. If
E
and
F
are two normed spaces over
\mathbb{F}
, with
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{F}
, then we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{K}}(E;F)
the space of continuous
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps
E\to F
, and by
\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{K}}(E;F)
, the space of
\mathbb{K}
-linear isomorphisms
E\to F
. Consider now the dual bundle of
H
,
H^{*}
, and let
\sharp
denote the adjoint operator with respect to the hermitian metric
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
of zero variation. Then we have the following characterization of smooth and holomorphic sections of
H^{*}
:

Proposition 1.3.3
.

With the above notation and set-up, the following holds:

(i)

Let
u^{*}\in\Gamma(U;H^{*})
. Then
u^{*}\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H^{*})\iff\sharp u^{*}\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
.

(ii)

Let
u^{*}\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H^{*})
. Then
u^{*}
is holomorphic if and only if
\sharp u^{*}
is antiholomorphic; that is, if
(\sharp u^{*})^{\prime}
is complex-antilinear.

Proof.

(i)

Let
\tilde{\sharp}
be defined on
B
by
\tilde{\sharp}(b):=\sharp^{b}
, where
\sharp^{b}
is defined by
\sharp^{b}u^{*}:=(\sharp u^{*})^{b}
for all
u^{*}\in H^{*}
. We claim that
\tilde{\sharp}
is a map
B\to\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H^{*};H)
. It is clear that
\tilde{\sharp}(b)
is real-linear for each
b\in B
. It is also continuous for we have for all
u^{*}\in H^{*}
, that:

(1.3.7)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\tilde{\sharp}(b)u^{*}\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left|(\sharp u^{*})^{b}\right|\right|=\left|\left|\sharp u^{*}\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{h}^{0}}(b)=\left|\left|u^{*}\right|\right|_{(\mathfrak{h}^{0})^{-1}}.

Thus,
\tilde{\sharp}(b)
is continuous with norm 1 for all
b\in B
. Finally, it also has an inverse, namely
(\tilde{\sharp}(b))^{-1}:=(\tilde{\sharp}^{-1})^{b}
, where
(\tilde{\sharp}^{-1})^{b}
is defined similar to
\sharp^{b}
:
(\tilde{\sharp}^{-1})^{b}u:=(\sharp^{-1}u)^{b}
for all
u\in H
. Since
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is of zero variation, it is easy to check that
\sharp^{b}=\sharp^{b^{\prime}}
for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
(see also the proof of Proposition
1.3.4
below). In particular,
\tilde{\sharp}
is constant in the sense that
\tilde{\sharp}(b)=\tilde{\sharp}(b^{\prime})
for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
, and hence smooth. It follows that for all local sections
u^{*}
of
H^{*}
,
\sharp u^{*}=\tilde{\sharp}\circ u^{*}
, which shows that
\sharp u^{*}
is smooth if
u^{*}
is smooth. For the converse, let
\mathfrak{i}
denote the inversion operator
\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H^{*};H)\to\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H;H^{*}),T\mapsto T^{-1}
. By Calculus (see
[
45
]
),
\mathfrak{i}
is smooth, so it follows that
\tilde{\sharp}^{-1}:=\mathfrak{i}\circ\tilde{\sharp}
is smooth as a map
B\to\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H,H^{*})
. Hence, if
\sharp u^{*}
is smooth, then
u^{*}=\tilde{\sharp}^{-1}\circ(\sharp u^{*})
is smooth. This proves (i).

(ii)

By the proof of (i), it follows that the dual of
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
,
(\mathfrak{h}^{0})^{-1}
, is of zero variation. Thus, by the proof of Corollary
1.3.2
,
u^{*}
is holomorphic if and only if

(1.3.8)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}(\mathfrak{h}^{0})^{-1}(u^{*},v^{*})=0

for all
v^{*}\in H^{*}
. By definition of
(\mathfrak{h}^{0})^{-1}
, we therefore have that
u^{*}
is holomorphic if and only if
\partial\sharp^{*}u=0
, which precisely means that
\sharp u^{*}
is antiholomorphic; one may also observe that
\partial\sharp u^{*}=\sharp(\overline{\partial}u^{*})
, (where the right-hand side has a natural and obvious meaning).

∎

If we try to prove a similar statement as Proposition
1.3.3
where
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
is replaced by a more general hermitian metric of zero variation, we seem to run into some problems. We will prove a similar statement under an additional assumption. Let
h
be a hermitian metric on
H
, and let
\sharp
be the adjoint operator with respect to
h
. We shall say that
\sharp
is
smooth
if
\sharp u^{*}
is (a) smooth (local) (section of
H
) for all smooth (local) (sections)
u^{*}
(of
H^{*}
). Similarly, we shall say that
\sharp^{-1}
is
smooth
if
\sharp^{-1}u
is (a) smooth (local section of
H^{*}
) whenever
u
is (a) smooth (local section of
H
). We may also consider similar definitions in the non-trivial setting, but let us not get into that here. The proof of Proposition
1.3.3
gives first the following proposition:

Proposition 1.3.4
.

Suppose that
h
is a hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
, and let
\sharp
denote the adjoint operator with respect to
h
. Assume that if we define
\tilde{\sharp}
as a map on
B
by
\tilde{\sharp}(b):=\sharp^{b}
with
\sharp^{b}u^{*}:=(\sharp u^{*})^{b}
for all
u^{*}\in H^{*}
,
\tilde{\sharp}
is a map
B\to\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H^{*};H)
. Then
\sharp
and
\sharp^{-1}
are smooth.

Proof.

By the proof of Proposition
1.3.3
, it suffices to show that
\tilde{\sharp}
is smooth, but this follows since
\tilde{\sharp}(b)=\tilde{\sharp}(b^{\prime})
for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
as
h
is of zero variation. Indeed, given
u^{*}\in H^{*}
and
u\in H
,

(1.3.9)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u\right\rangle(b)

\displaystyle=\left\langle u^{*},u\right\rangle(b^{\prime})\implies h(u,\sharp^{b}u^{*})=h(u,\sharp^{\tilde{b}}u^{*})\implies(\tilde{\sharp}(b)-\tilde{\sharp}(b^{\prime}))u^{*}=0.

∎

Using Proposition
1.3.4
(and its proof), we then get the following result that is similar to Proposition
1.3.3
:

Corollary 1.3.5
.

With the same assumptions, and using the same notation, as in Proposition
1.3.4
, the following holds:

(i)

Given
u^{*}\in\Gamma(U;H^{*})
,
u^{*}\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H^{*})\iff\sharp u^{*}\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
.

(ii)

Given
u^{*}\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
,
u^{*}
is holomorphic if and only if
\sharp u^{*}
is antiholomorphic.

Proof.

(i)

Suppose that
u^{*}
is smooth. Then
\sharp u^{*}
is smooth since
\sharp
is smooth by Proposition
1.3.4
. Conversely if
\sharp u^{*}
is smooth so is
u^{*}=\sharp^{-1}(\sharp u^{*})
, again by the proposition.

(ii)

By the proof of Proposition
1.3.4
, since
h
is of zero variation,
h^{-1}
, its dual metric, is also of zero variation. Thus the assertion follows from the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
1.3.3
(ii).

∎

Similar characterizations hold for the smooth and holomorphic structure on the quotient bundle
Q:=H/H_{0}
. Since the arguments are very similar to those in the case of the dual bundle, we will here content ourselves with stating the simplest result. Let
m_{l}
denote the minimal lifting operator with respect to
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
. Then we have the following proposition that can be thought of as the ”quotient analogue” to Proposition
1.3.3
:

Proposition 1.3.6
.

With the above notation, the following holds:

(i)

Given
[u]\in\Gamma(U;Q)
, we have
[u]\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;Q)\iff m_{l}[u]\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
.

(ii)

If
[u]\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;Q)
, then
[u]\in\mathscrsfs{O}(U;Q)\iff m_{l}[u]\in\mathscrsfs{O}(U;H)
.

We shall have use for, and come back to, the above characterizations later. For example in the proof of Corollary
1.3.9
below, and in section
1.4
, when we discuss
Chern connections
. In there, we shall also discuss more general versions of the above results (see Propositions
1.4.4
and
1.4.5
).

1.3.2.
Weighted hermitian metrics

Suppose now that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
, not necessarily of zero variation, and that
\overline{H_{0}}^{(H,h)}=H_{0}
. Let
P=P(h)
denote the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
(of
H_{0}
in
H
). Similar to the case of the adjoint operator (and its inverse), we shall say that
P
is
smooth
if
Pu
is (a) smooth (local) (section of
H_{0}
) whenever
u
is (a) smooth (local) (section of
H
). We will see several instances throughout the story where the smoothness of
P
is of natural interest and importance; Proposition
1.2.1
already provides an example: Using the notation in there, if
\sharp
is smooth, that
P
is smooth implies that
\sharp_{0}
is smooth. As we shall see, this may be viewed as a direct counterpart to Lemma
1.1.3
in the introduction. Another example is furnished by the smoothness of minimal lifting operators. First, we need to properly state what we mean by ”the smoothness of minimal lifting operators”; this will of course be similar to the smoothness of adjoint operators and total orthogonal projection maps: Let
m_{l}
denote the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h
. We shall say that
m_{l}
is
smooth
if
m_{l}[u]
is smooth whenever
[u]
is smooth. Similarly, we shall say that
m_{l}^{-1}
is
smooth
if
m_{l}^{-1}u
is smooth whenever
u
is smooth. With this, we then have that
m_{l}
is smooth if
P
is smooth: Suppose that
[u]
is smooth, and let
[u]
be a smooth representative of
[u]
(an example is
m_{l}^{0}[u]
where
m_{l}^{0}
denotes the minimal lifting operator with respect to
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
). Then
m_{l}[u]=u-Pu
, from which it follows that
m_{l}
is smooth given that
P
is. We also see from this that the converse is true. That is, if
m_{l}
is smooth, then so is
P
: Let
u
be smooth, and let
\pi_{Q}:H\to Q
be the natural projection. Then
\pi_{Q}u:=[u]
is smooth, so
Pu=u-m_{l}[u]
is smooth.

We shall show that if
h
is what we call a
smooth weighted hermitian metric

induced from a hermitian metric of zero variation
, then
P
is smooth. This is Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.3.8
below), one of our main results, and the generalization of Theorem
1.1.2
from the introduction to our current setting of (trivial) Hilbert bundles. Our notion of
weighted hermitian metrics
is motivated by the following elementary, but important, example:

Example 1.3.1
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
. Let
f>0
be a smooth function on
B
. We write
f:=e^{-\phi}
for some
\phi
(namely
\phi=-\log(f)
); this is of course inspired by the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{t}
in the introduction. If we let
u
be a smooth (local) section of
H
over
U
, some open subset of
B
, then by definition,
fu
is the smooth (local) section of
H
over
U
given by
(fu)(b):=f(b)u^{b}
. It follows that
\mathfrak{h}
defined by

(1.3.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(v_{1},v_{2}):=h(e^{-\phi}v_{1},v_{2})

for all local sections
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
of
H
, is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
. Moreover,
h
can be recovered from
\mathfrak{h}
by

(1.3.11)

\displaystyle h(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}(e^{\phi}v_{1},v_{2}).

Observe that for each
b\in B
, (multiplication by)
e^{-\phi(b)}
gives a complex-linear automorphism
H\to H
. We may therefore view
f
also as a map
f:B\to\text{Aut}(H)
, where
\text{Aut}(H)=\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{C}}(H;H)
is the space of complex-linear isomorphisms
H\to H
. To distinguish
f
as such a map from
f
as a map
B\to\mathbb{R}
, let us for the moment denote the former map
B\to\text{Aut}(H)
by
\tilde{f}
. Thus,
\tilde{f}=\iota\circ f
, where
\iota:\mathbb{R}\to\text{Aut}(H)
is the canonical inclusion map. Since
f
and
\iota
are both smooth, so is
\tilde{f}
. Note that
f
also induces a map
\hat{f}:B\times H\to H,(b,v)\mapsto\tilde{f}(b)v
, which in turn for a fixed local section
u
of
H
over
U
induces a map
U\to H,b\mapsto\tilde{f}(b)u^{b}
. This is precisely the map
fu
from earlier. By direct inspection, we have that
fu
is smooth. This follows simply because
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;H)
is a
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U)
-module. Now, given that
\tilde{f}
is smooth, the smoothness of
fu
can also be established as follows: Let
\hat{u}
denote the map
U\to B\times H,b\mapsto(b,u^{b})
. Then
\hat{u}
is smooth if and only if
u
is, and we have
fu=\hat{f}\circ\hat{u}
. We claim that
\hat{f}
is smooth when
\tilde{f}
is. To see this, we note that
\hat{f}
can be written
\hat{f}=m_{2}\circ m_{1}
, where
m_{2}:=\text{ev}
denotes the evaluation map
\text{Aut}(H)\times H\to H,(T,v)\mapsto Tv
, and
m_{1}=m_{1,\tilde{f}}
is the map
B\times H\to\text{Aut}(H)\times H,(b,v)\mapsto(\tilde{f}(b),v)
. By calculus (see
[
45
]
)
m_{2}
is continuous, thus (being bilinear) smooth. It is also clear that
m_{1}
is smooth when
\tilde{f}
is. Therefore
\hat{f}
is smooth as required. Finally, let
\mathfrak{i}:\text{Aut}(H)\to\text{Aut}(H)
denote the inversion map. We know (again, by calculus) that
\mathfrak{i}
is smooth. From this it follows that
f^{-1}u:=b\mapsto e^{\phi(b)}u^{b}
is smooth. The argument is analogous to before; using similar notation, the first step is that
\tilde{f}^{-1}
is smooth, which follows from
\tilde{f}
being smooth, since
\tilde{f}^{-1}=\mathfrak{i}\circ\tilde{f}
. The conclusion of all this is that given that
\tilde{f}
is smooth, we have that
fu
and
f^{-1}u
are smooth whenever
u
is.

The rather elaborate discussion in Example
1.3.1
has its purposes(s). Indeed, the property that the map
B\times H\to H
induced from
f
(
\tilde{f}
in the example), is smooth, is quite essential in the proof of Berndtsson’s regularity theorem below (Theorem
1.3.8
); see also the upcoming remark. The discussion in Example
1.3.1
leads to the following definition:

Definition 1.3.4
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a (smooth) hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. By a
(smooth) weight (map) for
h
we shall mean a (smooth) map

(1.3.12)

\displaystyle w:B\to\text{Aut}(H),b\mapsto w^{b}

such that defining
\mathfrak{h}
by

(1.3.13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=h(wu,v),

for all (smooth) local sections
u
and
v
of
H
, where
wu
is the (smooth) local section of
H
given by

(1.3.14)

\displaystyle(wu)^{b}:=w^{b}u^{b},

\mathfrak{h}
is a (smooth) hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. In this case, we refer to the (smooth) map

(1.3.15)

\displaystyle w^{-1}:B\to\text{Aut}(H),b\mapsto(w^{b})^{-1}:=(w^{-1})^{b}

as the
inverse (smooth) weight (map) of
w
, and to
\mathfrak{h}
as a
(smooth) weighted hermitian metric on
H
(induced by
h
)
. We also say in this case that
w
is a
(smooth) weight
of

\mathfrak{h}
.

Remark
.

If
w
is a smooth weight for
h
of
\mathfrak{h}
, then
w^{-1}
is a smooth weight for
\mathfrak{h}
of
h
; this follows by simply replacing
u
in the definition of
\mathfrak{h}
by
w^{-1}u
so that we get

(1.3.16)

\displaystyle h(u,v)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}(w^{-1}u,v).

Note also that by our discussion in Example
1.3.1
, if
w
is smooth, then
w^{-1}
is automatically smooth.

Example 1.3.2
.

Let
L^{2},A^{2},U
, and
\phi
be as in the introduction, with
\phi
smooth up to the boundary and
\Omega
bounded. We have seen that
L^{2}
and
A^{2}
are trivial Hilbert bundles over
U
and that
A^{2}\leq L^{2}
as such bundles. The fixed inner product on
L^{2}
(and
A^{2}
) is the standard
L^{2}
-inner product on
\Omega
. Let us denote the hermitian metric of zero variation induced by it by
h^{0}
. Then (multiplication by)
e^{-\phi^{t}}
, viewed as a map
U\to\text{Aut}(L^{2})
given by
t\mapsto e^{-\phi^{t}}
, defines a smooth weight for
h^{0}
. Let us denote the smooth weighted hermitian metric induced by
h^{0}
by
h
. Then for all
f,g\in L^{2}
,
h_{t}(f,g)=(f,g)_{t}
, where the right-hand side inner product is the one from the introduction.

Analogous to Example
1.2.1
, there is also a global analogue of the above example. Let
H,H_{0}
, and
h^{0}
be as in Example
1.2.1
. We have seen that
H
and
H_{0}
are trivial Hilbert bundles over
B
with
H_{0}\leq H
, and that
h^{0}
is a hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
(and
H_{0}
). For each
b\in B
, suppose that
\psi^{b}:=\{\psi^{b}\}
is a collection of locally defined real-valued functions such that
e^{-(\phi+\psi^{t})}
can be integrated over
X
when twisted with some
i^{n^{2}}u\wedge\bar{v}
, for
u,v\in H
, and let
\psi:=\{\psi\}
, where
\psi(b,z):=\psi^{b}(z)
for
b\in B
and
z\in X
. Suppose also for simplicity that
b\mapsto\psi
is smooth and that
e^{-\psi^{b}}
is bounded for each
b
. Then (multiplication by)
e^{-\psi}
defines a smooth weight for
h^{0}
. If we let
h
denote the induced smooth weighted hermitian metric with smooth weight
e^{-\psi}
, then for all
u,v\in H
and all
b\in B
, we have

(1.3.17)

\displaystyle h_{b}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}u\wedge\bar{v}e^{-\theta^{b}},

where we put
\theta:=\phi+\psi
and
\theta^{b}:=\phi+\psi^{b}
. Our most important examples in practice are akin to this example.

Our next result is a cute proposition on adjoint operators of weighted hermitian metrics:

Proposition 1.3.7
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a hermitian metric on
H
. Suppose also that
\mathfrak{h}
is a weighted hermitian metric on
H
induced by
h
with weight
w
. Let
\sharp_{h}
denote the adjoint operator for
H^{*}
with respect to
h
, and
\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}
the adjoint operator for
H^{*}
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
. Then

(1.3.18)

\displaystyle\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle=w^{-1}\sharp_{h}.

Proof.

Let
u^{*}
be local section of
H^{*}
. Then for all
u\in H
,

(1.3.19)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},u\right\rangle=h(u,\sharp_{h}u^{*})=\mathfrak{h}(u,\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}u^{*}).

Conjugating, we therefore have

(1.3.20)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}u^{*},u)=h(\sharp_{h}u^{*},u).

By definition of
\mathfrak{h}
, the left-hand side of this identity is equal to
h(w\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}u^{*},u)
, which implies that
w\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}=\sharp_{h}
. Applying
w^{-1}
then gives the desired relation.
∎

In particular, if
\sharp_{h}
and
w
are smooth, then
\sharp_{\mathfrak{h}}
is also smooth. Combining this with Proposition
1.2.1
and Corollary
1.3.5
, we get a natural generalization, or analogue, of Lemma
1.1.3
in the introduction.

1.3.3.
Berndtsson’s regularity theorem

We end this section with Berndtsson’s regularity theorem and a discussion on this. First up is Berndtsson’s regularity theorem, which generalizes Theorem
1.1.2
from the introduction:

Theorem 1.3.8

(Berndtsson’s regularity theorem)
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
,
h^{0}
a hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
, and
h
a smooth weighted hermitian metric on
H
induced by
h^{0}
. Suppose that
H_{0}\leq H
is closed in
H
with respect to
h
and
h^{0}
, and let
P=P(h)
denote the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
. Then
P
is smooth.

Proof.

We may consider
P
as a map
B\times H\to H_{0}
given by sending
(b,u)\in B\times H
to
P^{b}u
in
H_{0}
. It suffices to show that
P
as such a map is smooth, since it then follows that
P
is smooth (in our usual sense) by composition. It might be useful here to refer back to (our discussion in) Example
1.3.1
. Fix
a\in H
and
b_{0}\in B
. Since smoothness is a local property, it suffices to show that
P
as a map
B\times H\to H_{0}
is smooth near
(b_{0},a)
. Suppose that the smooth weight for
h^{0}
of
h
is
w
, and consider the auxiliary smooth map

(1.3.21)

\displaystyle F^{b_{0}}:B\times H\times H_{0}\to H_{0},(b,u,v)\mapsto P^{b_{0}}((w^{-1})^{b_{0}}(w^{b}(v-u))).

We claim that
F^{b_{0}}(b,u,v)=0
if and only if
v=P^{b}(u)
. Assume that the claim is true, and note that
F^{b_{0}}(b_{0},a,P^{b_{0}}a)=0
. By direct computation, for all
v,\Delta v\in H_{0}
, we have

(1.3.22)

\displaystyle F^{b_{0}}(b_{0},a,v+\Delta v)-F^{b_{0}}(b_{0},a,v)

\displaystyle=P^{b_{0}}(v+\Delta v-a)-P^{b_{0}}(v-a)=\Delta v,

since
P^{b_{0}}
is linear and restricts to the identity on
H_{0}
. It follows from this that the partial derivative (see
[
45
]
) of
F^{b_{0}}
at
(b_{0},a,v)
in the
v
-direction (in the
H_{0}
-direction), is the identity map. In particular, it is invertible. Thus, by the implicit mapping theorem (see
[
45
]
), there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
(b_{0},a)
and a unique smooth map
f
on
U
with values in
H_{0}
, such that

(1.3.23)

\displaystyle F^{b_{0}}(b,u,v)=0

if and only if

(1.3.24)

\displaystyle v=f(b,u)

for all
(b,u)\in U
. By the claim it follows then that
f=P
on
U
. Hence,
P
is smooth. To complete the proof, it therefore remains to show that the claim is true. We now do this. By definition,

(1.3.25)

\displaystyle F^{b_{0}}(b,u,v)=0

if and only if

(1.3.26)

\displaystyle P^{b_{0}}((w^{-1})^{b_{0}}(w^{b}(v-u)))=0.

That is, if and only if

(1.3.27)

\displaystyle h_{b_{0}}((w^{-1})^{b_{0}}(w^{b}(v-u)),v_{0})=0

for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
, which by definition of
w^{-1}
is equivalent to

(1.3.28)

\displaystyle(h^{0})_{b_{0}}(w^{b}(v-u),v_{0})=0

for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
. Now, since
h^{0}
is of zero variation,
(h^{0})_{b_{0}}=(h^{0})_{b}
, so this is in turn equivalent to

(1.3.29)

\displaystyle(h^{0})_{b}(w^{b}(v-u),v_{0})=0

for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
. By definition of
w
and
h
, the latter is equivalent to

(1.3.30)

\displaystyle h_{b}(v-u,v_{0})=0

for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
, which in turn is equivalent to

(1.3.31)

\displaystyle P^{b}(v-u)=0.

Finally, since
v\in H_{0}
already,
P^{b}v=v
, so linearity gives that (
1.3.31
) holds if and only if

(1.3.32)

\displaystyle v=P^{b}u.

This proves the claim, which completes the proof.
∎

A very important corollary to Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (and previous results) is now the following:

Corollary 1.3.9
.

With the same assumptions, and the same notation, as in Theorem
1.3.8
, let
\sharp^{0}
denote the adjoint operator for
H^{*}
with respect to
h^{0}
, and suppose that
B\ni b\mapsto(\sharp^{0})^{b}
defined by
(\sharp^{0})^{b}u:=(\sharp^{0}u)^{b}
, for all
u\in H
, gives a map
B\to\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H^{*};H)
. Let
\sharp
denote the adjoint operator with respect to
h
for
H^{*}
, and
\sharp_{0}
the adjoint operator with respect to (the restriction of)
h
(as a hermitian metric on
H_{0}
) for
