(H_{0})^{*}
. Then
\sharp_{0}
is smooth.

As will be seen from the proof, we may replace the assumption that
b\mapsto(\sharp^{0})^{b}
is a map
B\to\text{Iso}_{\mathbb{R}}(H^{*};H)
with the assumption that
\sharp^{0}
is smooth. We then get a slightly shorter and more general result.

Proof of Corollary
1.3.9
.

Let
w
denote the smooth weight map for
h^{0}
of
h
. By Corollary
1.3.5
,
\sharp^{0}
is smooth and by Proposition
1.3.7
, we have
\sharp=w^{-1}\sharp^{0}
. Thus, by Proposition
1.2.1
, we have
\sharp_{0}=P\sharp=P(w^{-1}\sharp^{0})
. Hence it follows by Theorem
1.3.8
that
\sharp_{0}
is smooth.
∎

Corollary
1.3.9
may be seen as a generalization, or a natural analogue, of Lemma
1.1.3
from the introduction. To explain this, let
L^{2}
,
A^{2}
,
U
,
\phi
,
h^{0}
, and
h=\{h_{t}\}_{t\in U}
be as in Example
1.3.2
. We know that
L^{2}
and
A^{2}
are trivial Hilbert bundles over
U
. The dual bundle of
A^{2}
is
(A^{2})^{*}
. Let
\sharp_{A^{2}}
denote the adjoint operator for it with respect to the smooth weighted hermitian metric
h
. By Corollary
1.3.9
, given any smooth local section
\xi^{*}
of
(A^{2})^{*}
,
\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi^{*}
is smooth. Fix
z\in\Omega
and consider
\xi:=\xi_{z}
to be the evaluation map at
z
for elements in
A^{2}
. That is, given any
f\in A^{2}
, we let
\left\langle\xi_{z},f\right\rangle:=f(z)
. It is clear that
\xi
is smooth as a section of
(A^{2})^{*}
. Using the same notation as in the introduction, let
k^{t}
denote the Bergman kernel of
A^{2}_{t}
. Then
\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi(t)
is precisely
k^{t}_{z}
. Thus we see that Lemma
1.1.3
is a special case of the corollary. An interesting generalization of this, which concerns derivatives of the Bergman kernel of
A^{2}_{t}
, is the following:

Example 1.3.3
.

With the same notation as above, let
\xi^{\alpha}_{z}
be defined as an element of
(A^{2})^{*}
by
\left\langle\xi^{\alpha}_{z},f\right\rangle:=\partial^{\alpha}f(z)
, where
\alpha
is a multiindex. We may of course replace
f
with a local section of
A^{2}
. Considering specifically
k^{t}_{w}
, we find (by the reproducing property of the Bergman kernel) that

(1.3.33)

\displaystyle\left\langle\xi^{\alpha}_{z},k^{t}_{w}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=h_{t}(k^{t}_{w},\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi^{\alpha}_{z})=\overline{h_{t}(\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi^{\alpha}_{z},k^{t}_{w})}=\overline{\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi^{\alpha}_{z}(w)}.

On the other hand, by definition of
\xi^{\alpha}_{z}
, this is also equal to
\partial^{\alpha}k^{t}_{w}(z)
. Thus,

(1.3.34)

\displaystyle\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi^{\alpha}_{z}(w)

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial^{\alpha}k^{t}_{w}(z)}=\overline{\partial}^{\alpha}k^{t}_{z}(w),

where the last equality follows by the conjugate symmetric property of the Bergman kernel (that is,
\overline{k^{t}(z,w)}=k^{t}(w,z)
). It follows that
\sharp_{A^{2}}\xi^{\alpha}_{z}=\overline{\partial}^{\alpha}k^{t}_{z}
, and hence by Corollary
1.3.9
that
\overline{\partial}^{\alpha}k^{t}_{z}
is smooth in
t
. The case
\alpha=0
gives back Lemma
1.1.3
.

1.4.
Positivity of Hilbert bundles and plurisubharmonic variation

1.4.1.
Connections and curvatures on holomorphic Hilbert bundles

In the previous section we showed how to carry out step (1) (in proving Theorem
1.1.1
) in the introduction in the (more) general setting of (trivial) Hilbert bundles; recall Corollary
1.3.9
, which is the natural counterpart of Lemma
1.1.3
from the introduction in this setting. In this section we will explore step (2) in the more general setting. The idea is to use what we call
Chern connections
(Definition
1.4.2
) to study, or rather compute, the variations of (squared norms of) smooth sections. This may not make too much sense at the moment; after all, we have yet to elaborate on the terminology. Hopefully, things will make more sense as we go along. Essentially, we are going to use calculus, in much the same way as we have been doing up till now, to compute
\partial\overline{\partial}\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{h}^{2}
, where
u
is a smooth local section of some (trivial) Hilbert bundle equipped with a smooth hermitian metric
h
.

To introduce the notion of
Chern connections
(and, later, also
Chern curvatures
) on (what we call holomorphic) Hilbert bundles, we first extend the notion of local sections of Hilbert bundles to what we may call
Hilbert-bundle-valued differential forms
:

Definition 1.4.1
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a (not necessarily trivial) Hilbert bundle over
B
. Then we define a(n)
(local)
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential
k
-form on (an open subset
U
of)
B
to be an assignment to each
b\in(U\subseteq)B
an
\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
-valued alternating complex-
k
-multilinear map on
T_{b}B\otimes\mathbb{C}
, the (complexification of the) tangent space at
b
of
B
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be as in the definition, and let
U\subseteq B
be open. Locally, with respect to local holomorphic coordinates, say
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
, on
U
, defined say on
V\subseteq U
, an
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential form
u
on
U
takes the form

(1.4.1)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=p,|J|=q}^{{}^{\prime}}u_{I\bar{J}}dt_{I}\wedge d\bar{t}_{J},

where
I,J
are multiindices of respective lengths
p
and
q
such that
p+q=k
,
dt_{I}=dt_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dt_{i_{p}},d\bar{t}_{J}=d\bar{t}_{j_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\bar{t}_{j_{q}}
, the symbol "
′
" in the summation sign designates that we sum over strictly ascending (increasing) multiindices, and
u_{I\bar{J}}
are local sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
over
V
. If we write
u
above as

(1.4.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{p+q=k}u^{p,q},

we shall say that each
u^{p,q}
of
bidegree
or
type

(p,q)
, and we shall call
k

the (total) degree of
u
. In the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
admits a smooth structure,
u
above is smooth if each coefficient
u_{I\bar{J}}
is. We will denote the family of smooth
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential
k
-forms, respectively
(p,q)
-forms, on
U
, by
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\Lambda^{k}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
, respectively
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\Lambda^{(p,q)}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
; in the global case that
U=B
, we simply write
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{k}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
, respectively
\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{(p,q)}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
. Let now
h
be a hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We extend
h
to a real-bilinear (and not quite sesquilinear) form acting on pairs of (local)
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential forms, possibly of different degrees. We denote the extension by the same symbol
h
as the metric, and define it as follows: Consider first pairs of local
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential forms
u
and
v
of the special forms
u=\hat{u}\otimes\xi,v=\hat{v}\otimes\eta
, where
\hat{u}
and
\hat{v}
are local sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and
\xi
and
\eta
are (local) differential forms, possibly of different degrees. For
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential forms of this special form, we let
h
be defined by

(1.4.3)

\displaystyle h(u,v)

\displaystyle:=h(\hat{u},\hat{v})\xi\wedge\overline{\eta}.

We then define
h
in general from this by invoking real-bilinearity and appealing to (
1.4.1
). We are now ready to define
Chern connections
on
holomorphic
Hilbert bundles (over
B
); let us agree to call
\mathscrsfs{H}
a
holomorphic Hilbert bundle
if it admits a holomorphic structure such that there is a well-defined
\overline{\partial}
operator on
B
acting on smooth local sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
which vanishes precisely on the holomorphic ones:

Definition 1.4.2
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and let
h
be a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. A
Chern connection on
\mathscrsfs{H}
with respect to
h
, or simply
a Chern connection for/of
h
, is a complex-linear map
D
with the following properties:

(i)

D
acts on an element
u\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\mathscrsfs{H})
with value
Du\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\Lambda^{1}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
, where
U
is an open subset of
B
.

(ii)

D
splits according to type or bidegree of image points as

(1.4.4)

\displaystyle D=\delta+\overline{\partial},

where
\delta u\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\Lambda^{(1,0)}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
and
\overline{\partial}u\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\Lambda^{(0,1)}(T^{*}B)\otimes\mathscrsfs{H})
, for all
u\in\mathscrsfs{C}^{\infty}(U;\mathscrsfs{H})
, and
U\subseteq B
open.

(iii)

For all smooth local sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
,

(1.4.5)

\displaystyle dh(u,v)

\displaystyle=h(Du,v)+h(u,Dv).

We shall refer to
\delta
as
the (1,0)-part of
D
and to
\overline{\partial}
as
the (0,1)-part of
D
. Often we will also refer to (iii) as
(the) metric compatibility of
D
.

If there exists a Chern connection
D
on
\mathscrsfs{H}
with respect to
h
, where both are as in Definition
1.4.2
, then we shall also say that
\mathscrsfs{H}

admits

D
as Chern connection (with respect to
h
). In the case of holomorphic hermitian vector bundles of finite rank, it is well-known that the Chern connection always exists uniquely. In general, it is not clear to us whether we have existence, but we may ensure uniqueness by adding an additional assumption on
\mathscrsfs{H}
(which immediately holds in the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
is trivial):

Proposition 1.4.1
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and
h
a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Suppose that the following holds: Given
b\in\mathscrsfs{B}
and
a_{b}\in\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
, there exists a smooth (local) section
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
such that
v^{b}=a_{b}
.
Then
\mathscrsfs{H}
admits at most one Chern connection with respect to
h
.

Proof.

Suppose that
D=\delta+\overline{\partial}
and
D^{\prime}=\delta^{\prime}+\overline{\partial}
are two Chern connections for
h
, and let
u
be a smooth (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, say over
U
. Fix
b\in U
and
a_{b}\in\mathscrsfs{H}_{b}
. It suffices by the non-degeneracy of
h_{b}
to show that
h_{b}(((\delta-\delta)^{\prime}u)^{b},a_{b})=0
. By assumption there exists a smooth (local) section
v
(near
b
) of
\mathscrsfs{H}
such that
v^{b}=a_{b}
. Using the metric compatibility of each of the connections and subtracting, we get

(1.4.6)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial h(u,v)-\partial h(u,v)=h((\delta-\delta^{\prime})u,v).

Evaluating this at
b
gives then

(1.4.7)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=h_{b}(((\delta-\delta^{\prime})u)^{b},a_{b}),

which proves the assertion.
∎

While it is not clear to us whether Chern connections exist in general, there is an important case in which the existence of Chern conenction can be established, namely when our Hilbert bundle is trivial. Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
. Then
H
is automatically holomorphic. In the previous section, we discussed two particular types of hermitian metrics on trivial Hilbert bundles. Let us first consider the simplest of these two: Those of zero variation. Observe that an equivalent way of stating Lemma
1.3.1
is that a hermitian metric
h
on
H
is of zero variation if and only if it is smooth and admits the trivial Chern connection
d
. Hence it follows that the Chern connection on
H
with respect to any hermitian metric of zero variation exists and is equal to the trivial connection
d
. Since the connection is trivial, this does not seem to give much, but when this is combined with the next result, we actually get a way of manufacturing a lot of smooth hermitian metrics admitting non-trivial Chern connections. Let us first give the result and then explain this a bit more closely. The result concerns the second type of hermitian metrics with which we have been concerned in the previous section, namely, the weighted ones, and is the following lemma:

Lemma 1.4.2
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
that admits the Chern connection
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{h}
is a smooth weighted hermitian metric on
H
induced by
h
with smooth weight
w
. Then
H
admits the Chern connection
\mathfrak{D}:=\mathfrak{d}+\overline{\partial}
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
, where

(1.4.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}=w^{-1}\circ\delta\circ w:=w^{-1}\delta w.

Proof.

Let
u
and
v
be smooth local sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. By definition of
\mathfrak{h}
and metric compatibility of
D
, we have

(1.4.9)

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{h}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\partial h(wu,v)=h(\delta wu,v)+h(wu,\overline{\partial}v)=\mathfrak{h}(w^{-1}\delta wu,v)+\mathfrak{h}(u,\overline{\partial}v).

This shows that
\mathfrak{D}
satisfies metric compatibility with
\mathfrak{h}
, so the assertion follows by uniqueness (Proposition
1.4.1
).
∎

Using Lemma
1.4.2
, we may (at least in theory) manufacture numerous smooth hermitian metrics on
H
admitting non-trivial Chern connections. The process is as follows: We begin with a smooth hermitian metric on
H
that admits Chern connection. We then consider a new smooth hermitian metric on
H
given as a smooth weighted hermitian metric induced from the former metric. By the lemma, this new metric also admits Chern connection, and there is even an explicit relationship between the ((1,0)-part of the) new and old Chern connections given by twisting with the smooth weight and its inverse. We now repeat the above process with a new smooth weight to get yet another smooth hermitian metric that admits Chern connection, and so on. Of course, this process would be void if we cannot from the outset find a smooth hermitian metric on
H
that admits Chern connection. This is where hermitian metrics of zero variation enter. We know that these always admit the trivial connection, so we may commence the above process using these.

Example 1.4.1
.

Let
H,h^{0}
, and
h
be as in Example
1.3.2
. Then
H
is a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and
h^{0}
is a (smooth) hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
. The metric
h
is a smooth weighted hermitian metric on
H
induced by
h^{0}
with smooth weight
w=e^{-\psi}
. By Lemma
1.4.2
, the Chern connection on
H
with respect to
h
exists and is given by
D:=\delta_{t}+\overline{\partial}_{t}
(where we as usual use
t
-subscripts to emphasize that we are considering differential operators on
B
and not
X
, taking
t
as generic local holomorphic coordinates on
B
), (and) where

(1.4.10)

\displaystyle\delta_{t}

\displaystyle=w^{-1}\partial_{t}w=e^{\psi}\partial_{t}(e^{-\psi})=\partial_{t}-\partial_{t}\psi\wedge;

a formula that might look familiar to many.

Suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
that admits the Chern connection
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Suppose also that
H_{0}\leq H
and that
\overline{H_{0}}^{(H,h)}=H_{0}
, and let
P=P(h)
be the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
. It is natural to ask whether
H_{0}
admits a (necessarily unique) Chern connection with respect to
h
. Let us suppose that the Chern connection does exist and write it as
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
. Let
u
and
v
be smooth (local) sections of
H_{0}
. By metric compatibility of
D_{0}
, we have

(1.4.11)

\displaystyle\partial h(u,v)

\displaystyle=h(\delta_{0}u,v)+h(u,\overline{\partial}v).

On the other, by metric compatibility of
D
we also have

(1.4.12)

\displaystyle\partial h(u,v)

\displaystyle=h(\delta u,v)+h(u,\overline{\partial}v)=h(P\delta u,v)+h(u,\overline{\partial}v);

the last equality following by orthogonality. Comparing, we then get that

(1.4.13)

\displaystyle\delta_{0}u=P\delta u,

which gives that

(1.4.14)

\displaystyle\delta_{0}=P\delta.

Note that we have not assumed any smoothness on
P
from the outset. Nevertheless, we see that the existence of
\delta_{0}
forces
P\delta
to be smooth (when restricted to sections of
H_{0}
). Conversely, we see that if
P\delta
is smooth and we define
\delta_{0}:=P\delta
, then
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
must be the Chern connection of
H_{0}
. Our discussion therefore gives the following result:

Proposition 1.4.3
.

With the above notation and set-up, the Chern connection of
H_{0}
with respect to
h
exists if and only if
P\delta
(restricted to sections of
H_{0}
) is smooth, in which case it is given by
D_{0}=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
, with
\delta_{0}=P\delta
.

In particular, by Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.3.8
) and our discussion above (in which Lemma
1.4.2
plays an important role), it follows that if
h
is a smooth weighted hermitian metric on
H
induced by a hermitian metric of zero variation, then the Chern connection on
H_{0}
with respect to
h
exists (and we also have a formula for it). Let
h^{-1}
be the dual hermitian metric of
h
on the dual bundle
∗
. We may similarly ask whether
D
induces a (necessarily unique) Chern connection on
H^{*}
with respect to
h^{-1}
. From the outset, this is already more subtle than the case of the subbundle. Indeed, it is not even clear whether
h^{-1}
is smooth, so the question may not even make sense without additional assumptions. Adding the assumptions that the associated adjoint operator and its inverse be smooth, we get the following result:

Proposition 1.4.4
.

With the set-up and notation above, let
\sharp
denote the adjoint operator with respect to
h
, and
\sharp^{-1}
its inverse. Assume that both
\sharp
and
\sharp^{-1}
are smooth. Then the Chern connection for
H^{*}
with respect to
h^{-1}
exists and is given by

(1.4.15)

\displaystyle D^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\vee}+\overline{\partial}^{\vee},

where

(1.4.16)

\displaystyle\delta^{\vee}=\sharp^{-1}\overline{\partial}\sharp

and

(1.4.17)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{\vee}=\sharp^{-1}\delta\sharp.

Proof.

Let
u^{*}
and
v^{*}
be smooth (local) sections of
H^{*}
. By metric compatibility of
D
and the definition of
h^{-1}
,

(1.4.18)

\displaystyle\partial h^{-1}(u^{*},v^{*})

\displaystyle=h^{-1}(\sharp^{-1}\overline{\partial}\sharp u^{*},v^{*})+h^{-1}(u^{*},\sharp^{-1}\delta\sharp v^{*}).

This shows by our assumptions that
D^{\vee}
as defined satisfies metric compatibility with
h^{-1}
. To complete the proof it remains only to check that
\overline{\partial}u^{*}=0
precisely when
\overline{\partial}^{\vee}u^{*}=0
. In fact, we actually have

(1.4.19)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}=\sharp^{-1}\delta\sharp.

To see this, let
u^{*}
be smooth and let
v\in H
. By the chain rule,

(1.4.20)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle=\left\langle\overline{\partial}u^{*},v\right\rangle.

Since, by definition of
\sharp
, the latter is also equal to
h(v,\sharp\overline{\partial}^{*}u)
, we get that

(1.4.21)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle=h(v,\sharp\overline{\partial}u^{*}).

On the other hand, we also have by metric compatibility of
D
, and the definition of
\sharp
again (applied to
u^{*}
now instead of
\overline{\partial}u^{*}
), that

(1.4.22)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle=\overline{\partial}h(v,\sharp u^{*})=h(v,\delta\sharp u^{*}).

Hence we have

(1.4.23)

\displaystyle h(v,\sharp\overline{\partial}u^{*})=h(v,\delta\sharp u^{*}),

which gives
\sharp\overline{\partial}u^{*}=\sharp\delta u^{*}
, and hence (
1.4.19
).
∎

Note that in the case that
h
is of zero variation, we recover Corollary
1.3.5
part (ii) from Proposition
1.4.4
. The proposition therefore gives a more general description of holomorphic sections of dual bundles: In the case of hermitian metrics of zero variation, a smooth (local) section
u^{*}
of the dual bundle is holomorphic precisely when the the adjoint of
u^{*}
(assuming additionally that the adjoint operator and its inverse are smooth) with respect to the metric vanishes under the action of the
\partial
-operator. If
h
is on the other hand more generally only assumed to be smooth with a (necessarily unique) Chern connection, then
u^{*}
is holomorphic precisely when its adjoint with respect to
h
(still assuming the adjoint operator and its inverse are smooth) vanishes under the action of the
(1,0)
-part of the Chern connection. This is indeed more general since the
(1,0)
-part of the trivial connection, which is the Chern connection of any hermitian metric of zero variation, is precisely equal to
\partial
.

We have looked at the Chern connections on
H_{0}
and
H^{*}
, with respect to the natural hermitian metrics (induced by
h
) on these. Let us lastly discuss, in a similar fashion, the case of the quotient bundle. Thus, let
Q:=H/H_{0}
be the quotient bundle of
H_{0}
in
H
. We ask whether the Chern connection of
Q
with respect to
h^{Q}
, the quotient hermitian metric on
Q
induced by
h
, exists, and have then the following ”quotient analogue” of Proposition
1.4.4
:

Proposition 1.4.5
.

With the above set-up and notation, let
P=P(h)
denote the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
, and let
m_{l}
denote the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h
. Assume that
P
is smooth. Then the Chern connection of
Q
with respect to
h^{Q}
exists and is given by

(1.4.24)

\displaystyle D^{Q}

\displaystyle=\delta^{Q}+\overline{\partial}^{Q},

where

(1.4.25)

\displaystyle\delta^{Q}=m_{l}^{-1}\delta m_{l}

and

(1.4.26)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{Q}=m_{l}^{-1}\overline{\partial}^{\perp}m_{l},

and we use the notation
\overline{\partial}^{\perp}=(1-P)\overline{\partial}
.

Proof.

Recall that
P
is smooth if and only if
m_{l}
is smooth. Thus,
m_{l}
is smooth under our assumptions. Let
u^{\perp}_{0}
be a smooth (local) section of
H_{0}^{\perp_{h}}
, and let
\pi_{Q}:H\to Q
denote the canonical projection map. Then
[u]:=\pi_{Q}\circ u^{\perp}_{0}
is smooth and satisfies
m_{l}[u]=u_{0}^{\perp}
. Thus,
m_{l}^{-1}u_{0}^{\perp}=[u]
is smooth. Therefore
m_{l}^{-1}
is also smooth. We claim that
\overline{\partial}^{Q}=\overline{\partial}
as an operator acting on smooth local sections of
Q
. Let
[u]
be a smooth (local) section of
Q
. It suffices to show that
[u]
is holomorphic if and only if
\overline{\partial}^{Q}[u]=0
. Let
h^{0}
denote the hermitian metric of zero variation induced by the fixed inner product on
H
, and let
m_{l}^{0}
denote the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h^{0}
. By Proposition
1.3.6
,
[u]
is holomorphic if and only if
m_{l}^{0}[u]
is holomorphic, which by Corollary
1.3.2
is if and only if
\overline{\partial}m_{l}^{0}[u]=0
. By orthogonal decomposition we may write

(1.4.27)

\displaystyle m_{l}^{0}[u]

\displaystyle=Pm_{l}^{0}[u]\oplus m_{l}(m_{l}^{0}[u])=Pm_{l}^{0}\oplus m_{l}[u],

so we find that
[u]
is holomorphic if and only if

(1.4.28)

\displaystyle-\overline{\partial}P(m_{l}^{0}[u])

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}m_{l}[u].

The key observation now is that the left-hand side of this identity is
H_{0}
-valued. It therefore follows that the identity holds if and only if the
H^{\perp_{h}}
-valued part of the right-hand side vanishes, which is precisely means
\overline{\partial}^{Q}[u]=0
. To complete the proof it remains to explain that
\delta m_{l}
lies in the image of
m_{l}
so that we can apply
m_{l}^{-1}
to it; that is,
\delta^{Q}
is well-defined, and verify that
D^{Q}
as defined satisfies metric compatibility with
h^{Q}
. Indeed, if we can do this, then the assertion follows by uniqueness (Proposition
1.4.1
). To see that
\delta m_{l}
lies in the image of
m_{l}
, it suffices to check that
h(\delta m_{l}[u],v_{0})=0
for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
. By orthogonality,
h(m_{l}[u],v_{0})=0
for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
. Applying
\partial
to this, and using the the definition of
D
, we get that
0=h(\delta m_{l}[u],v_{0})=0
for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
as required. It remains to verify metric compatibility, but this follows immediately from the definition of
h^{Q},D
,
D^{Q}
, and orthogonality (
h(m_{l}[u],\overline{\partial}m_{l}[v])=h(m_{l}[u],\overline{\partial}^{\perp}m_{l}[v])
).
∎

Analogous to how Proposition
1.4.4
may be seen to generalize Corollary
1.3.5
, so can Proposition
1.4.5
be seen to generalize Proposition
1.3.6
. Indeed, note that

(1.4.29)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}m_{l}[u]=\overline{\partial}^{\perp}m_{l}[u]

for all smooth (local) sections
[u]
of
Q
in the case that
h
is of zero variation.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
that admits the Chern connection
D
. We shall next define what we call the
Chern curvature of
D
. To to do this, we first extend
D
from acting on smooth local sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
to acting on smooth local
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential forms of total degree greater than 0; we denote as usual the extension using the same symbol
D
as the connection. Let
f
be a smooth (local) complex-valued function on (some open subset of)
B
. By metric compatibility, if
u
is a smooth (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, we have that

(1.4.30)

\displaystyle D(fu)

\displaystyle=df\wedge u+fDu.

We shall often refer to (
1.4.30
) as
the Leibniz rule for
D
; for obvious reasons. To extend
D
, we first define it on smooth (local)
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential forms of the form
u=\omega\wedge\hat{u}
, where
\omega
is a smooth (local) differential form of degree
\text{deg}(\omega)
, and
\hat{u}
is a smooth (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, by

(1.4.31)

\displaystyle D(\omega\wedge\hat{u})

\displaystyle:=d\omega\wedge\hat{u}+(-1)^{\text{deg}(\omega)}\omega\wedge D\hat{u}.

We then define
D
in general from this by requiring it to be complex-linear. The extension of
D
satisfies ”an extended” metric compatibility of the form:

(1.4.32)

\displaystyle Dh(u,v)

\displaystyle=h(Du,v)+(-1)^{\text{deg}(u)}h(u,Dv),

where
u
and
v
are smooth (local)
\mathscrsfs{H}
-valued differential forms, possibly of different degrees, and the total degree of
u
is
\text{deg}(u)
. We may now define (the)
Chern curvature
(of
D
) as follows:

Definition 1.4.3
.

Suppose that
D
is a Chern connection. Then we define its
Chern curvature
to be
D^{2}:\equiv D\circ D
.

We shall generically denote the Chern curvature of
D
by
\Theta
, or
\Theta^{D}
if we want to emphasize the connection. In the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
is a finitely-ranked vector bundle it is well-known and easy to see that
\Theta
is a zeroth-order differential operator, a tensor. The same is true, and just as easy to see, also in the our current setting. That is, if
f
is a smooth (local) complex-valued function and
u
is a smooth (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, then

(1.4.33)

\displaystyle\Theta(fu)

\displaystyle=D(df\wedge u+fDu)=d^{2}f\wedge u-df\wedge Du+df\wedge Du+f\Theta u=f\Theta u.

Since
D
is a differential operator of order 1 by the (
1.4.30
), one might expect that the curvature is a second-order differential operator, but somewhat surprisingly it thus turns out that the curvature is a tensor. Let us write
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
and denote its (Chern) curvature by
\Theta
. One may easily check that
\delta^{2}=0
under the assumptions in Proposition
1.4.1
. As a result,
\Theta
is in that setting always of type (1,1) and equal to
\delta\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}\delta
. More generally, it is true without any further assumptions, that for all smooth (local) sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, we have

(1.4.34)

\displaystyle h(\delta^{2}u,v)

\displaystyle=0

and

(1.4.35)

\displaystyle h(\Theta u,v)

\displaystyle=h((\delta\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}\delta)u,v).

To see this we may use (
1.4.32
). A similar argument, using instead
d^{2}
, shows that
\Theta
in general also satisfies the following antisymmetric property:

(1.4.36)

\displaystyle h(\Theta u,v)

\displaystyle=-h(u,\Theta v)

for all smooth (local) sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We will express this property by writing

(1.4.37)

\displaystyle\Theta^{*}=-\Theta.

1.4.2.
The subbundle and quotient bundle curvature formulae

Now, suppose that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
, that
\overline{\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, and that
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
is a Chern connection for
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
with respect to
h
. Let
u
and
v
be smooth (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. By metric compatibility of
D
and
D_{0}
, we have

(1.4.38)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=h((\delta-\delta_{0})u,v).

This shows that the operator
\delta-\delta_{0}=D-D_{0}
satisfies a certain orthogonality property. We shall give this operator a special name:

Definition 1.4.4
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
that admits a Chern connection
D
. Suppose also that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
, that
\overline{\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, and that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
admits a Chern connection
D_{0}
with respect to
h
. Then we define the
second fundamental form associated with
D
and
D_{0}
to be
D-D_{0}
.

For fixed connections
D
and
D_{0}
(such as above) we shall generically denote the second fundamental form associated with
D
and
D_{0}
by
S_{f}
. Using the orthogonality property of
S_{f}
, we may now prove the following generalization of a subbundle curvature formula of Griffiths’s from the setting of finitely ranked holomorphic hermitian vector bundles (
[
58
]
); henceforth, referred to as
the subbundle curvature formula
:

Theorem 1.4.6

(The subbundle curvature formula)
.

Suppose that
D,D_{0}
and
S_{f}
are as above, and denote the Chern curvature of
D
and
D_{0}
by respectively
\Theta
and
\Theta_{0}
. Then we have the following subbundle curvature formula:

(1.4.39)

\displaystyle\Theta_{0}

\displaystyle=\Theta-S_{f}^{*}S_{f},

by which we shall mean more precisely the following: For all smooth (local) sections
u
and
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, we have:

(1.4.40)

\displaystyle h(\Theta_{0}u,v)

\displaystyle=h(\Theta u,v)-h(S_{f}u,S_{f}v).

Proof.

Let
u
and
v
be smooth (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, and let us write
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
and
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
. Note that while we actually do not know in the current setting whether it is true that
\delta^{2}_{(0)}=0
, and hence that
\Theta_{(0)}=\delta_{(0)}\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}\delta_{(0)}
, it
is
true by our discussion above that

(1.4.41)

\displaystyle h(\delta_{(0)}^{2}u,v)

\displaystyle=0,

and hence that

(1.4.42)

\displaystyle h(\Theta_{(0)}u,v)

\displaystyle=h((\delta_{(0)}\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}\delta_{(0)})u,v).

Thus we find; for simplicity we write
S_{f}
as
S
,

(1.4.43)

\displaystyle h((\Theta_{0}-\Theta)u,v)

\displaystyle=-h((S\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}S)u,v).

By the orthogonality property of
S
,
h(S\overline{\partial}u,v)=0
, so we are left with

(1.4.44)

\displaystyle h((\Theta_{0}-\Theta)u,v)

\displaystyle=-h(\overline{\partial}Su,v).

By the orthogonality property of
S
again,
h(Su,v)=0
. Thus applying
\overline{\partial}
to this we get (recall (
1.4.31
))

(1.4.45)

\displaystyle h(\overline{\partial}Su,v)=h(Su,\delta v)=h(Su,Sv),

where the last equality follows by orthogonality. Substituting this we get the desired curvature formula.
∎

Note that in the case that
H
is trivial, it follows by Proposition
1.4.3
that
D_{0}=PD
, where
P=P(h)
is the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
. Then it is immediate that the image of
S=D-D_{0}
is
H_{0}^{\perp}
-valued. In the more general case we do not know that we have
D_{0}=PD
, but we still get an orthogonality property of
S
and the subbundle curvature formula above nonetheless. Using a similar argument as in the proof of the subbundle curvature formula (Theorem
1.4.6
above), we may also prove a corresponding formula for the curvature of the quotient bundle
Q:=H/H_{0}
in the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}:=H
and
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}:=H_{0}
are trivial, and
P
is smooth; note that in this case, Proposition
1.4.5
gives an explicit formula for the Chern connection of
Q
with respect to the quotient hermitian metric
h^{Q}
on
Q
induced by
h
. We shall refer to this curvature formula as the
quotient bundle curvature formula
. It is given by the following theorem:

Theorem 1.4.7

(The quotient bundle curvature formula)
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
that admits the Chern connection
D
. Let
H_{0}\leq H
with
\overline{H_{0}}^{(H,h)}=H_{0}
, and suppose that
P=P(h)
, the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
, is smooth. Let
Q:=H/H_{0}
, and let
D^{Q}
denote the Chern connection of
Q
with respect to
h^{Q}
, the quotient hermitian metric on
Q
induced by
h
. Let
m_{l}
denote the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h
, and let
\overline{\partial}_{0}:=P\overline{\partial}
. Denote the Chern curvature of
D
and
D^{Q}
by respectively
\Theta
and
\Theta^{Q}
. Then the following quotient bundle curvature formula holds:

(1.4.46)

\displaystyle m_{l}\Theta^{Q}

\displaystyle=\Theta m_{l}-(\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l})^{*}(\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}),

by which we shall mean more precisely the following: For all smooth (local) sections
[u]
and
[v]
of
Q
, we have:

(1.4.47)

\displaystyle h^{Q}(\Theta^{Q}[u],[v])

\displaystyle=h(\Theta m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])-h(\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[u],\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[v]).

Proof.

Let
[u]
and
[v]
be smooth (local) sections of
Q
, and let us also write
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. By Proposition
1.4.5
, we have
D^{Q}=\delta^{Q}+\overline{\partial}^{Q}
with
\delta^{Q}=m_{l}^{-1}\delta m_{l}
and
\overline{\partial}^{Q}=m_{l}^{-1}\overline{\partial}^{\perp}m_{l}
, where
\overline{\partial}^{\perp}=(1-P)\overline{\partial}
. From our discussion above we also have:

(1.4.48)

\displaystyle h^{Q}(\Theta^{Q}[u],[v])

\displaystyle=h^{Q}((\delta^{Q}\overline{\partial}^{Q}+\overline{\partial}^{Q}\delta^{Q})[u],[v])

and

(1.4.49)

\displaystyle h(\Theta m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])

\displaystyle=h((\delta\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}\delta)m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v]).

By definition of
h^{Q}
and
D^{Q}
, we thus get

(1.4.50)

\displaystyle h^{Q}(\Theta^{Q}[u],[v])

\displaystyle=h((\delta\overline{\partial}^{\perp}+\overline{\partial}^{\perp}\delta)m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v]).

Hence,

(1.4.51)

\displaystyle h^{Q}(\Theta^{Q}[u],[v])-h(\Theta m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])

\displaystyle=-h((\delta\overline{\partial}_{0}+\overline{\partial}_{0}\delta)m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v]).

By orthogonality,
h(\overline{\partial}_{0}\delta m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])=0
, so it remains to deal with
h(\delta\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])
. By orthogonality again,
h(\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])=0
. Applying
\partial
to this, and using the definition of
D
, we get

(1.4.52)

\displaystyle h(\delta\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[u],m_{l}[v])

\displaystyle=h(\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[u],\overline{\partial}m_{l}[v])=(\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[u],\overline{\partial}_{0}m_{l}[v]),

where the last equality follows by orthogonality. Substituting this we get the desired curvature formula.
∎

We have discussed a subbundle curvature formula and a quotient bundle formula. It therefore stands to reason to ask for a formula for the curvature of the dual bundle of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We comment on the case, similar to that of the quotient bundle, that
\mathscrsfs{H}:=H
is trivial. Let
\sharp
denote the adjoint operator with respect to
h
, and suppose that
\sharp
and
\sharp^{-1}
are smooth. Then we have an explicit formula for the Chern connection
D^{\vee}
of the dual bundle
H^{*}
with respect to the dual hermitian metric
h^{-1}
of
h
, by Proposition
1.4.4
. Namely, if we write
D^{\vee}=\delta^{\vee}+\overline{\partial}^{\vee}
, then we have
\delta^{\vee}=\sharp^{-1}\overline{\partial}\sharp
and
\overline{\partial}^{\vee}=\sharp^{-1}\delta\sharp
. Let
\Theta^{\vee}
denote the Chern curvature of
D^{\vee}
, and
\Theta
the Chern curvature of
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
, the Chern connection of
H
with respect to
h
. By our discussion above, we have in this situation that

(1.4.53)

\displaystyle\Theta^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\vee}\overline{\partial}^{\vee}+\overline{\partial}^{\vee}\delta^{\vee}=\sharp^{-1}\Theta\sharp.

Let
u^{*}
and
v^{*}
be two smooth (local) sections of
H^{*}
. Thus, by definition of
h^{-1}
and the antisymmetric property
\Theta=-\Theta^{*}
of the curvature
\Theta
,

(1.4.54)

\displaystyle h^{-1}(\Theta^{\vee}u^{*},v^{*})=h(\sharp v^{*},\Theta^{\vee}\sharp u^{*})=-h(\Theta\sharp u^{*},\sharp v^{*}).

As a short-hand form we shall write this as

(1.4.55)

\displaystyle\Theta^{\vee}

\displaystyle=-\Theta\sharp

This identity says that
\Theta^{\vee}
, the curvature for
H^{*}
, and
\Theta
, the curvature for
H
, in some sense are
negative
of each other. The precise sense in which the two are negative of each other is part of our upcoming discussion(s).

1.4.3.
Positivity notions and variational formulae

We are now ready to explore step (2) in proving Theorem
1.1.1
from the introduction in our more general setting. Recall, using the same notation as in the introduction, that this is to compute
i\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}_{t}\log K(z)
given that
K(z)
is smooth (from step (1)). Looking back at Corollary
1.3.9
and Example
1.3.3
, the counterpart of this in our current setting is then the following: Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a Hilbert bundle over
B
with a smooth structure, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Assume also that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
with
\overline{\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, and let
\sharp_{0}
denote the adjoint operator for
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}^{*}
with respect to
h
. Assuming that
\sharp_{0}
is smooth, which, as we have seen, corresponds to
K(z)
being smooth (recall Example
1.3.3
and its prequel remarks), the counterpart to computing
\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}_{t}\log K(z)
is now to compute
i\partial\overline{\partial}\log\left|\left|\sharp_{0}u^{*}\right|\right|_{h}^{2}
, say for
\sharp_{0}u^{*}
not vanishing. Discarding the logarithm (and the constant
i
), this motivates us to compute and study ”second-order derivatives” of the squared norms of smooth (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
of the form
\partial\overline{\partial}\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
. We shall think of and refer to these second-order derivatives as
variations of second order
. The key observation here is to note that
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}=h(u,u)
, so these variations can be computed by means of Chern connections (presuming existence). Let
f
be a two times differentiable function of real variables. We introduce the following notation: The first order variations
\mathscrsfs{V}^{1}_{f(h)}
and
\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1}}_{f(h)}
are the operators acting on any smooth (local) section
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
given by:

(1.4.56)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle:=\partial f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\quad\quad\text{ and }

(1.4.57)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\partial}f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}),

and the second order variations
\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}
and
\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1},1}_{f(h)}
are the operators acting on any smooth (local) section
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
given by:

(1.4.58)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle:=\partial\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)\quad\quad\text{ and }

(1.4.59)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1},1}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\partial}\mathscrsfs{V}^{1}_{f(h)}(u).

We simply write
f(h)
as
h
in the case that
f
is the function 1. With the above notation it follows immediately that we have the second-order variational formula:

(1.4.60)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}

\displaystyle=f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{h}^{2})\mathscrsfs{V}^{1}_{h}\wedge\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1}}_{h}+f^{\prime}(\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}.

By a direct computation we furthermore have the following basic, but important, second-order variational formulae; the rest of what we do in the paper will be based on these formulae (and on formula
1.4.60
):

Lemma 1.4.8
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
that admits a Chern connection
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Let
\Theta
denote the Chern curvature of
D
. Then we have the following second-order variational formula:

(1.4.61)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\partial}+\delta^{*}\delta-\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}+(\overline{\partial}\delta)^{*},

by which we more precisely mean the following: For all smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, we have:

(1.4.62)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}(u)

\displaystyle=h(\delta\overline{\partial}u,u)+\left|\left|\delta u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}-\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+h(u,\overline{\partial}\delta u).

In particular, we have:

(i)

(1.4.63)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}|_{\text{ker}(\overline{\partial})}=-\Theta+\delta^{*}\delta,

by which we more precisely the following: For all holomorphic (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, we have

(1.4.64)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}(u)

\displaystyle=-h(\Theta u,u)+\left|\left|\delta u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}.

(ii)

(1.4.65)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{\text{ker}(\delta)}

\displaystyle=\Theta-\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial},

by which we more precisely mean the following: For all smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
such that
\delta u=0
, we have

(1.4.66)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}(u)

\displaystyle=h(\Theta u,u)-\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}.

Proof.

The first formula follows from (
1.4.31
) and the definition of
D
. Then (ii) follows since
h(\Theta u,u)=h((\delta\overline{\partial}+\overline{\partial}\delta)u,u)
for all smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Finally, (i) follows in the same as (ii) by using that
\Theta=-\Theta^{*}
.
∎

The connection between complex Brunn-Minkowski theory and the above variational formulae in Lemma
1.4.8
, is that if
i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}(u)\geq 0
as a (1,1)-form, then
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic on the domain of definition of
u
. We also see from the above formulae that there is an intimate relationship between variations of smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, say such that
\delta u=0
, and the positivity of
ih(\Theta u,u)
. Indeed, from (ii) in Lemma
1.4.8
, it follows immediately that if
ih(\Theta u,u)\geq 0
for such a
u
, then
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic on its domain of definition. This motivates the following definition of
Griffiths (semi)positivity of
\Theta
:

Definition 1.4.5
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
that admits the Chern connection
D
, and let
\Theta
denote the Chern curvature of
D
. Then we shall say that
\Theta
is
semipositive in the sense of Griffiths
, or
Griffiths semipositive
, and write
i\Theta\geq_{G}0
, if

(1.4.67)

\displaystyle h(i\Theta u,u)

\displaystyle\geq 0

for all smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We shall also say that
\Theta
is
positive in the sense of Griffiths
, or
Griffiths positive
, and write
i\Theta>_{G}0
, if the inequality above is strict.

The Griffiths (semi)positivity of
\Theta
(in Definition
1.4.5
) may also be described as follows: Let us choose
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
as generic holomorphic local coordinates on
B
, and let us write
\Theta
with respect to these locally as
\Theta=\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\Theta_{j\bar{k}}dt_{j}\wedge d\bar{t}_{j}
;
[\Theta_{j\bar{k}}]
is a matrix whose entries at each point (in some open subset of
B
) are complex-linear maps that act on (local) smooth sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Then
\Theta
is Griffiths semipositive if at each point
b

(1.4.68)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u,u)(b)\xi_{j}\bar{\xi}_{k}\geq 0

for all smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and all
\xi:=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
;
\Theta
is Griffiths positive if the above holds with equality only when
\xi=\mathbf{0}
or
u(b)=0
. This description of Griffiths (semi)positivity leads to a stronger notion of (semi)positivity for
\Theta
, namely what we call
Nakano (semi)positivity
:

Definition 1.4.6
.

We shall say that
\Theta
is
semipositive in the sense of Nakano
, or
Nakano semipositive
, and write
i\Theta\geq_{N}0
, if at each point
b\in B
,

(1.4.69)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})(b)\geq 0

for all smooth (local) sections
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We shall also say that
\Theta
is
positive in the sense of Nakano
, or
Nakano positive
, and write
i\Theta>_{N}0
, if the above inequality is strict unless
u_{j}(b)=0
for all
j
.

By choosing
u_{j}:=\xi_{j}u
we see that Nakano (semi)positivity implies Griffiths (semi)positive. We define Griffiths and Nakano (semi)
negativity
of
\Theta
of course in a similar fashion, using the opposite inequalities of those above, and the notation
i\Theta\leq_{G}0
(or
i\Theta<_{G}0
) and
i\Theta\leq_{N}0
(or
i\Theta<_{N}0
). We may now interpret the earlier identity (
1.4.55
) as
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\vee}
being ”negative of each other” in the precise sense that

(1.4.70)

\displaystyle i\Theta_{G}\geq 0\iff i\Theta^{\vee}_{G}\leq 0.

What we have said earlier subsequent to Lemma
1.4.8
may be formulated using the notion of Griffiths (semi)positivity as the following result:

Corollary 1.4.9
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
1.4.8
, and using the same notation as in there, suppose that
i\Theta_{G}\geq 0
. Then
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic on its domain of definition for any smooth (local) section
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
such that
\delta u=0
.

As mentioned, the corollary follows directly from Lemma
1.4.8
. Note that the lemma, however, says more. Indeed, due to the term
-\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
, we may allow for some negativity of the curvature. Note also that if we
first fix

u
, then the variational formula shows that it is sufficient that
ih(\Theta u,u)-i\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\geq 0
as a (1,1)-form for just the particular chosen
u
. A stronger statement, which is also somehow more in line with the variation of Bergman kernels, would be that even
\log\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic. This does indeed hold under the same assumptions, and can be seen from the following general result:

Theorem 1.4.10
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
1.4.8
, and using the same notation as in there and above, suppose that
i\Theta\geq_{G}0
and that
f=f(x)
is strictly increasing and concave. Assume moreover that for all
x>0
,
f
satisfies that

(1.4.71)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x\geq 0.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Then
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+\epsilon)
is plurisubharmonic for all smooth (local) sections
u
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
such that
\delta u=0
.

Proof.

Let
u
be a section such as asserted. For simplicity we put
\epsilon:=0
assuming that
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\neq 0
. Since
\delta u=0
, it follows by Lemma
1.4.8
, (ii), that
\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{h}(u)=h(\Theta u,u)-\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
. By a direct computation (using the definition of
D
) we also get
\mathscrsfs{V}^{1}_{h}(u)=h(u,\overline{\partial}u)
and
\mathscrsfs{V}^{\bar{1}}_{h}(u)=h(\overline{\partial}u,u)
. Hence by (
1.4.60
),

(1.4.72)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle=f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left|h(u,\overline{\partial}u)\right|^{2}+f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left(h(\Theta u,u)-\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right),

where we use the notation
\left|h(u,\overline{\partial}u)\right|^{2}:=h(u,\overline{\partial}u)\wedge h(\overline{\partial}u,u)
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have that

(1.4.73)

\displaystyle i\left|h(u,\overline{\partial}u)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-i\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h},

and since
f
is concave,
f^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
, so it follows that

(1.4.74)

\displaystyle if^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left|h(u,\overline{\partial}u)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq-if^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}.

Substituting this into the above, we therefore have

(1.4.75)

\displaystyle i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle\geq if^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})h(\Theta u,u)-i\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left(f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})+f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right).

Since
f
is assumed to be strictly increasing,
f^{\prime}>0
, so we may divide by
f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
. The assertion now follows from noting that
i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)
gives that
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic, and by considering
x:=\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
.
∎

Note that if we let
x>0
and
f(x):=\log(x)
, then
f
is strictly increasing and concave (on the positive real line) with
1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x=0
, so the result on logarithmic plurisubharmonicity does indeed follow from Theorem
1.4.10
. Note also that we can get rid of
\epsilon
altogether by considering
f
above to be defined on
[0,\infty)
and satisfy that
1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x\geq 0
there; of course,
f
should still be strictly increasing concave and twice differentiable. The previous version is recovered by considering
f_{\epsilon}(x):=f(x+\epsilon)
for each
\epsilon>0
.

Above we have used (ii) in Lemma
1.4.8
. Using (i) in Lemma
1.4.8
instead, gives a similar result for holomorphic (local) sections of
\mathscrsfs{H}
to which we shall come back below. Note also that the actual proof of the theorem gives a more general result that what is stated: If we define
x:=\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+\epsilon
and let
x
be a function on some open subset
U
of
B
on which
u
is defined, then
f(x)
is plurisubharmonic on the set of all
b\in U
such that
1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x\geq 0
. We also see that regardless of the positivity assumption on
\Theta
, we in general have the inequality

(1.4.76)

\displaystyle i\frac{\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\geq ih(\Theta u,u)-i\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left(1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+\epsilon)}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+\epsilon)}\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right).

To use Theorem
1.4.10
, we need to exploit the condition
\delta u=0
. One scenario in which we can do this is as follows: With the same set-up as in Lemma
1.4.8
, consider the dual bundle
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and assume that it is holomorphic. Let
u
be a smooth (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
, and define the (local) section
u^{*}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}^{*}
by

(1.4.77)

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle:=h(v,u)

for all (local) sections
v
of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. We then get the following lemma:

Lemma 1.4.11
.

With the above notation and set-up, assume furthermore that the following holds: If
h(v,\delta u)=0
for all
v
holomorphic, then
\delta u=0
. Then
\delta u=0
if
u^{*}
is holomorphic.

Proof.

If
u^{*}
and
v
are holomorphic,
\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle
is holomorphic by composition. Thus,
h(v,u)
is holomorphic so
\overline{\partial}h(u,v)=h(u,\delta u)=0
since
v
is holomorphic, and by definition of
D
. By our assumptions this implies that
\delta u=0
.
∎

In particular the lemma holds when
H
is trivial. Indeed, then
h(v,\delta u)=0
for all
constant

v
already gives
\delta u=0
. In that case we also automatically have
h(v,\delta u)=0
for all
v
holomorphic if
u^{*}
is holomorphic. Considering Example
1.3.3
and replacing the Hilbert bundle above with a suitable
subbundle
of it, we get the following generalization of, or counterpart to, the variation of Bergman kernels from the introduction (Theorem
1.1.1
):

Theorem 1.4.12
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
that admits the Chern connection
D=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Let
h^{-1}
denote the dual hermitian metric of
h
for
H^{*}
, and
\sharp
the adjoint operator for
H^{*}
with respect to
h
. Suppose that
\sharp
is smooth, that
H_{0}\leq H
with
\overline{H_{0}}^{(H,h)}=H_{0}
, and that the total orthogonal projection map
P=P(h)
associated with
h
is smooth. Let
D_{0}
denote the Chern connection on
H_{0}
with respect to
h
, and
\Theta_{0}
its Chern curvature. Suppose that
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{G}0
. Then for all holomorphic local sections
u^{*}
of
H_{0}
, and all strictly increasing and concave second-order differentiable functions
f
on
[0,\infty)
,
f(\left|\left|u^{*}\right|\right|_{h^{-1}}^{2})
is plurisubharmonic on the domain of definition of
u^{*}
intersected with the set of all
b\in B
such that writing
x=x(b)
, we have

(1.4.78)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x\geq 0.

In particular,
\log(\left|\left|u^{*}\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{-1}})
is purisubharmonic on the whole domain of definition of
u^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
u^{*}
be a holomorphic (local) section of
H_{0}^{*}
, and let
\sharp_{0}
denote the adjoint operator for
H_{0}^{*}
with respect to
h
. Then if we let
u:=\sharp_{0}u^{*}
, it follows that
\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle=h(v,u)
for all (local) sections
v
of
H_{0}^{*}
, and moreover that
\left|\left|u^{*}\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{-1}}=\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
. Since
\sharp
is assumed smooth,
\sharp_{0}
is smooth by Proposition
1.2.1
and the assumption that
P
is smooth. Hence
u
is smooth. Let us write
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
(
D_{0}
exists by Proposition
1.4.3
, and we moreover have
\delta_{0}=P\delta
). The assertion now follows by applying Theorem
1.4.10
to
H_{0}
instead of
H
, since
\delta_{0}u=0
as
u^{*}
is holomorphic (if
u^{*}
is holomorphic, then
\left\langle u^{*},v\right\rangle=h(v,u)
is holomorphic whenever
v
is holomorphic, by composition, so we get
\overline{\partial}h(v,u)=h(v,\delta_{0}u)=0
by definition of
D_{0}
).
∎

In particular, the conclusion of Theorem
1.4.12
holds by Berndtsson’s regularity theorem (Theorem
1.3.8
), under the assumptions that
h
is a smooth weighted hermitian metric induced by a hermitian metric of zero variation
h^{0}
, that
H_{0}
is closed in
H
with respect to both these metrics, and that the adjoint operator with respect to
h^{0}
is smooth (recall Proposition
1.3.7
).

We next consider the case of holomorphic (local) sections, that is, (i) in Lemma
1.4.8
above. An immediate analogue in this case to Theorem
1.4.10
is the following:

Theorem 1.4.13
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
\mathscrsfs{H}
that admits a Chern connection
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
with Chern curvature
\Theta
. Let
f=f(x)
be a twice differentiable function on
[0,\infty)
which is strictly increasing and concave, and let
u
be a holomorphic (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
. Then

(1.4.79)

\displaystyle i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle\geq h(-i\Theta u,u)+\left|\left|\delta u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left(f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})+f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right).

In particular, if
i\Theta\leq_{G}0
, then
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisuharmonic where

(1.4.80)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})}\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\geq 0.

Proof.

The first assertion follows by (
1.4.60
) and Lemma
1.4.8
(i) together with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (similar as in the proof of Theorem
1.4.10
since
f
is assumed concave). The second assertion follows then by dividing the first inequality by
f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})>0
and the definition of Griffith (semi)negativity of
\Theta
.
∎

Using the same notation and assumptions as in the theorem, Theorem
1.4.13
says that if
i\Theta_{G}\leq 0
, then
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic if
f
is such that we always have
1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x
for
x>0
. We may ask whether the converse is true. That is, if
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic for all holomorphic
u
and such
f
’s, whether it follows that
i\Theta\leq_{G}0
. In the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
is trivial, this is indeed the case. To prove this, we shall have use for the following preliminary result, which may also be of independent interest in itself:

Lemma 1.4.14
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
which admits the Chern connection
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Let
a\in H
and
b\in B
. Then there exist an open neighbourhood
U
of
b
and a holomorphic local section
u
of
H
over
U
such that
u^{b}=a
and
\delta u(b)=0
.

The lemma says that we can find local holomorphic sections of
H
with prescribed values and vanishing (1,0)-part of the Chern connection at a point.

Proof.

Let
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
be holomorphic local coordinates near
b
such that
t(b)=0
. We let
U
be the domain of definition of
t
. Let us locally, with respect to the
t
-coordinates, write
\delta:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}dt_{j}\wedge\delta_{j}
. The idea is to choose
u
to be of the form

(1.4.81)

\displaystyle u:=a+\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}\delta_{j}(a)

where
f_{j}
are holomorphic functions of
t
to be chosen such that
u^{b}=a
and
\delta u(b)=0
. Using (
1.4.30
) and computing we find,

(1.4.82)

\displaystyle\delta u

\displaystyle=\delta a+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\partial f_{j}\wedge\delta_{j}(a)+f_{j}\delta(\delta_{j}(a))\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\delta_{k}(a)dt_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\partial f_{j}}{\partial t_{k}}\delta_{j}(a)dt_{k}+f_{j}\delta(\delta_{j}(a))\right).

Hence, we may take
f_{j}:=-t_{j}
.
∎

Using Lemma
1.4.14
, we now get the following characterization for Griffiths (semi)negativity of trivial Hilbert bundles:

Theorem 1.4.15
.

Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
which admits the Chern connection
D
with curvature
\Theta
. Let
f
be a twice differentiable function on
[0,\infty)
which is strictly increasing and concave, and which satisfies that

(1.4.83)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x\geq 0

for all
x\geq 0
. Then
i\Theta\leq_{G}0
if and only if
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic for all (local) holomorphic sections
u
of
H
.

Proof.

Suppose that
i\Theta_{G}\leq 0
, and let
u
a holomorphic (local) section of
H
. Then
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic by Theorem
1.4.13
. Conversely, suppose that
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic for all holomorphic (local) sections
u
of
H
. By (the first part of) Theorem
1.4.13
, regardless of any assumption on
\Theta
, we have

(1.4.84)

\displaystyle i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)

\displaystyle\geq h(-i\Theta u,u)+i\left|\left|\delta u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left(F(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\right),

where
F
is some function depending on (derivatives) of
f
. Fix
b\in B
and
a\in H
. It suffices to show that
ih(\Theta a,a)(b)\leq 0
. We now apply Lemma
1.4.14
. By the lemma we can choose
u
holomorphic near
b
such that
u^{b}=a
and
\delta u(b)=0
. Evaluating at
b
, (
1.4.84
) therefore gives
i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{f(h)}(u)(b)=h(-i\Theta u,u)(b)=h(-i\Theta a,a)(b)
, which by our assumption is no less than 0. It follows that
h(i\Theta a,a)(b)\leq 0
, which completes the proof.
∎

In particular, Theorem
2.1.1
applies to
f(x):=\log(x+\epsilon)
for any
\epsilon>0
. This recovers in our possibly infinitely-ranked setting the well-known characterization of Griffiths (semi)negativity of finitely-ranked holomorphic vector bundles asserting that the vector bundle is (semi)negative in the sense of Griffiths if and only if every non-vanishing holomorphic section is logarithmically plurisubharmonic. Also, by applying the theorem to the dual bundle of
H
, under appropriate conditions (for example, when the adjoint operator and its inverse, with respect to the metric
h
, are smooth), we get that
H
is Griffiths (semi)positive (with respect to
h
) if and only if
f(\left|\left|u^{*}\right|\right|_{h^{-1}}^{2})
is plurisubharmonic for all non-vanishing holomorphic (local) sections of the dual bundle, and where
h^{-1}
of course is the dual metric of
h
.

We conclude the paper with two novel results of similar sorts to Theorem
1.4.12
. We obtain these from our discussions above. The first is the following result on plurisubharmonic variational properties of (certain image points of) total orthogonal projection maps:

Theorem 1.4.16
.

Let
\mathscrsfs{H}
be a holomorphic Hilbert bundle over
B
, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
which admits the Chern connection
D:=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Assume that
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}\leq\mathscrsfs{H}
, that
\overline{H_{0}}^{(\mathscrsfs{H},h)}=\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, and that
P=P(h)
, the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
(of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
in
\mathscrsfs{H}
) is smooth. Suppose also that for
b\in B
, given any
a_{b,0}\in(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
, we can find a holomorphic (local) section
v_{0}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
near
b\in B
, such that
(v_{0})^{b}=a_{b,0}
.
Let the Chern connection of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
be
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
, and let
\Theta
and
\Theta_{0}
denote respectively the Chern curvatures of
D
and
D_{0}
. Let
f
be a twice differentiable function on
[0,\infty)
which is strictly increasing and concave. Let
u
be a smooth (local) section of
\mathscrsfs{H}
and suppose that
\delta_{0}u=0
. Put
u_{0}:=Pu
. Then if

(1.4.85)

\displaystyle ih(\Theta_{0}u_{0},u_{0})-i\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left(1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}}\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

f(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic.
In particular, if
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{G}0
, then
f(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic wherever

(1.4.86)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}}\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\geq 0.

Proof.

From our discussion above, and (the proof of) Theorem
1.4.10
applied to
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, it suffices to show that we have
\delta_{0}u_{0}=0
. Our assumption is that
\delta_{0}u=0
. Let
u_{m}:=u-u_{0}
. Then
u_{m}\perp_{h}v_{0}
for all (local) sections
v_{0}
of
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
, and
u_{m}
is smooth. We claim that
\delta_{0}u_{m}=0
. Let
b
be in the domain of definition of
\delta_{0}u_{m}
. It suffices to show that
\delta_{0}u_{m}(b)=0
. Let
a_{b,0}\in(\mathscrsfs{H}_{0})_{b}
be arbitrary. It suffices to show that
h_{b}(\delta_{0}u_{m}(b),a_{b,0})=0
. By our assumption we can find a holomorphic
v_{0}
such that
v_{0}(b)=a_{b,0}
. By orthogonality we have
h(u_{m},v_{0})=0
. Applying
\partial
to this and using the definition of
D
, and orthogonality again, we get

(1.4.87)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=h(\delta_{0}u_{m},v_{0}).

Evaluating at
b
then gives our claim. Since
\delta_{0}u_{m}=0
, it is clear that
\delta_{0}u_{0}=0
from the assumption that
\delta_{0}u=0
. This completes the proof.
∎

The assumption that we can find holomorphic
v_{0}
’s such as in the statement of the theorem is rather strong. In the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}
is trivial, however, it is immediately satisfied. Consider the case that
\mathscrsfs{H}:=H
is trivial, and let
Q:=H/H_{0}
, where we write
H_{0}
for
\mathscrsfs{H}_{0}
. Let
m_{l}
denote the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h
, and
[u]
be a smooth (local) section of
Q
. Assume that
u
is a representative of
[u]
. Then
[u]=u-u_{0}
, where
u_{0}=Pu
, and we use the same notation as in Theorem
1.4.16
. The following interesting consequence of Theorem
1.4.16
, which is our second result, therefore gives in this setting variational properties of
[u]
:

Corollary 1.4.17
.

With the same assumptions, and the same notation, as in Theorem
1.4.16
, suppose furthermore that
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is pluri
super
harmonic, and let
u_{m}:=u-u_{0}
. Then
-\left|\left|u_{m}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic.

Proof.

By orthogonality we have
\left|\left|u_{m}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}=\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}-\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
, so
-\left|\left|u_{m}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}=-\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}+\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
. Hence the assertion follows from Theorem
1.4.16
taking
f(x):=x
.
∎

Let
T
be a linear operator on
H
and suppose that
v
lies in the image of
T
, and that
u
is a solution to
T(\cdot)=v
. Assume also that
\text{ker}(T):=H_{0}
is a closed subspace of
H
. By
the minimal solution
to the equation
T(\cdot)=v
(with respect to
h
) we shall mean the solution
\hat{u}
such that if
\tilde{u}
is any other solution, we have
\left|\left|\hat{u}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\leq\left|\left|\tilde{u}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
. With the notation above, it follows that
u_{m}
is the minimal solution to the equation
T(\cdot)=T(u)
. Thus, Corollary
1.4.17
can also be seen to give plurisubharmonicity properties of (the negative of of the squared norms of) minimal solutions.

Example 1.4.2
.

Let
H,H_{0}
, and
h
be as in Example
1.4.1
. Then we know that
\delta=\partial_{t}-\partial_{t}\psi
. Let
u=u(z)
be an element in
H
which is smooth and suppose also that for all
z\in\text{supp}(u)
,
\partial_{t}\psi(t,z)=0
. Then
\delta_{0}u=0
and Theorem
1.4.16
applies. That is, (for example, if
h(i\Theta_{0}u_{0},u_{0})\geq 0
,
\log\left|\left|u-u_{m}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}=\log\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic, where
u_{m}
is the minimal solution to the equation
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=\overline{\partial}_{z}(u)
(with respect to
h
). In this situation, we also have that
\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}_{t}\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}=\partial_{t}(h(u,\delta u))=0
so
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is also plurisuperharmonic. Therefore, Corollary
1.4.17
also applies and
-\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{m}
is plurisubharmonic.

Chapter 2
Paper 2

A HILBERT BUNDLES APPROACH TO COMPLEX BRUNN-MINKOWSKI THEORY, II

TAI TERJE HUU NGUYEN

2.1.
Introduction

The following is a sequel to
[
15
]
. We begin with a brief recollection of some of the main results proved in that paper, sticking to the following setting; we shall be in this setting also throughout the rest of this paper: Let
H
be a trivial Hilbert bundle over an
m
-dimensional complex manifold, and suppose that
h
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
which admits the Chern connection
D
. Following the notation in
[
15
]
, we shall generically write
D
as
D=\delta+\overline{\partial}
. Suppose also that
H_{0}
is a subbundle of
H
, closed in
H
with respect to
h
, and let
P=P(h)
denote the total orthogonal projection map associated with
h
(of
H_{0}
in
H
). One of the main results in
[
15
]
, Berndtsson’s regularity theorem, asserts that
P
is smooth if
h
is a smooth weighted hermitian metric induced by a hermitian metric of zero variation with respect to which
H_{0}
is also closed in
H
. Suppose that
P
is smooth. Then, as was shown in
[
15
]
, the Chern connection on
H_{0}
with respect to (the restriction of)
h
(as a hermitian metric on
H_{0}
) exists and is given by
D_{0}:=\delta_{0}+\overline{\partial}
with
\delta_{0}=P\delta
. Let
\Theta
and
\Theta_{0}
denote the Chern curvatures of
D
and
D_{0}
respectively. From
[
15
]
, we have the following three results on plurisubharmonic variations of smooth sections of
H
and
H_{0}
(with certain vanishing properties):

Theorem 2.1.1

(Theorem 4.15 in
[
15
]
)
.

With the above notation,
and set-up, let
f
be a twice differentiable function on
[0,\infty)
which is strictly increasing and concave, and which satisfies that

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}x\geq 0

for all
x\geq 0
. Then
i\Theta\leq_{G}0
(or
i\Theta_{0}\leq_{G}0
) if and only if
f(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic for all (local) holomorphic sections
u
of
H
(or
H_{0}
).

Theorem 2.1.2

(Theorem 4.16 in
[
15
]
)
.

With the above notation and set-up, let
f
be a twice differentiable function on
[0,\infty)
which is strictly increasing and concave. Suppose that
u
is a smooth (local) section of
H
satisfying
\delta_{0}u=0
, and put
u_{0}:=Pu
. Then if

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle ih(\Theta_{0}u_{0},u_{0})-i\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\left(1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}}\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

f(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic.
In particular, if
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{G}0
, then
f(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic wherever

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})}{f^{\prime}(\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}}\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\geq 0.

Theorem 2.1.3

(Corollary 4.17 in
[
15
]
)
.

With the same assumptions and notation as in Theorem
2.1.2
, suppose furthermore that
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is pluri
super
harmonic. Then
-\left|\left|u-u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}
is plurisubharmonic.

As explained in
[
15
]
, Theorem
2.1.1
in the case of
H_{0}
may be seen as a counterpart to the variation of Bergman kernels from
[
2
]
in the (more) general setting of (trivial) Hilbert bundles,
while Theorem
2.1.3
may be seen as a result on plurisubharmonic variation of minimal solutions to certain linear equations. Theorem
2.1.2
evidently gives variational properties of the total orthogonal projection map
P
, and is also naturally related to the the variation of Bergman kernels.

A common factor for the above results is that we presume some kind of positivity on the subbundle curvature
\Theta_{0}
. In the original statement of the variation of the Bergman kernels from
[
2
]
, or rather, the vector bundles analogue of it (see Theorem 1.2, or Theorem 1.1 in the case of trivial fibrations, in
[
3
]
), this presumption is in fact the conclusion of the result. More specifically, the result asserts that a certain vector bundle associated with Bergman type of spaces is (semi)positive in the sense of Nakano. In this paper we shall address the positivity of the subbundle curvature
\Theta_{0}
in a particular setting. The result that we give subsumes in the case of trivial fibrations the previous variation of Bergman kernels as well as its vector bundles analogue. In the vector bundles analogue, the sections of the relevant vector bundle are
L
-valued sections of the canonical bundle of some
n
-dimensional complex compact Kähler manifold, where
L
is a(n) (ample) holomorphic hermitian line bundle over the manifold. Let us denote the manifold by
X
, and its canonical bundle by
K_{X}
. As the complex dimension of
X
is
n
, these sections may alternatively be viewed as
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms where
q=0
, and our result also subsumes a generalization of this where we allow for
q
to be greater than 0, and
X
to belong to a larger class of Kähler manifolds (namely, the class of so-called
quasi-complete Kähler
manifolds); see Theorem
2.1.5
. In particular, all complete Kähler manifolds belong to this class, but the class is even closed under finite intersections with complements of analytic subsets. Using similar arguments, we also prove a result on plurisubharmonicity properties of holomorphic sections of the quotient bundle
Q:=H/H_{0}
, which is motivated by, and in the same spirit as, Theorem
2.1.3
(see Theorems
2.1.8
and
2.1.9
). Finally, by considering certain special metrics on
H
, we prove versions of the former two results where the metric on
L
may be possibly singular (see Theorems
2.1.6
and
2.1.10
). The key here is a Hörmander type of theorem with singular weights (Theorem
2.1.7
), which we obtain using regularization of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions (or currents) on compact manifolds, due to Demailly (see Theorem 16.3 in
[
25
]
).

The Hilbert bundles that we are going to consider will be ”function spaces”, or ”functional spaces”, equipped with hermitian metrics given by integrating against some measure. Our formulation will be somewhat abstract, but the reader may have settings like those mentioned above (in the variation of Bergman kernels and its vector bundles analogue), and in Example 3.2 from
[
15
]
, in mind for more concrete examples. See also Theorem
2.1.5
. Let
X
be a measure space with measure
d\mu_{X}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of maps on
X
such that
H
is some subfamily of
\mathcal{F}
. Let
Y
be the
m
-dimensional complex manifold over which we have our Hilbert bundles
H
and
H_{0}
, and suppose that
f=\{f_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
is a collection of maps on
H\times H
such that for each pair of elements
u
and
v
in
H
,
f_{y}(u,v)
is a complex-valued function on
X
that can be integrated over
X
against
d\mu_{X}
. Assume moreover that this defines a smooth hermitian metric
h=h^{f}
on
H
. That is, explicitly,
h:=\{h_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
is a smooth hermitian metric on
H
given by

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle h(u,v)(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}f_{y}(u,v)\;d\mu_{X}.

Let next
T
be a linear operator that can act on complex-valued functions, and assume that
T
extends to an operator, also denoted
T
, on (local) sections of
H
, and even on (local)
H
-valued differential forms on
Y
. We assume moreover that if
\chi
is a smooth function in the domain of definition of
T
, then
T(\chi)
is, or at least can be identified with, a (collection of) (local) vector-valued map (we will also say a vector field) on
X
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Here is an example illustrating what we mean by this: Consider the case that
T=\overline{\partial}^{\Omega}
, where
\overline{\partial}^{\Omega}
is the
\overline{\partial}
-operator on
\Omega
, and
\Omega
is some domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then if
\chi
is a function on
\Omega
, and we choose
z:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
as generic (global) coordinates on
\Omega
, we may identify
\overline{\partial}^{\Omega}\chi
with the vector-valued map (we will also say the vector field)

(2.1.5)

\displaystyle a\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial z_{1}}(a)\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial z_{n}}(a)\end{pmatrix}

on
\Omega
. We can also do something similar in the case that
\Omega
is a more general
n
-dimensional complex manifold, but then the identifications are in general only local. In this case, we therefore identify
T(\chi)
instead with a
collection
of (local) vector fields on
Y
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Finally, we suppose that
\text{ker}(T)\subseteq H
is closed in
H
with respect to
h
, and take
H_{0}:=\text{ker}(T)
. We choose generic (local) holomorphic coordinates
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
on
Y
, write with respect to these,

(2.1.6)

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}dt_{j}\wedge d\bar{t}_{k}\wedge\Theta_{j\bar{k}},

and assume that for each
x\in X
,
[\Theta_{j\bar{k}}(\cdot,x)]
defines a (collection of local) matrix field(s) on
Y
. With the above set-up specified, we may now proceed to state our main results. The following definition, whose motivation and relevance will be elaborated on in section
2.2.2
, is used:

Definition 2.1.1
.

With the above notation (and set-up), suppose that
v\in\text{Im}(T)
(the image of
T
), and let
u\in H
be a solution to the equation
T(\cdot)=v
. Let for each
x\in X
,
\mathbf{F}_{x}
and
A_{x}
be (a collection of local) matrix fields of sizes
n\times 1
and
n\times n
respectively, on
Y
. Suppose also that for all
y\in Y
and each
x\in X
,
A_{x}(y)
is semi positive definite hermitian. Then we shall say that (the triple)
\{u,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the
Hörmander
h
-estimate for the equation
T(\cdot)=v
if

(2.1.7)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\leq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{X}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}_{x}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}\;d\mu_{X}(x),

assuming
\left|\left|\mathbf{F}_{x}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}
, as
x
varies, can be integrated over
X
with respect to
d\mu_{X}
for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, where
I_{p}
denotes the identity matrix of size
p\times p
, and where

(2.1.8)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}_{x}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{F}_{x}^{*}(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{x},

with
\mathbf{F}_{x}^{*}
denoting the adjoint (that is, the complex conjugate of the transpose) of
\mathbf{F}_{x}
.

Our first main result concerns (Nakano) (semi)positivity of
\Theta_{0}
, and is the following theorem:

Theorem 2.1.4
.

With the above notation and set-up, suppose that
i\Theta\geq_{G}0
, let
M:=M(y,x)
be the matrix whose
(j,k)
th entry is
\Theta_{j\bar{k}}(y,x)
, and put
M_{x}:=M(\cdot,x)
. Suppose that, for each
x\in X
,
A_{x}
and
B_{x}
are collections of (local) matrix fields on
Y
of sizes
n\times n
and
n\times m
respectively, and that each
A_{x}
semi positive definite (at each point
y\in Y
). Let
B
be defined by
B(y,x):=B_{x}(y)
, and let
\mathbf{b}_{j}
denote the
j
th column of
B
. Assume that for all
\epsilon>0

(2.1.9)

\displaystyle(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(M_{x}+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}(B_{x})^{*}\geq 0,

for each
x\in X
, and that for all
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in H_{0}
there exists a solution
u\in H
such that
\{u,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the Hörmander
h
-estimate for the equation
T(\cdot)=v
, where

(2.1.10)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=T\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}u_{j}\right),

(2.1.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sqrt{f(u_{j},u_{j})}\mathbf{b}_{j},

and
\mathbf{F}_{x}:=\mathbf{F}(\cdot,x)
.
Then
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
.

The following setting is of particular interest: Consider a
quasi-complete Kähler manifold

(X,\omega)

=\{(X_{j},\omega_{j},\chi_{j})\}
of (complex) dimension
n
, and let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a hermitian holomorphic line bundle over
X
. The precise definition of
quasi-complete Kähler manifolds
will come later (see Definition
2.4.1
), and we here simply mention that in particular all complete Kähler manifolds, and also the Zariski open subsets in these, are quasi-complete Kähler manifolds. Denote by
h^{0}=h^{0,\phi}
the
L^{2}
-type of hermitian metric (inner product) on
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms induced by the metric on
L
and
\omega
, and by
\mathscrsfs{D}^{n,q}(L)
the space of smooth
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms with compact support in
X
. That is, explicitly,
h^{0}
is given by

(2.1.12)

\displaystyle h^{0}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}(u,v)_{\omega}e^{-\phi}\omega_{n},

where
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\omega}
denotes the
\omega
-metric on differential forms, and
\omega_{n}:=\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}
(the product here is of course the exterior product). As is common, we shall usually omit (writing)
\omega_{n}
in the integral. Let
H
be the completion of
\mathscrsfs{D}^{n,q}(L)
with respect to
h^{0}
. We view
H
as a trivial Hilbert bundle over
B
, and
h^{0}
as a hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
. Suppose that
\psi=\{\psi\}
is a (collection of local) function(s) on
Y\times X
such that multiplication by
e^{-\psi}
defines a smooth weight for
h^{0}
, and such that
e^{-(\phi+\psi)}
is a hermitian metric on
L
. Let
h
