denote the smooth weighted hermitian metric induced from
h^{0}
with the smooth weight given by multiplication by
e^{-\psi}
. We put

(2.1.13)

\displaystyle\theta:=\phi+\psi,

denote by
\theta^{y}
, for each
y\in Y
, the restriction of
\theta
to
\{y\}\times X
, and let
H_{0}:=H\cap\text{ker}(\overline{\partial}^{X})
, where
\overline{\partial}^{X}
is the
\overline{\partial}
-operator on
X
. In this setting, the following theorem generalizes in the case of trivial fibrations the positivity of direct images from
[
3
]
(to
(n,q)
-forms with
q\geq 0
, and more general Kähler manifolds):

Theorem 2.1.5
.

With the same above notation and set-up, suppose that
\theta
is plurisubharmonic. Then
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
.

Theorem
2.1.5
is a special case of Theorem
2.1.4
. Now, by considering (a) special
\psi
(and
Y
) in Theorem
2.1.5
, we may even allow
\phi
to be possibly singular. More precisely, we have the following variant of Theorem
2.1.5
where
\phi
may be singular:

Theorem 2.1.6
.

With the same notation and set-up as in Theorem
2.1.5
, suppose that
Y=\mathbb{C}
, that
\phi
is pseudoeffective (that is,
\phi
is plurisubharmonic; in particular,
\phi
may be singular), and that
G
is a non-positive function on
X
. Let
\lambda>0
, and let
\chi
be a smooth convex function on
\mathbb{R}
such that
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
, and
\chi(s)=0
for all
s\leq 0
. Consider that
\theta
is defined by

(2.1.14)

\displaystyle\theta^{y}:=\phi+\chi(G-\text{Re}(y)),

for each
y\in Y
, and suppose that

(2.1.15)

\displaystyle i\partial_{z}\overline{\partial}_{z}(\phi+\lambda G)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

where the
\partial
and
\overline{\partial}
operators here are those on
X
. Then
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
.

The main step in proving both Theorems
2.1.5
and
2.1.6
, is to verify that the conditions of Theorem
2.1.4
, with
T:=\overline{\partial}_{z}
, are satisfied in the setting in which these theorems take place. In particular, we need to verify that the Hörmander
h
-estimate for a certain
\overline{\partial}_{z}
-equation, as formulated in Definition
2.1.1
, is satisfied. The main novelty here is the possibly non-smoothness of
\phi
in Theorem
2.1.6
(and also Theorem
2.1.10
later), and for this, we shall use the following Hörmander type of theorem for special weights with possibly singular parts, on quasi-complete Kähler manifolds:

Theorem 2.1.7
.

With the same notation and set-up as in Theorem
2.1.6
, fix
y\in\mathbb{C}
, put
t:=\text{Re}(y)
, and let
u_{0}\in H_{0}
be given such that

(2.1.16)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{h_{y}}:=\left|\left|u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{t}=\int_{X}\left|u_{0}\right|_{\omega}^{2}e^{-\theta^{y}}<\infty,

where
\left|\cdot\right|^{2}_{\omega}=(\cdot,\cdot)_{\omega}
. Then there exists a solution
u\in H
to the equation

(2.1.17)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=-\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta_{t}\wedge u_{0}

satisfying

(2.1.18)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{t}=\int_{X}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}e^{-\theta^{y}}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\sqrt{\theta_{t\bar{t}}}u_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{t}=\int_{X}(\theta_{tt}u_{0},u_{0})_{\omega}e^{-\theta^{y}},

where we use the notation
\theta_{t}
for
\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial t}
, and
\theta_{tt}
for
\frac{\partial^{2}\theta}{\partial t^{2}}
.

Theorem
2.1.7
may be seen as a main result in itself. As mentioned above, we shall use regularization of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions (currents) on compact manifolds, due to Demailly (see Theorem 16.3 in
[
25
]
), to prove it. Note that due to the
\chi(G-t)
-term we cannot apply the regularization directly, so there
is
some work that needs to be done and shown. We will refrain from going into details here, and simply mention that the main idea is to apply the regularization to two (collections of) (quasi-)plurisubharmonic functions simultaneously and separately.

Our next main result is the following theorem on plurisubharmonicity properties of holomorphic sections of the quotient bundle
Q:=H/H_{0}
:

Theorem 2.1.8
.

With the above notation, suppose that
i\Theta\geq_{G}0
, and let
[u]
be a holomorphic (local) section of
Q
. Denote by
m_{l}
the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h
, and by
h^{Q}
the hermitian metric on
Q
induced by
h
. Let
A_{x},B_{x}
, and
M_{x}
, for each
x\in X
, be as in Theorem
2.1.4
. Assume that given any
\xi:=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and any
\epsilon>0
,

(2.1.19)

\displaystyle(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(M_{x}+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}(B_{x})^{*}\geq 0,

and that there exists
U\in H
such that
\{U,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the Hörmander
h
-estimate for the equation
T(\cdot)=v
, where

(2.1.20)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=T\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{j}\delta_{j}m_{l}[u]\right),

(2.1.21)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sqrt{f(m_{l}[u],m_{l}[u])}\left|\xi_{j}\right|\mathbf{b}_{j},

\mathbf{b}_{j}
denotes the
j
th column if
B
, defined also as in Theorem
2.1.4
(that is,
B(\cdot,x)=B_{x}
for each
x\in X
), and
\mathbf{F}_{x}:=F(\cdot,x)
.
Then for all strictly increasing and concave twice differentiable functions
\tilde{f}
on
[0,\infty)
,
-\tilde{f}(\left|\left|[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{Q}})
is plurisubharmonic.

In particular, if the conditions of the theorem are met, then
-\left|\left|[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{Q}}
and
-\log(\left|\left|[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{Q}})
are plurisubharmonic. A special case of Theorem
2.1.8
in the setting of Theorem
2.1.5
, is the following theorem; we use the same notational scheme for operators with subscripts as in
[
15
]
:

Theorem 2.1.9
.

In the same setting as Theorem
2.1.5
, and using the same notation as in there, suppose that
\theta
is plurisubharmonic. Let
v
be an
L
-valued
(n,q+1)
-form, and suppose that

(2.1.22)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta=0

on the support of
v
, choosing
t
as generic (local) holomorphic coordinates on
Y
. Choose also
z
as generic (local) holomorphic coordinates on
X
, and suppose that
u\in H
satisfies
\overline{\partial}_{z}u=v
. Let
u_{m}
denote the
h
-minimal solution to
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=v
. Then for all strictly increasing and concave twice differentiable functions
\tilde{f}
on
[0,\infty)
,
-\tilde{f}(\left|\left|u_{m}\right|\right|_{h}^{2})
is plurisubharmonic.

Theorem
2.1.9
gives plurisubharmonicity properties of minimal solutions (to certain inhomogeneous
\overline{\partial}_{z}
-equations), and may (also) be viewed as a main result in itself. It might be related to variations of so-called generalized Green energies (
[
38
]
), but the details are far from clear to us. We hope that this might be a future story for another time. Finally, similar to Theorem
2.1.6
, by considering (a) special
\psi
(and
Y
), we may allow for
\phi
in Theorem
2.1.9
to be possibly singular. More precisely, we have the following version of Theorem
2.1.9
, analogous to Theorem
2.1.6
, where
\phi
may be singular:

Theorem 2.1.10
.

With the same notation and assumptions as in Theorem
2.1.9
, suppose that the metric
\theta
is of the same form as in Theorem
2.1.6
. That is, for each
y\in\mathbb{C}
,
t:=\text{Re}(y)
,
\theta(y,\cdot)=\theta^{y}
is given by

(2.1.23)

\displaystyle\theta^{y}

\displaystyle=\phi+\chi(G-t),

where
\phi
is pseudoeffective (in particular, it may be singular),
G\leq 0
on
X
and satisfies that

(2.1.24)

\displaystyle i\partial_{z}\overline{\partial}_{z}(\phi+\lambda G)

\displaystyle\geq 0

for some
\lambda>0
, and
\chi
is a smooth convex function on
\mathbb{R}
which vanishes identically on
(-\infty,0]
and which satisfies that
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
. Then the conclusion of Theorem
2.1.9
holds. That is,
-\tilde{f}(\left|\left|u_{m}\right|\right|^{2}_{h})
is plurisubharmonic for all strictly increasing and concave twice differentiable functions
\tilde{f}
on
[0,\infty)
.

As in the case for Theorem
2.1.6
, the key to proving Theorem
2.1.10
(from Theorem
2.1.9
) is Theorem
2.1.7
.

This concludes our introduction. In the remainder of the paper we shall detail the proofs of the above theorems, and give the motivation behind, and relevance of, Definition
2.1.1
. We will also give the precise definition of
quasi-complete Kähler manifolds
, which we have yet to do. We end the paper by briefly commenting on the theme of the sequel of this paper (
[
17
]
).

2.2.
A linear algebraic result and motivations

In this section we discuss a few preliminary linear algebraic results that we shall have use for when proving our main results from the introduction. We also explain in this section the motivation behind, and the relevance of, the Hörmander estimate in Definition
2.1.1
. As one might expect, the latter is naturally related to Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates for solutions of the
\overline{\partial}
-equation (see, for example,
[
3
]
, section 3). Actually, the motivation for our upcoming linear algebraic discussion comes from there as well.

2.2.1.
Some linear algebra on norms induced by matrices

We shall view elements in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
as column vectors. Let
A
be a positive hermitian matrix of size
n\times n
. The matrix
A
defines an inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
given simply by

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})_{A}:=\mathbf{w}^{*}A\mathbf{v},

for
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
elements in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
\mathbf{w}^{*}
the adjoint of
\mathbf{w}
(that is, the complex conjugate of its transpose). The converse is of course also true. We will denote the norm induced from the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}
by
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{A}
. Note that we may do similar things also in the case that
A
is only semi positive definite, but in this case,
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{A}
is in general only a semi-norm. Let
\mathbf{F}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We may view
\mathbf{F}
also as a complex-antilinear functional on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
given simply by

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}:\mathbf{v}\mapsto\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle:=\mathbf{v}^{*}\mathbf{F}.

Let us denote the operator norm of
\mathbf{F}
, when viewed as such a functional, with respect to the norm
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{A}
, by
\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{*,A}
. By definition,

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{*,A}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left|\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left|\left|\mathbf{v}\right|\right|^{2}_{A}}.

We may also consider the norm of
\mathbf{F}
viewed as a vector in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with respect to
A^{-1}
, the inverse of
A
. It follows by the Riesz representation theorem (or simply by a direct computation) that these two norms are the same. That is,

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{*,A}=\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{A^{-1}}.

Observe that, for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\left|\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle\right|^{2}
is equal to the semi norm of
\mathbf{v}
with respect to a certain semi positive definite matrix
M
. Namely,
M=\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}
. Indeed, since
\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle
is a number,

(2.2.5)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle^{*}=(\mathbf{v}^{*}\mathbf{F})(\mathbf{v}^{*}\mathbf{F})^{*}=\mathbf{v}^{*}(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*})\mathbf{v}.

From this we immediately get the following lemma:

Lemma 2.2.1
.

With the above notation, suppose that there is some
\eta>0
such that

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}\leq\eta A.

Then

(2.2.7)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}\leq\eta.

Consider now the special case that
\mathbf{F}
is of the form

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{b}_{j}

where each
\mathbf{b}_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In this situation we may try and compare
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}
with the matrix
BB^{*}
, where
B
is the matrix of size
n\times m
whose
j
th column is
\mathbf{b}_{j}
. Let
I_{p}
denote the identity matrix of size
p\times p
. We may of course also write
BB^{*}=BI_{m}^{-1}B^{*}
. We generalize this somewhat by replacing
I_{m}
with a diagonal matrix
\Lambda
consisting of positive entries, and compare
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}
with
B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}
. Granted, this may seem a bit unmotivated at the moment, but as with everything else, we shall in due time explain our motivation(s) for this. Hopefully things become clearer as we go along, and in the end, all come together nicely. A pertinent example where the above is of natural interest, albeit still somewhat unmotivated, is the following: Consider a block matrix

(2.2.9)

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&B^{*}\\
B&A\end{pmatrix}.

Then
M\geq 0
if and only if

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle A-B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}\geq 0;

the interesting ”part” here is of course the ”term”
B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}
. This is a special case of the following more general
Schur complement theorem
from matrix analysis (see
[
59
]
):

Theorem 2.2.2
.

Let
M
be a hermitian matrix of the block form

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma&B^{*}\\
B&C\end{pmatrix},

where
\Sigma
is non-singular. Then
M\geq 0
if and only if
\Sigma\geq 0

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle C-B\Sigma^{-1}B^{*}\geq 0.

Our main linear algebraic result is the following theorem on the comparison of
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}
and
B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}
:

Theorem 2.2.3
.

With the above notation and set-up, let

(2.2.13)

\displaystyle\Lambda:=\text{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m})=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}\\
&\lambda_{2}\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\lambda_{m}\end{pmatrix}

and

(2.2.14)

\displaystyle\lambda:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}.

Then

(2.2.15)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}\leq\lambda B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma:=(\Lambda^{-1})^{1/2}
and
B:=[\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{b}_{m}]
. Then we have

(2.2.16)

\displaystyle B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}

\displaystyle=(B\Sigma)(B\Sigma)^{*},

(2.2.17)

\displaystyle B\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left[B\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}\cdots B\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\ 0\\
\vdots\\
\lambda_{m}^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\right],

and

(2.2.18)

\displaystyle B\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\vdots\\
\lambda_{j}^{-1/2}\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\mathbf{b}_{j}.

Hence,

(2.2.19)

\displaystyle B\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdots\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{m}}}\mathbf{b}_{m}\right],

so

(2.2.20)

\displaystyle(B\Sigma)^{*}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\mathbf{b}_{1}^{*}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{m}}}\mathbf{b}_{m}^{*}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\{\mathbf{0}\}
. We then get

(2.2.21)

\displaystyle(B\Sigma)^{*}\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}}\mathbf{b}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{v}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{m}}}\mathbf{b}_{m}^{*}\mathbf{v}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{*}(B\Lambda^{-1}B)\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{*}B\Sigma(B\Sigma)^{*}\mathbf{v}=((B\Sigma)^{*}\mathbf{v})^{*}((B\Sigma)^{*}\mathbf{v})

(2.2.22)

\displaystyle=\left|\left|(B\Sigma)^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|\right|^{2}_{e}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\left|\mathbf{b}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}+\cdots+\frac{1}{\lambda_{m}}\left|\mathbf{b}_{m}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}.

Since
\mathbf{F}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{b}_{j}
, we also directly get, where
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{e}
here denotes the usual euclidean norm,

(2.2.23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{*}(\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*})\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\mathbf{F}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|\right|^{2}_{e}=\left|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{b}_{j}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|\right|^{2}_{e}=\left|\mathbf{b}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{v}+\cdots+\mathbf{b}_{m}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}.

Multiplying (
2.2.22
) by
\lambda
and subtracting (
2.2.23
), gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{*}(\lambda B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}-\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*})\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{j}}\left|\mathbf{b}^{*}_{j}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}-\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{b}_{j}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}

(2.2.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda}\left|\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{j}}\mathbf{b}_{j}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}-\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{j}}\mathbf{b}_{j}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right)\right|^{2}.

Hence, as
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda}=1
, it follows by convexity of
\left|\cdot\right|^{2}
that the right-hand side in (
2.2.24
), thus the left-hand side, is no less than 0. That is:

(2.2.25)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{*}(\lambda B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}-\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*})\geq 0,

which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

We have the following important corollary to Theorem
2.2.3
:

Corollary 2.2.4
.

Let
A
and
M
be positive definite hermitian matrices of sizes
n\times n
and
m\times m
respectively, and let
B
be a matrix of size
n\times m
. Let
\mathbf{b}_{j}
denote its
j
th column, and define
\mathbf{F}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{b}_{j}
. Suppose that

(2.2.26)

\displaystyle A-BM^{-1}B^{*}\geq 0.

Then

(2.2.27)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq T_{r}(M),

where
T_{r}(M)
denotes the trace of
M
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma
be the orthogonal diagonalization of
M
. Then

(2.2.28)

\displaystyle BM^{-1}B^{*}

\displaystyle\geq B\Sigma^{-1}B^{*}.

The sum of entries of
\Sigma
is
T_{r}(M)
, so it follows by Theorem
2.2.3
and Lemma
2.2.1
that

(2.2.29)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq T_{r}(M).

∎

2.2.2.
Motivations

Having finished section
2.2.1
, we are now in a position to prove both Theorems
2.1.4
and
2.1.8
from the introduction. Before we do this, however, let us first discuss the motivation(s) behind everything up to this point.

Consider a domain
U\times\Omega
in
\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, where
U
is open and
\Omega
is bounded. Consider next a strictly plurisubharmonic function
\phi
on
U\times\Omega
which is smooth up to the boundary, and let for each
t\in U
,
\phi^{t}
denote the restriction of
\phi
to
\{t\}\times\Omega
. The set-up that we shall now consider is precisely that in the introduction in
[
15
]
. We denote the holomorphic functions on
\Omega
by
\mathscrsfs{O}(\Omega)
, and let

(2.2.30)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:=\left\{f\in L^{2}_{\text{loc}}(\Omega):\int_{\Omega}\left|f\right|^{2}<\infty\right\}\quad\quad\text{ and }

(2.2.31)

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle:=H\cap\mathscrsfs{O}(\Omega),

where we integrate with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Then
H
and
H_{0}
are trivial Hilbert bundles over
U
with respect to the usual
L^{2}
-metric, and
H_{0}\leq H
. We consider the
L^{2}
-metric as a hermitian metric
h^{0}
of zero variation on
H
and
H_{0}
. Multiplication by
e^{-\phi}
defines a smooth weight for
h^{0}
(on
H
(!)), and we denote the induced smooth weighted hermitian metric by
h
. That is,
h
is defined by

(2.2.32)

\displaystyle h_{t}(\cdot,\cdot):=h^{0}(e^{-\phi^{t}}\cdot,\cdot),

for each
t\in U
. We have seen in
[
15
]
that the Chern connection on
H
and
H_{0}
with respect to
h
exist. Let use denote these by
D
and
D_{0}
, respectively, and let us write for their respective Chern curvatures,
\Theta
and
\Theta_{0}
. We choose
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
and
z:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
as generic coordinates on
U
and
\Omega
, respectively. We may consider the
\partial
and
\overline{\partial}
operators on both
U
and
\Omega
, and will then as usual use subscripts with
t
and
z
to distinguish whether we consider the operators on
U
or
\Omega
. Thus,
\overline{\partial}_{t}
denotes the
\overline{\partial}
-operator on
U
,
\partial_{z}
denotes the
\partial
-operator on
\Omega
, and so on. In the situation at hand, we have (seen that we have)

(2.2.33)

\displaystyle\delta=\partial_{t}-\partial_{t}\phi\wedge

and

(2.2.34)

\displaystyle\Theta=\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}_{t}\phi\wedge.

Let us consider (Nakano) (semi)positivity of
\Theta_{0}
. For simplicity, and to fix the ideas, we will here stick to the case that
m=1
. Let
S:=D-D_{0}
be the second fundamental form (associated with
D
and
D_{0}
). Our starting point is the subbundle curvature formula

(2.2.35)

\displaystyle\Theta_{0}

\displaystyle=\Theta-S^{*}S,

from
[
15
]
. Since
\phi
is strictly plurisubharmonic, we have
i\Theta>_{G}0
, and following Berndtsson (see
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
), the idea is to use
L^{2}
-methods to control the
S^{*}S
term in (
2.2.35
). We first repeat Berndtsson’s argument. This will (hopefully) make the motivation behind our discussion in section
2.2.1
, and our interest in norms like
\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{A^{-1}}
in there, apparent. Let
u\in H_{0}
. Then
Su\perp H_{0}
(in fact this holds even if
u\in H
), which means that
Su
is, slightly imprecisely spoken, the
h
-minimal solution to the equation

(2.2.36)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=\overline{\partial}_{z}(Su)=\overline{\partial}_{z}(\delta u)=-\overline{\partial}_{z}\partial_{t}\phi\wedge u.

Under the additional assumption that
\Omega
is pseudoconvex, we may therefore (try and) use Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates for (solutions of) the
\overline{\partial}_{(z)}
-equation to control the
h
-norm of
Su
. The precise estimates that we shall need says the following (see, for example, section 2 in
[
2
]
, section 3 in
[
3
]
): Let

(2.2.37)

\displaystyle f=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}d\bar{z}_{j}

be a
\overline{\partial}_{z}
-closed (0,1)-form on
\Omega
. Then there exists a solution
v
to the equation
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=f
(in the sense of currents) such that

(2.2.38)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|v\right|^{2}e^{-\phi^{t}}\leq\int_{\Omega}\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}(\phi^{t})^{j\bar{k}}f_{j}\overline{f}_{k}e^{-\phi^{t}}

for each
t\in U
, where
(\phi^{t})^{j\bar{k}}
denotes the
(j,k)
th entry of the inverse of the complex Hessian matrix
[\phi^{t}]
of
\phi^{t}
. Consider
f:=-\overline{\partial}_{z}\partial_{t}\phi\wedge u
(”divided by”, or without,
dt
). The point, of course, is that since
Su
(again, ”divided by”, or without,
dt
) is the
minimal solution
to this equation, it too must satisfy the estimate in (
2.2.38
). Indeed, we have (still somewhat imprecisely),

(2.2.39)

\displaystyle\left|\left|Su\right|\right|^{2}_{h_{t}}\leq\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|v\right|^{2}e^{-\phi^{t}}

(and more precisely,
i\left|\left|Su\right|\right|^{2}_{h_{t}}\leq i\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|v\right|^{2}e^{-\phi^{t}}dt\wedge d\bar{t}
) if
v
is any solution to the equation. To show that

(2.2.40)

\displaystyle ih(\Theta u,u)-i\left|\left|Su\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\geq 0,

which is what we want to do according to (
2.2.35
), it therefore suffices to show that
h(\Theta u,u)(t)
(”divided by”, or without,
dt
) is no smaller than the right-hand side in (
2.2.38
), where
f
is chosen as above to be

(2.2.41)

\displaystyle f=-\left(\overline{\partial}_{z}\phi^{t}_{t}\right)\wedge u,

and where we here use the notation
\phi_{t}
for
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}
and
\phi_{t\bar{t}}
for
\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}
. Similarly, we write,
\phi_{\bar{z}_{j}}
for
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\bar{z}_{j}}
,
\phi_{\bar{z}_{j}t}
for
\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial\bar{z}_{j}t}
, and so on. Unravelling everything, we are then interested in showing that

(2.2.42)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\phi_{t\bar{t}}-\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}(\phi^{t})^{j\bar{k}}\phi_{\bar{z}_{j}t}\overline{\phi_{\bar{z}_{k}t}}\right)\left|u\right|^{2}e^{-\phi^{t}}\geq 0.

The key now, as observed by Berndtsson (see
[
2
]
), is that the expression inside the parenthesis in (
2.2.42
) is the determinant of the full complex Hessian of
\phi
divided by the determinant of the complex Hessian of
\phi^{t}
. Thus (
2.2.42
) is indeed true (under our assumptions of strict plurisubharmonicity, we even have strict inequality). The case
m>1
is analogous, but somewhat ”messier”; see
[
3
]
. The connection to our linear algebraic discussion in section
2.2.1
is the following: Let
[\phi^{t}]:=A
(that is
A
is the complex Hessian of
\phi
with respect to the
z
-variable), and identify
f
, as we may, with the vector field

(2.2.43)

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:=\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
\vdots\\
f_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Then the right-hand side in (
2.2.38
) is equal to

(2.2.44)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|\left|\mathbf{f}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}e^{-\phi^{t}}.

This is precisely where our motivation to study norms like
\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{A^{-1}}
, from earlier, comes from. We may next consider a similar example as above, but now in the global setting of complex manifolds; see also example in
[
15
]
. In this setting,
u
above is a holomorphic
L
-valued section of
K_{X}
, the canonical bundle of
X
, an
n
-dimensional complex manifold that plays the role of
\Omega
. The role of the weight function
e^{-\phi}
is played by a smooth hermitian metric on
L
, and we also replace
U
with an
m
-dimensional manifold
Y
. The hermitian metric
h^{0}
of zero variation is in this case defined by

(2.2.45)

\displaystyle h^{0}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})=\displaystyle\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}u^{\prime}\wedge\bar{v^{\prime}},

for
L
-valued sections
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
of
K_{X}
. In this setting, under appropriate assumptions on
X
and
\phi
, we still have analogous
L^{2}
-estimates for the
\overline{\partial}_{(z)}
-equation as above, and the (more or less) same arguments as before give (Nakano) (semi)positivity of the subbundle. Since the complex dimension of
X
is
n
,
u
above in this setting may also be interpreted as an
L
-valued
(n,q)
form with
q=0
, and as mentioned in the introduction, we can generalize this further by also allowing for
q\geq 1
. In the case that
q\neq 0
, we can of course not directly use the same definition as before for
h^{0}
, but there are natural analogues if we add to the picture some hermitian metric on
X
, that is, on its holomorphic tangent bundle. In these situations we still have Hörmander
L^{2}
-estimates for the
\overline{\partial}_{z}
-equation, but these estimates differ somewhat from those above and the case
q=0
. Fortunately, they do not differ too much, and with some more work, we are able to relate these to certain estimates where the right-hand side upper bound is of the form (an integral of)
\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}
like before, for suitable local vector and matrix fields
\mathbf{F}
and
A
. More specifically, we are able to show that a Hörmander
h
-estimate for a certain
\overline{\partial}_{z}
-equation, as defined in Definition
2.1.1
, holds. From here, we apply Theorem
2.2.3
. As we shall show, this provides an alternative way, which differs somewhat from the argument given earlier, of showing that the right-hand side (
2.2.35
) is positive. In particular, there will be no mentioning of any determinants, and the (slightly messy) equation (
2.2.42
) above will also come into play, at least not as directly as earlier. Moreover, there is virtually no difference between the case
m=1
and the case
m>1
, and the arguments that we give are in either of these cases more or less identical. Finally, Theorem
2.2.3
will also be very useful when we later consider
\phi
to be possibly singular; that is, when we prove Theorem
2.1.7
.

We now explain this (first part) a bit more, without requiring that
m=1
. For simplicity, we stick, however, still to the the case that
q=0
. We want to prove that
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
, and we want to use what we have discussed in section
2.2.1
. Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in H
. According to the subbundle curvature formula, that is (
2.2.35
), we need to show, point-wisely, that

(2.2.46)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})-\left|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{h},

where we write
S:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}dt_{j}
. Let

(2.2.47)

\displaystyle f:=\overline{\partial}_{z}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}\right).

A similar computation as before shows that

(2.2.48)

\displaystyle f=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\partial}_{z}(\partial_{t_{j}}\phi)\wedge u_{j},

where we as usual write
\phi_{t_{j}}
for
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t_{j}}
and so on. Let

(2.2.49)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{j}:=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{t_{j}\bar{z}_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{t_{j}\bar{z}_{n}}\end{pmatrix}

and

(2.2.50)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(-u_{j})\mathbf{b}_{j}.

We let as before
[\phi^{t}]:=A
. The
L^{2}
-estimates of Hörmander, (
2.2.42
), now says that if
v
is any solution to
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=f
, then for each
t\in U
,

(2.2.51)

\displaystyle\left|\left|v\right|\right|^{2}_{h}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}e^{-\phi^{t}}(t).

The solution
\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}
to the equation is the
h
-minimal solution, so we of course also have

(2.2.52)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}e^{-\phi^{t}}(t).

To prove that
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
, it therefore suffices to show that at each
t\in U
,

(2.2.53)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})(t)-\int_{\Omega}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}e^{-\phi^{t}}(t).

That is,

(2.2.54)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j}\bar{u}_{k}-\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}\right)e^{-\phi^{t}}(t)\geq 0.

Fix
z\in\Omega
. It suffices to show that the integrand in (
2.2.54
) is non-negative at
z
. Having fixed
z
, we may choose the coordinates
t
such that the matrix
\Theta_{j\bar{k}}(t,z)
is a diagonal matrix
\Lambda
. We denote the (positive) entries of
\Lambda
by
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}
. Then, want we want to show is that; for simplicity we drop evaluations at points:

(2.2.55)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}\left|u_{j}\right|^{2}-\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}\geq 0.

By replacing
\mathbf{b}_{j}
with
u_{j}\mathbf{b}_{j}
, and
\lambda_{j}
with
\left|u_{j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{j}
, this now follows by (the proof of) Theorem
2.2.3
and Corollary
2.2.4
if we can show that
A\geq B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}
, where
B
is the matrix whose
j
th column is
\mathbf{b}_{j}
. Consider the (full) complex Hessian matrix of
\phi
(at the point
(t,z)
). It is of the block form

(2.2.56)

\displaystyle[\phi]=\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&B^{*}\\
B&A\end{pmatrix},

and from Theorem
2.2.2
, since
[\phi]\geq 0
, we know that

(2.2.57)

\displaystyle A-B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}\geq 0.

Thus we get
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
as required.

We see from our discussion where the motivation to include
\Lambda^{-1}
in
B^{*}\Lambda^{-1}B
comes from. That is, why we earlier have insisted on comparing
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{*}
with
B\Lambda^{-1}B^{*}
instead of just
BB^{*}
. We also see that the argument just given is indeed a little bit different from Berndtsson’s original argument that we previously recapitulated. Of course, the key idea
is still owing to Berndtsson, namely to use
L^{2}
-methods to control the second fundamental form term in the subbundle curvature formula. Our contribution is instead Theorem
2.1.4
, which is more general than what we have discussed, and which also applies to the case that
q\geq 1
(see Theorems
2.1.5
and
2.1.6
). It remains to say a few words about the motivation behind Definition
2.1.1
. This will be more apparent from the proof of Theorem
2.1.4
that we give below, but the main idea is of course that it replaces the
L^{2}
-estimates of Hörmander above, (
2.2.38
). To access these estimates, we usually need to impose certain pseudoconvexity assumptions on the domain, and plurisubharmonicity properties on the weight function. Definition
2.1.1
gives us the estimates somehow for free without imposing such conditions, allowing for some general statements. In practice, we must of course verify that we do have these estimates, and in these cases, the former conditions on the domain and weight function again must be imposed. As mentioned above, and in the introduction, there are analogous situations, or settings, in which we also have Hörmander
L^{2}
-estimates, but the required conditions, and also the directly given estimates, may vary somewhat. For example, in the global setting of complex manifolds, there is usually some kind of compact or complete Kähler condition on the underlying manifold (
[
12
]
). The idea is that by using Definition
2.1.1
, Theorem
2.1.4
somehow places all these analogous examples under the same umbrella. Finally, taking the approach through Theorem
2.1.4
, has also led us to discover Theorem
2.1.8
(and its subsequent special cases; Theorems
2.1.9
and
2.1.10
).

This concludes our discussion on linear algebraic preliminaries, and the motivation behind, and relevance of, Definition
2.1.1
. In the remainder of the paper, starting with the next section, we give the proofs of the theorems in the introduction (which are not from
[
15
]
). We begin with the proofs of Theorem
2.1.4
and Theorem
2.1.8
, as we are going to use theorems in the proofs of the remaining theorems.

2.3.
Proofs of Theorems 1.4 and 1.8

In this section we prove Theorems
2.1.4
and
2.1.8
from the introduction. Since much of its proof has actually already been discussed in the previous section, we start with Theorem
2.1.4
. We will use similar notation as above, choosing generic (local) holomorphic coordinates
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
on
Y
, and writing with respect to these:

(2.3.1)

\displaystyle\Theta_{(0)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}dt_{j}\wedge d\bar{t}_{j}\Theta_{j\bar{k}}^{(0)},

and

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle\delta_{(0)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}dt_{j}\wedge\delta^{(0)}_{j}.

2.3.1.
Nakano (semi)positivity of subbundles: Proof of Theorem 1.4

Proof.

Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in H_{0}
. We want to show that at each
y\in Y
,

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta^{0}_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})

\displaystyle\geq 0.

Let
y\in Y
,
\epsilon>0
, and put
s_{j}:=\delta_{j}-\delta_{j}^{0}
. By the subbundle curvature, (
2.2.35
), it suffices to show that

(2.3.4)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})-\left|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right)(y)\geq 0.

Note that
T(s_{j}u_{j})=T(\delta_{j}u_{j})
, so linearity gives

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=T\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}\right).

It therefore follows by orthogonality properties of
s_{j}
that
\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}
is the
h
-minimal solution to the equation
T(\cdot)=v
. By assumption, there is
u\in H
such that
\{u,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the Hörmander
h
-estimate for the equation
T(\cdot)=v
. In particular, by (
h
)-minimality of
\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}
, we have

(2.3.6)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m}s_{j}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}.

Hence, it suffices to show that

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})-\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right)(y)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

By assumption,

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{X}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}_{x}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}\;d\mu_{X}(x).

Writing out the definition of
h
, it therefore suffices to show that

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle\int_{X}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}u_{j},u_{k})-\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}_{x}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}\right)\;d\mu_{X}(x)\left(y\right)

\displaystyle\geq 0

For this, it suffices to verify that the integrand is non-negative at each
x\in X
. Hence, fix
x\in X
. Having fixed
x
, we may choose
t
-coordinates near
y
such that
\Theta_{j\bar{k}}(y,x)
is diagonal. Let
\Lambda
denote the associated diagonal matrix, and let
\lambda_{j}
denote its
j
th entry (on the main diagonal). Thus, it suffices to show that

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(u_{j},u_{j})\lambda_{j}-\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}\right)(y,x)\geq 0.

In the above sum, we may assume that each
f(u_{j},u_{j})(y,x)\neq 0
, else we reduce the sum. By assumption, at
y
,

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(\Lambda+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}(B_{x})^{*}\geq 0.

Let
\tilde{B}
be the (collection of local) matrix (fields) whose
j
th column is
\sqrt{f(u_{j},u_{j})}\mathbf{b}_{j}
, and let
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\epsilon}
be the diagonal matrix whose
j
th entry is
f(u_{j},u_{j})(\lambda_{j}+\epsilon)
. Then,

\displaystyle B^{*}_{x}(\Lambda+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}(B_{x})^{*}

\displaystyle=\tilde{B}_{x}(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\epsilon})^{-1}(\tilde{B}_{x})^{*},

so it follows by (the proof of) Corollary
2.2.4
that

(2.3.12)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathbf{F}_{x}\right|\right|^{2}_{(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(u_{j},u_{j})(\lambda_{j}+\epsilon).

Substituted into the above, this shows that (
2.3.10
) holds, which completes the proof.
∎

2.3.2.
Variations of holomorphic sections of quotient bundles: Proof of Theorem 1.8

Next, we prove Theorem
2.1.8
. The proof is analogous to that of Theorem
2.1.4
, but we shall additionally need from
[
15
]
, the quotient bundle curvature formula (Theorem 4.7 in
[
15
]
) and a variational formula (equation (4.60) in
[
15
]
).

Proof of Theorem
2.1.8
.

Let
u
be a holomorphic (local) section of
H
. By the variational formula (4.60) in
[
15
]
, we have

(2.3.13)

\displaystyle\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{\tilde{f}(u)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left|h(\delta u,u)\right|^{2}+\tilde{f}^{\prime}(\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{h})\left(-h(i\Theta u,u)+\left|\left|\delta u\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right).

Now, replace in the above
H
with
Q
,
u
with
[u]
,
h
with
h^{Q}
, and
\Theta
with
\Theta^{Q}
, the latter being the Chern curvature of the Chern connection on
Q
with respect to
h^{Q}
. Multiplying the previous variational formula by
(-i)
and using the quotient bundle curvature formula (Theorem 4.7 in
[
15
]
) from
[
15
]
, since
\tilde{f}
is concave, we then find

(2.3.14)

\displaystyle-i\mathscrsfs{V}^{1,\bar{1}}_{\tilde{f}(h^{Q})}([u])

\displaystyle\geq i\tilde{f}^{\prime}(\left|\left|[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{Q}})\left(h(\Theta m_{l}[u],m_{l}[u])-\left|\left|\delta m_{l}[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right).

Since
\tilde{f}
is strictly increasing, it therefore suffices to prove that

(2.3.15)

\displaystyle i\left(h(\Theta m_{l}[u],m_{l}[u])-\left|\left|\delta m_{l}[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

That is, for all
\xi:=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})
, that at each
y\in Y
,

(2.3.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\Theta_{j\bar{k}}m_{l}[u],m_{l}[u])\xi_{j}\bar{\xi}_{k}-\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}h(\delta_{j}m_{l}[u],\delta_{k}m_{l}[u])\xi_{j}\bar{\xi}_{k}\geq 0.

For simplicity we put
u_{m}:=m_{l}[u]
. Note that since for all
v_{0}\in H_{0}
,
h(\delta_{j}u_{m},v_{0})=0
, it follows that
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}\xi_{j}u_{m}
is the
h
-minimal solution to the equation
T(\cdot)=v
. The rest of the proof now follows in exactly the same manner as the proof of Theorem
2.1.4
, taking
u_{j}:=\xi_{j}u_{m}
for each
j
.
∎

2.4.
Proof of Theorems 1.5, 1.6, 1.9, and 1.10

In this section we prove the remaining theorems from the introduction which we have yet to prove, starting with Theorem
2.1.5
.

2.4.1.
Quasi-complete Kähler manifolds and Hörmander’s
L^{2}
theorem

The first step in proving either of the remaining theorems is of course to give the definition of
quasi-complete Kähler
, which appears in the statement of the theorems, but which we have yet to give. The notion of
quasi-complete Kähler (manifolds)
generalizes that of complete Kähler (manifolds), and is due to Xu Wang and Bo Yong Chen (
[
38
]
). The definition is as follows:

Definition 2.4.1
.

A Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
is said to be
(a) quasi-complete (Kähler manifold)
if there exists a family of Kähler manifolds
\{(X_{j},\omega_{j})\}_{j}
and a family of smooth
[0,1]
-valued functions
\{\chi_{j}\}_{j}
on
X
such that the following properties are satisfied:

(i)

For each
j
,
X_{j}\subseteq X_{j+1}
,
X_{j}
is open in
X
, and
\bigcup_{j}X_{j}=X
.

(ii)

For each
j
,
\omega_{j}\geq\omega
on
X_{j}
, and for each compact subset
K
of
X
,

(2.4.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\sup_{K}\left|\omega_{j}-\omega\right|_{\omega}=0.

(iii)

Each
\chi_{j}
has compact support in
X_{j}
, satisfies

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\sup_{X_{j}}\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j}\right|_{\omega_{j}}=0,

and that for each compact subset
K
of
X
, there is
j=j(K)
such that
\chi_{j}|_{K}\equiv 1
for all
j\geq j(K)
.

In the case that
(X,\omega)
is quasi-complete Kähler, and
\{(X_{j},\omega_{j})\}
and
\{\chi_{j}\}
are as above, satisfying the needed criteria, we will refer to
\{(X_{j},\omega_{j},\chi_{j})\}_{j}
as an
approximation family for
(X,\omega)
. In this case, we will also frequently write
(X,\omega)=\{(X_{j},\omega_{j},\chi_{j})\}_{j}
.

All complete Kähler manifolds are quasi-complete Kähler manifolds. In fact, if
X
admits a complete Kähler metric, then
(X,\omega)
is quasi-complete Kähler. Indeed, suppose that
\hat{\omega}
is a complete Kähler metric on
X
. It suffices to observe that
\{(X,\omega_{j},\chi_{j})\}
is an approximation family for
(X,\omega)
where,
\omega_{j}:=\frac{1}{j^{k}}\hat{\omega}+\omega
,
\chi_{j}:=\chi(\frac{\rho}{j^{k+1}})
, for
k\geq 1
,
\chi
is a smooth
[0,1]
-valued function on
\mathbb{R}
satisfying that
\chi|_{(-\infty,1]}\equiv 1,\chi_{[a,\infty)}=0
for some
a>1
,
\left|\chi^{\prime}\right|\leq 1
, and
\rho
is a smooth exhaustion function for
X
such that
\left|d\rho\right|_{\hat{\omega}}\leq 1
(which exists since
\hat{\omega}
is complete). One may further show that if
Y
is a complete Kähler manifold,
\omega
is a Kähler metric on
Y
, and
S
is an analytic subset of
Y
, then
(Y\backslash S,\omega)
is quasi-complete Kähler (
[
38
]
). In fact, there is the following result:

Lemma 2.4.1
.

If
(X,\omega)
is quasi-complete Kähler, then
(X\backslash S,\omega)
is also quasi-complete Kähler for every analytic subset
S
in
X
.

We include a proof here, courtesy of Xu Wang, since we shall have use for it later (see the proof of Theorem
2.1.7
below):

Proof.

By Lemma 2.12 in
[
26
]
, we know that there exists
\psi<-1
on
X
such that

S=\{x\in X:\psi(x)=-\infty\},\ \ \psi\in C^{\infty}(X\setminus S)

and
i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi+\theta\geq 0
for some real smooth
(1,1)
-form
\theta
on
X
. Note that

i\partial\overline{\partial}(-\log-\psi)\geq-A+i\partial\log(-\psi)\wedge\overline{\partial}\log(-\psi),\ \ \ A:=\max\{0,\theta\}.

Choose a decreasing sequence of sufficiently small positive numbers
a_{k}
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}2^{k}a_{k}^{2}=0

and
5^{k}a_{k}^{2}A<\omega
on
X_{k}
(note that one may take an approximation family
(X_{j},\omega_{j},\chi_{j})
for
(X,\omega)
such that each
X_{j}
is relatively compact). Take
\kappa\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},[0,1])
such that
\kappa\equiv 1
on
(-\infty,1/2)
and
\kappa\equiv 0
on
(1,\infty)
. Put

w_{j,k}:=\omega_{j}+2^{-k}\omega+2^{k}a_{k}^{2}\cdot i\partial\overline{\partial}(-\log-\psi)

and

\chi_{j,k}:=\chi_{j}\cdot\kappa(a_{k}\log-\psi).

Then
(X_{j}\setminus S,\penalty\ w_{j,j},\penalty\ \chi_{j,j})
is an approximation family for
(X\setminus S,\penalty\ \omega)
.
∎

The next step is that we have a Hörmander
L^{2}
-type of theorem on quasi-complete Kähler manifolds for the
\overline{\partial}
-equation. We now discuss this; it is known that we have this on complete Kähler manifolds (see
[
12
]
). To set the stage, let
(X,\omega):=\{(X_{j},\omega_{j},\chi_{j})\}_{j}
be an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kähler manifold,
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a positive holomorphic line bundle over
X
, and let
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
denote the
L^{2}
-type of inner product on
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms on
X
induced by
\omega
and
e^{-\phi}
, given by

(2.4.3)

\displaystyle\left\langle u,v\right\rangle

\displaystyle:=\int_{X}(u,v)_{\omega}e^{-\phi}

for all
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms
u
and
v
on
X
. Now, in general, if
u
is an
L
-valued
(n,q)
-form, the hard Lefschetz theorem provides the existence of a unique
L
-valued (and
\omega
-primitive)
(n-q,0)
-form
\gamma_{u}
, such that

(2.4.4)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\gamma_{u}\wedge\omega_{q}.

Let
\Theta^{L}
denote the Chern curvature of the Chern connection on
L
with respect to the metric
e^{-\phi}
. We define what we shall call the ”
B
-operator”, denoted by
B
, on (
L
-valued)
(n,q)
-forms by:

(2.4.5)

\displaystyle Bu:=i\Theta^{L}(\gamma_{u}\wedge\omega_{q-1}).

One may then check that if we write (locally) with respect to a local orthonormal frame
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
for
\Lambda^{1,0}(T^{*}X)
, the holomorphic cotangent bundle of
X
,
i\Theta^{L}
as

(2.4.6)

\displaystyle i\Theta^{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}e_{j}\wedge\bar{e}_{j},

then

(2.4.7)

\displaystyle B(e\wedge\bar{e}_{J})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j\in J}\lambda_{j}\right)e\wedge\bar{e}_{J},

where
e:=e_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{n}
and
\bar{e}_{J}:=\bar{e}_{j_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{e}_{j_{q}}
for
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{q})
a strictly increasing multiindex of length
q
. In particular it follows that
B^{-1}
, the inverse operator of
B
, exists when
(L,e^{-\phi})
is positive (and that it is given by, using the same notation as above,
e\wedge\bar{e}_{J}\mapsto\left(\sum_{j\in J}\lambda_{j}\right)^{-1}e\wedge\bar{e}_{J}
). Let us choose
z:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
as generic (local) holomorphic coordinates on
X
, put
\overline{\partial}_{z}:=\overline{\partial}
, and denote the formal adjoint of
\overline{\partial}
with respect to the metric
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
by
\overline{\partial}^{*}
. Let us denote by
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|
the norm induced by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
. Then we have the following Hörmander
L^{2}
-type of theorem for the
\overline{\partial}
-equation, on quasi-complete Kähler manifolds under positive holomorphic line bundles:

Theorem 2.4.2
.

With the above notation and set-up, let
v
be a
\overline{\partial}
-closed
L
-valued
(n,q+1)
-form such that
\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle<\infty
. Then there exists an
L
-valued
(n,q)
-form
u
such that
\overline{\partial}u=v
in the sense of currents, and

(2.4.8)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle.

As mentioned earlier, the theorem is known in the case that
(X,\omega)
is complete Kähler. The quasi-complete Kähler case for
q=0
was taught to us by Xu Wang (
[
38
]
). The following proof is therefore essentially due to him:

Proof of Theorem
2.4.2
.

Let
\square_{j}
denote the
\overline{\partial}
-Laplace operator on
X_{j}
with respect to the metric
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{j}
, where the latter is defined like
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
, except that we replace
X
with
X_{j}
and
\omega
with
\omega_{j}
. Let similarly
B_{j}
denote the ”
B
-operator” with respect to
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{j}
. We first solve the (
\overline{\partial}
-Laplace) equation
\square_{j}(\cdot)=v
in the sense of currents and obtain a solution
u_{j}
satisfying

(2.4.9)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\overline{\partial}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{j}+\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}u_{j}\right|\right|_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle B^{-1}_{j}v,v\right\rangle,

where we denote by
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{j}
the norm induced by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{j}
, and
B^{-1}_{j}=(B_{j})^{-1}
. Since
\overline{\partial}v=0
, we get that

(2.4.10)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\square_{j}u_{j}

\displaystyle=\square_{j}\overline{\partial}u_{j}=\overline{\partial}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}u_{j}=0.

Hence, by elliptic regularity, we may assume that
\overline{\partial}u_{j}
is smooth. Using smoothness and compact support we can then write

(2.4.11)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{j}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\overline{\partial}(\chi_{j}^{2}\overline{\partial}u_{j}),\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}u_{j}\right\rangle_{j}.

We use the Leibniz rule to compute
\overline{\partial}(\chi^{2}\overline{\partial}u_{j})
. The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (and (
2.4.9
)) then give(s)

(2.4.12)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{j}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{j}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{X_{j}}\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j}\right|_{\omega_{j}}\sqrt{\left\langle B_{j}^{-1}v,v\right\rangle}.

Now fix
X_{0}\Subset X
. We will denote restricted norms and inner products to
X_{0}
using subscripts. Letting
j\to\infty
, we find that

(2.4.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,X_{0}}=0.

Thus on
X_{0}
,
\overline{\partial}(\overline{\partial}^{*}u_{j})\to v
as a current as
j\to\infty
. Note that
\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}u_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,X_{0}}\leq\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle_{X_{0}}
, so we get that
\{\overline{\partial}^{*}u_{j}|_{X_{0}}\}_{j}
is a uniformly bounded sequence with respect to the
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{X_{0}}
-norm. Hence it admits a convergent subsequence. Let us denote by
u_{X_{0}}
the weak limit of this convergent subsequence. Then
\overline{\partial}u_{X_{0}}=v
on
X_{0}
, and
\left|\left|u_{X_{0}}\right|\right|_{X_{0}}^{2}\leq\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle_{X_{0}}
. We now use a diagonal argument. Let
\{U_{k}\}_{k}
be an increasing sequence of relatively compact subsets of
X
. To each
U_{k}
we have by the previous argument a
u_{k}
such that
\overline{\partial}u_{k}=v
in the sense of currents on
U_{k}
and
\left|\left|u_{k}\right|\right|^{2}_{U_{k}}\leq\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle_{U_{k}}
. Consider first the sequence
\{u^{(1)}_{j}\}_{j}
given by restricting, for large
j
, each
u_{j}
to
U_{1}
. Then let
\{u^{(2)}_{j}\}_{j}
be the sequence given by restricting each
u^{(1)}_{j}
, again for large
j
, to
U_{2}
, and so on. Finally, let
u
be the weak limit of the diagonal sequence
\{u^{(j)}_{j}\}_{j}
. Then
u
satisfies that
\overline{\partial}u=v
in the sense of currents on
X
and
\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}\leq\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle
.
∎

We shall need a variant of Theorem
2.4.2
where
(L,e^{-\phi})
is semi positive. In this case,
B^{-1}
may not make sense, and the condition
\left\langle B^{-1}v,v\right\rangle<\infty
needs to be replaced with the condition

(2.4.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left\langle(B+\epsilon(q+1))^{-1}v,v\right\rangle<\infty.

Since the proof is analogous to that of Theorem
2.4.2
, we shall content ourselves here with simply stating the result:

Theorem 2.4.3
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kähler manifold, and let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be an ample holomorphic line bundle over
X
. Let
\Theta
denote the Chern curvature of
L
, and let
B
be defined by

(2.4.15)

\displaystyle B(\omega_{q}\wedge\gamma_{u}):=i\Theta\gamma_{u}\wedge\omega_{q-1}

for all
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms
u=\gamma_{u}\wedge\omega_{q}
. Let
v
be a
\overline{\partial}
-closed
L
-valued
(n,q+1)
-form such that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left\langle(B+(q+1)\epsilon)^{-1}v,v\right\rangle<\infty
, where
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
is the
L^{2}
-type of inner product on
L
-valued differential forms induced by
e^{-\phi}
and
\omega
. Then there exists an
L
-valued
(n,q)
-form
u
such that
\overline{\partial}u=v
in the sense of currents, and

(2.4.16)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}<\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left\langle(B+\epsilon(q+1))^{-1}v,v\right\rangle,

where
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|
denotes the norm induced by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
.

2.4.2.
Nakano (semi)positivity for quasi-complete Kähler manifolds and (semi)positive line bundles: Proof of Theorem 1.5

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
2.1.5
, for which we shall use Theorem
2.4.3
:

Proof of Theorem
2.1.5
.

Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in H_{0}
be given, and let
\epsilon>0
. The idea is to use Theorem
2.1.4
with
T:=\overline{\partial}_{z}
, taking as usual
z:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
as generic (local) holomorphic coordinates on
X
. We take, also as usual,
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
as generic (local) holomorphic coordinates on
Y
. For each
x\in X
, let
A_{x}
denote the matrix field associated to
\partial_{z}\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta(\cdot,x)
. We write
\theta_{t_{j}}
for
\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial t_{j}}
,
\partial_{t_{j}}
for
\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}
,
\theta_{t_{j}\bar{t}_{k}}
for
\frac{\partial^{2}\theta}{\partial t_{j}\bar{t}_{k}}
,
\theta_{t_{j}\bar{z}_{k}}
for
\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}_{k}}\theta_{t_{j}}
, and so on. We have seen that in our situation,

(2.4.17)

\displaystyle\delta_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t_{j}}-\theta_{t_{j}},

so that

(2.4.18)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{z}(\delta_{j}u_{j})

\displaystyle=-\overline{\partial}_{z}(\theta_{t_{j}})\wedge u_{j}.

We put
v:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\partial}_{z}(\delta_{j}u_{j})
, and let, as in Theorem
2.1.4
,
M_{x}
be the matrix field associated with
\Theta(\cdot,x)
. Thus, letting
M
be defined by
M(\cdot,x):=M_{x}
, the
(j,k)
th entry of
M
is
\Theta_{j\bar{k}}
. We have seen that we have (and this also follows from the expression of
\delta
above)

(2.4.19)

\displaystyle\Theta_{j\bar{k}}=\theta_{t_{j}\bar{t}_{k}}.

Let

(2.4.20)

\displaystyle b_{s}:=\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta_{t_{s}},

and let
\mathbf{b}_{s}
be the (collection of) (local) vector fields identified with
b_{s}
. It follows by the plurisubharmonicity of
\theta
that, at each
x\in X
,

(2.4.21)

\displaystyle(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(M_{x}+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}(B_{x})^{*}\geq 0.

Let
B
be the ”
B
-operator” with respect to the metric
h
, and let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be a (local) orthonormal frame for
\Lambda^{1,0}(T^{*}X)
with respect to which we write

(2.4.22)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}e_{j}\wedge\bar{e}_{j}

(2.4.23)

\displaystyle u_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|J_{j}|=q}^{{}^{\prime}}(-1)\alpha^{J_{j}}e\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{j}}

(2.4.24)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta_{t_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\beta^{l}_{j}\bar{e}_{l}.

Then we get

(2.4.25)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{|J_{j}|=q}^{{}^{\prime}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha^{J_{j}}\beta^{l}_{j}e\wedge\bar{e}_{l}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{j}},

and we may assume that
l\not\in J_{j}
in the above sum. Computing, we now find

\displaystyle((B+(q+1)\epsilon)^{-1}v,v)_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\left((B+(q+1)\epsilon)^{-1}\left(\sum_{j,J_{j},l}\alpha^{J_{j}}\beta^{l}_{j}e\wedge\bar{e}_{l}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{j}}\right),\sum_{k,J_{k},p}\alpha^{J_{k}}\beta^{p}_{k}e\wedge\bar{e}_{p}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{k}}\right)_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j,J_{j},l}\alpha^{J_{j}}\beta^{l}_{j}\left(\sum_{r\in\{l\}\cup J_{j}}(\eta_{r}+\epsilon)\right)^{-1}e\wedge\bar{e}_{l}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{j}},\sum_{k,J_{k},p}\alpha^{J_{k}}\beta^{p}_{k}e\wedge\bar{e}_{p}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{k}}\right)_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j,J_{j},l}\frac{1}{\eta_{l}+\epsilon}\alpha^{J_{j}}\beta^{l}_{j}e\wedge\bar{e}_{l}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{j}},\sum_{k,J_{k},p}\alpha^{J_{k}}\beta^{p}_{k}e\wedge\bar{e}_{p}\wedge\bar{e}_{J_{k}}\right)_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k}(e,e)_{\omega}\left(\sum_{J_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}}\alpha^{J_{j}}\bar{e}_{J_{j}},\sum_{J_{k}}^{{}^{\prime}}\alpha^{J_{k}}\bar{e}_{J_{k}}\right)_{\omega}\left(\sum_{l}\frac{1}{\eta_{l}+\epsilon}\beta^{l}_{j}\bar{e}_{l},\sum_{p}\beta^{p}_{k}\bar{e}_{p}\right)_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,k}\left|\sum_{J_{j}}^{{}^{\prime}}\alpha^{J_{j}}\bar{e}_{J_{j}}\right|_{\omega}\left|\sum_{J_{k}}^{{}^{\prime}}\alpha^{J_{k}}\bar{e}_{J_{k}}\right|_{\omega}\left(\sum_{l}\frac{1}{\eta_{l}+\epsilon}\beta^{l}_{j}\bar{e}_{l},b_{k}\right)_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k}\left(\sum_{l}\frac{1}{\eta_{l}+\epsilon}\beta^{l}_{j}\bar{e}^{l}\left|u_{j}\right|_{\omega},b_{k}\left|u_{k}\right|_{\omega}\right)_{\omega}

(2.4.26)

\displaystyle=\left((B+\epsilon)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}\left|u_{j}\right|_{\omega},\sum_{k=1}^{m}b_{k}\left|u_{k}\right|_{\omega}\right)_{\omega}.

Let
\mathbf{F}:=\sum_{s=1}^{m}\left|u_{s}\right|_{\omega}\mathbf{b}_{s}
. Then the (last) right-hand side in (
2.4.26
) is equal to
\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{(A+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}^{2}
. That is, we have (locally)

(2.4.27)

\displaystyle((B+(q+1)\epsilon)^{-1}v,v)_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|_{(A+\epsilon I_{n})^{-1}}^{2}.

Integrating this and applying Corollary
2.2.4
, we find that
\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left\langle(B+(q+1)\epsilon)^{-1}v,v\right\rangle<\infty
. Hence, we may apply Theorem
2.4.3
and find a
U\in H
such that
\overline{\partial}_{z}(U)=v
and

(2.4.28)

\displaystyle\left|\left|U\right|\right|^{2}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left\langle(B+(q+1)\epsilon)^{-1}v,v\right\rangle.

Incidentally, (
2.4.27
) also shows that
\{U,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the Hörmander
h
-estimate for the equation
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=v
, so the assertion follows by Theorem
2.1.4
.
∎

2.4.3.
A Hörmander
L^{2}
-type of theorem with singular weights: Proof of Theorem 1.7

Having proved Theorem
2.1.5
, we proceed to prove the variant of it where
\phi
may possibly be singular. That is, Theorem
2.1.6
. The first step in proving Theorem
2.1.6
, is to prove a singular variant of Theorem
2.4.2
in this setting. That is, Theorem
2.1.7
. We now do this. For parts of the proof it might be helpful to refer back to the proof of Theorem
2.4.2
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1.7
.

Since
\theta^{y}
is independent of
t
when
t\geq 0
, we may assume that
t<0
. Fix
X_{j}\Subset X
and
t<0
; we are of course later going to let
j\to\infty
. Let
\phi_{0}
be a fixed smooth metric on
L
. The idea is to apply the regularization of quasi-plurisubharmonic currents (see Theorem 16.3 in
[
25
]
) to
\theta-\phi_{0}
(or rather
\theta^{t}-\phi_{0})
), but we cannot do this directly. Instead, note that if we define
\psi:=\phi+\lambda G
, then
\phi
and
\psi
are plurisubharmonic by assumption. We shall apply the regularization to
\phi-\phi_{0}
and
\psi-\phi_{0}
simultaneously and separately. The idea is that since

(2.4.29)

\displaystyle G=\frac{\phi-\psi}{\lambda}=\frac{\phi\pm\phi_{0}-(\psi\pm\phi_{0})}{\lambda},

approximations of
\phi-\phi_{0}
and
\psi-\phi_{0}
, give approximations for
G
, and hence
\theta-\phi_{0}
. Let
\epsilon_{j}>0
. Applying the regularization to
\phi-\phi_{0}
and
\psi-\phi_{0}
, we find decreasing sequences
\{\phi^{j}_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
and
\{\psi^{j}_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
converging to
\phi-\phi_{0}
and
\psi-\phi_{0}
respectively, and such that

(2.4.30)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}(\phi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0}),i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi_{\nu}^{j}+\phi_{0})\geq-\epsilon_{j}\omega.

The
\partial
and
\overline{\partial}
operators are here with respect to
X
, the corresponding operators for
Y(=\mathbb{R})
will have no play in the proof. We put

(2.4.31)

\displaystyle G_{\nu}^{j}:=\frac{\phi^{j}_{\nu}-\psi^{j}_{\nu}}{\lambda}

and

(2.4.32)

\displaystyle\theta^{j}_{\nu}:=\phi^{j}_{\nu}+\chi\left(G_{\nu}^{j}-t\right)

(as with
\theta
,
\theta^{j}_{\nu}
really depends on
t
, but we will neglect to write this to avoid too messy a notation). A direct computation now gives

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}(\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})

\displaystyle=i\partial\overline{\partial}(\phi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})\left(1-\frac{\chi^{\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)}{\lambda}\right)+\frac{\chi^{\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)}{\lambda}i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})

(2.4.33)

\displaystyle+\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)i\partial G_{\nu}^{j}\wedge\overline{\partial}G_{\nu}^{j}.

Since
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
, (
2.4.30
), gives

(2.4.34)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}(\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})

\displaystyle\geq-2\epsilon_{j}\omega+\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)i\partial G_{\nu}^{j}\wedge\overline{\partial}G_{\nu}^{j}.

Our approximations
\phi^{j}_{\nu}
(and
\psi^{j}_{\nu}
) are smooth outside analytic subsets
Z^{j}_{\nu}:=\{\phi^{j}_{\nu}=-\infty\}\subseteq X_{j}
. We are going to solve a perturbed
\overline{\partial}
-Laplace equation outside
Z^{j}_{\nu}
, and for this we will mimic the proof of Lemma
2.4.1
. By choosing
\phi_{0}
sufficiently large, we may assume that

(2.4.35)

\displaystyle\phi^{j}_{\nu}<-1+\lambda t,

which gives
\theta^{j}_{\nu}<-1
. With this, let
A:=\max\{i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi_{0}+\omega\}
, and let
\{a_{k}\}
be a decreasing sequence of sufficiently small positive real numbers such that

(2.4.36)

\displaystyle 2^{k}a_{k}\to 0

as
k\to\infty
, and

(2.4.37)

\displaystyle 5^{k}a_{k}^{2}A<\omega

for large
k
. Let
\kappa
be a smooth
[0,1]
-valued function on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\kappa|_{(-\infty,1/2)}\equiv 1
and
\kappa_{(1,\infty)}\equiv 0
. Define

(2.4.38)

\displaystyle\omega_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle:=\omega_{j}+2^{-k}\omega+2^{k}a_{k}^{2}\partial\overline{\partial}(-\ln(-\theta^{j}_{\nu}))

(2.4.39)

\displaystyle\chi_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{j}\kappa\left(a_{k}\ln(-\theta^{j}_{\nu})\right).

We now mimic the first part of the proof of Theorem
2.4.2
(actually the proof of Theorem
2.4.3
) for the family
(X_{j}\backslash Z^{j}_{\nu},\omega_{j,\nu,k},\chi_{j,\nu,k})
, for fixed
j
. Let
\square_{j,\nu,k}
be the
\overline{\partial}
-Laplace operator associated with
X^{j}\backslash Z^{j}_{\nu}
and
\omega_{j,\nu,k}
. We find a solution
v_{j,\nu,k}
such that

(2.4.40)

\displaystyle(\square_{j,\nu,k}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1))v_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle=\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)\overline{\partial}G_{\nu}^{j}\wedge u:=c_{j,\nu,k}

and

(2.4.41)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}^{2}+\left|\left|\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1)\left|\left|v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle(B^{\epsilon}_{j,\nu,k})^{-1}c_{j,\nu,k},c_{j,\nu,k}\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k},

where
B^{\epsilon_{j}}_{j,\nu,k}
is the ”
B
-operator” associated with
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k}
and the positive ”curvature form”
i\partial\overline{\partial}(\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})+2\epsilon_{j}\omega_{j,\nu,k}
, and where
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k}
is defined similar as
h
, except that we replace
\omega
with
\omega_{j,\nu,k}
,
X
with
X_{j}\backslash Z^{j}_{\nu}
, and
\theta
with
\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0}
, Let us denote by
B_{j,\nu,k}
the ”
B
-operator” associated with
i\partial\overline{\partial}(\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})
. Then
B_{j,\nu,k}^{\epsilon_{j}}=B_{j,\nu,k}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1)
. Let
b:=\overline{\partial}G_{\nu}^{j}
and let
\mathbf{b}
be the vector (field) identified with
b
. Let
\mathbf{F}:=\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)\left|u\right|_{\omega_{j,\nu,k}}\mathbf{b}
, and let
A
be the matrix (field) identified with
\partial\overline{\partial}(\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})+2\epsilon_{j}\omega_{j,\nu,k}
. By (
2.4.34
) we have

(2.4.42)

\displaystyle A\geq\mathbf{b}\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)\mathbf{b}^{*}.

Hence, as in the proof of Theorem
2.1.5
, it follows by Corollary
2.2.4
(in our case now,
m=1
, which simplifies things)

(2.4.43)

\displaystyle((B^{\epsilon_{j}}_{j,\nu,k})^{-1}c_{j,\nu,k},c_{j,\nu,k})_{\omega_{j,\nu,k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\mathbf{F}\right|\right|^{2}_{A^{-1}}\leq\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega_{j,\nu,k}}.

Integrating we therefore get that
v_{j,\nu,k}
satisfies

(2.4.44)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}^{2}+\left|\left|\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1)\left|\left|v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)u,u\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k}.

Now,
\overline{\partial}c_{j,\nu,k}=0
, so applying
\overline{\partial}
to (
2.4.40
) we get

(2.4.45)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\square_{j,\nu,k}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1))\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}.

Hence we may assume that
\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}
is smooth on
X^{j}\backslash Z^{j}_{\nu}
. Thus

(2.4.46)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left\langle\chi_{j,\nu,k}^{2}\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k},(\overline{\partial}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1)\overline{\partial})v_{j,\nu,k}\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k},

which gives

(2.4.47)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{j,\nu,k}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k},2\chi_{j,\nu,k}\wedge\overline{\partial}\chi_{j,\nu,k}\wedge\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k}-2\epsilon_{j}(q+1)\left|\left|\chi_{j,\nu,k}\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}.

Hence,

(2.4.48)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{j,\nu,k}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}\left|\left|\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}.

Put
C_{j,\nu,k}:=\left\langle\chi^{\prime\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j}-t)u,u\right\rangle_{j,\nu,k}
. Then
\left|\left|\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}\leq\sqrt{C_{j,\nu,k}}
. Computing we have

(2.4.49)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\chi_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}\chi_{j}\kappa(a_{k}\ln(-\theta^{j}_{\nu}))+\chi_{j}\kappa^{\prime}(a_{k}\ln(-\theta^{j}_{\nu}))a_{k}\overline{\partial}\ln(-\theta^{j}_{\nu}).

Hence there exist a constant
M^{j}_{\nu}
such that

(2.4.50)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j,\nu,k}\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j}\right|\right|_{j,\nu}+a_{k}M^{j}_{\nu},

where
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{j,\nu}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{j,\nu,k}
. Put
C_{j,\nu}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}C_{j,\nu,k}
. We now let
k\to\infty
, for fixed
j
and
\nu
. By (
2.4.44
),
\{v_{j,\nu,k}\}_{k}
is uniformly bounded with respect to the
L^{2}
-metric on
X^{j}\backslash Z^{j}_{\nu}
determined by
\omega_{j}
and
\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0}
. Let
v_{j,\nu}
denote the weak limit of a converging subsequence. Then

(2.4.51)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{j}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu}\right|\right|_{j,\nu}\leq\left|\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j}\right|\right|_{j,\nu}\sqrt{C_{j,\nu}}

We have that
v_{j,\nu}
satisfies a perturbed
\overline{\partial}
-Laplace equation outside the analytic subset
Z^{j}_{\nu}
. By standard results (see
[
26
]
), it satisfies the equation on all of
X_{j}
. We now let
\nu\to\infty
. Let us define
C_{j}:=\left\langle\chi^{\prime\prime}(G-t)u,u\right\rangle_{X_{j}}
, where the subscript notation here means that we restrict integration to over
X_{j}
. The weight that we are integrating with respect to here is
\theta
(or rather
\theta^{t}
). Letting
\nu\to\infty
, and letting similarly as above
v_{j}
be the weak limit of a convergent subsequence of
\{v_{j,\nu}\}_{\nu}
, we have

(2.4.52)

\displaystyle(\overline{\partial}\overline{\partial}^{*}+\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1))v_{j}

\displaystyle=\chi^{\prime\prime}(G-t)\overline{\partial}G\wedge u

on
X_{j}
, that

(2.4.53)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\overline{\partial}v_{j}\right|\right|_{X_{j}}^{2}+\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}v_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{X_{j}}+2\epsilon_{j}(q+1)\left|\left|v_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{X_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{j},

and moreover that

(2.4.54)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{j}\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}v_{j}\right|\right|_{X_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\overline{\partial}\chi_{j}\right|\right|_{X_{j}}\sqrt{C_{j}}.

We may assume that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. From here we mimic the last part of the proof of Theorem
2.4.2
: Let
U\Subset X
. Letting
j\to\infty
, we find

(2.4.55)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\left|\left|\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}v_{j}\right|\right|^{2}_{j,U}=0.

Let then
v_{U}
denote the weak limit of a convergent subsequence of
\{\overline{\partial}^{*}v_{j}|_{U}\}_{j}
, consider an exhaustion of
X
by relatively compact subsets, say
\{U_{l}\}_{l}
, and let finally
v
be the weak limit a diagonal (sub)sequence constructed from (subsequences of)
\{v_{U_{l}}\}_{l}
. Then

(2.4.56)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}v

\displaystyle=c

in the sense of currents on
X
, where
c:=\chi^{\prime\prime}(G-t)\overline{\partial}G\wedge u
, and

(2.4.57)

\displaystyle\left|\left|v\right|\right|^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle\leq h_{t}(\chi^{\prime\prime}(G-t)u,u).

Noting that
\chi^{\prime\prime}(G-t)=\theta_{tt}(t,\cdot)
and
c=-(\overline{\partial}\theta_{t})\wedge u
, this completes the proof.
∎

2.4.4.
Nakano (semi)positivity for quasi-complete Kähler manifolds and pseudoeffective line bundles: Proof of Theorem 1.6

We may now prove Theorem
2.1.6
. Apart from a slight technicality on
e^{-\theta+\phi}
being a valid smooth weight for the hermitian metric of zero variation
h^{0}
, we get the theorem quite directly from Theorem
2.1.7
. That
e^{-\theta+\phi}
is indeed a valid smooth weight is explained below. Assuming this to be true for now, the proof of the theorem can be given as follows:

Proof of Theorem
2.1.6
.

Let
y\in Y
. We use the same notation as above, and let
t
generically be the real part of
y
. We also use
t
as generic coordinates on
Y
; hopefully, this will not cause any confusion. Let
u\in H_{0}
, and let
dt\wedge s
be second fundamental form associated with
D
and
D_{0}
, where
D
is the Chern connection of
H
, and
D_{0}
is the Chern connection of
H_{0}
, both with respect to
h
. Then
su
is the
h
-minimal solution to
\overline{\partial}(su)=-\overline{\partial}\theta_{t}\wedge u
, where the
\overline{\partial}
operator here is the one on
X
. Using Theorem
2.1.7
, let
v\in H
be such that
\overline{\partial}v=\overline{\partial}\theta_{t}\wedge u
and such that
\left|\left|v\right|\right|^{2}_{t}\leq h(\theta_{tt}u,u)(y)
. Let
\Theta
be the Chern curvature of
D
. Then
\Theta=\theta_{tt}\wedge
. Hence we have

(2.4.58)

\displaystyle h(\theta_{tt}u,u)(y)\geq\left|\left|v\right|\right|^{2}_{h}(y)\geq\left|\left|su\right|\right|^{2}_{h}(y),

which gives that

(2.4.59)

\displaystyle h(\theta_{tt}u,u)(t)-\left|\left|su\right|\right|^{2}_{h}(t)\geq 0.

Hence
i\Theta\geq_{N}0
by the subbundle curvature formula, (
2.2.35
).

Alternatively, we can use Theorem
2.1.4
to give a proof. Let
u\in H_{0}
be given, and let for each
x\in X
,
A_{x}
be the matrix (field) associated with
\partial_{z}\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta(\cdot,x)
, and
M_{x}:=\theta_{tt}(\cdot,x)
. For each
x\in X
, we also let
B_{x}
be the vector (field) identified with
\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta_{t}(\cdot,x)
, and put
\mathbf{F}_{x}:=\left|u\right|_{\omega}(x)B_{x}
. Let
\epsilon>0
. Since
\theta
is plurisubharmonic, it follows that we have, for each
x\in X
,

(2.4.60)

\displaystyle(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(M_{x}+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}B_{x}^{*}\geq 0.

Let
v:=\overline{\partial}_{z}(su)
. By Theorem
2.1.7
, we can find
u\in H
such that
\{u,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the Hörmander
h
-equation
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=v
. Hence the assertion follows by Theorem
2.1.4
.
∎

Some special weights

We now deal with the aforementioned technicality that
h
is a valid smooth weighted hermitian metric as defined in
[
15
]
, and which in the above proof was taken for granted. For this we do not really need all the assumptions in the theorem:

Let
X
be a complex
n
-dimensional manifold with a hermitian metric (on its complex tangent bundle), and let
\omega
denote the fundamental form of said metric. Suppose that
dV_{X}
is a given volume form on
X
, and also that
L
is a given holomorphic line bundle over
X
with a smooth hermitian metric
e^{-\phi}
. Let
G\leq 0
be a negative function on
X
, and for each
t\in(-\infty,0]
, let
\psi^{t}:=\chi(G-t)
, where
\chi
is a non-negative smooth real-valued function on
\mathbb{R}
which vanishes on
(-\infty,0]
. Let
H
be the Hilbert space consisting of
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms
u
on
X
such that

(2.4.61)

\displaystyle\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{\phi}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{X}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}e^{-\phi}dV_{X}<\infty.

We first show that multiplication by
e^{-\psi^{t}}
, for each
t
, is an element in
\text{Aut}(H)
, the space of complex-linear automorphisms
H\to H
. That is, so that
f:=e^{-\psi}:=t\mapsto e^{-\psi^{t}}
is a map
\mathbb{R}\to\text{Aut}(H)
.

Proposition 2.4.4
.

With the above notation,
f
is a map
\mathbb{R}\to\text{Aut}(H)
.

Proof.

It is clear that
f(t):=f^{t}
is linear. We need to show that
f^{t}u\in H
for
u\in H
, and that
f^{t}
is continuous. Let
u\in H
. We look at
\left|\left|f^{t}u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}
. For simplicity we shall write
dV
for
e^{-\phi}dV_{X}
. We have

\displaystyle\left|\left|f^{t}u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left|f^{t}u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV=\int_{X}\left|e^{-\chi(G-t)}u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV=\int_{X}\left|e^{-\chi(G-t)}\right|^{2}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV.

Since
\chi\geq 0
we obviously have
e^{-\chi}\leq 1
, so

\displaystyle\left|\left|f^{t}u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}\leq\int_{X}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV=\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}.

Thus, the proposition is true.
∎

Next, we shall show that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{(n)}(t)\in\text{Aut}(H)
.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

For each
t\mathbb{R}
,
f^{(n)}(t)\in\text{Aut}(H)
.

Proof.

We may assume that
t<0
, and again, linearity is clear. Let
u\in H
. We shall study
\left|\left|f^{(n)}(t)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}
, and we write as in the previous proof
e^{-\phi}dV_{X}
simply as
dV
. We find

\displaystyle\left|\left|f^{(n)}(t)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left|f^{(n)}(t)\right|\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV=\int_{X}\left|\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}e^{-\chi(G-t)}\right|\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV.

The key observation now is that since
\chi|_{(-\infty,0]}=0
, we have that
\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}\left(e^{-\chi(G-t)}\right)
when
G-t\leq 0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left|\left|f^{(n)}(t)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\int_{G>t}\left|\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}e^{-\chi(G-t)}\right|\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV.

Note that since
G\leq 0
, on
G>t
, it follows that
0\geq G>t
so that
-t>G-t>0
. Thus, for any
\epsilon>0
,
G-t\in[-\epsilon,-t+\epsilon]
, a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Since
\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}e^{-\chi(G-t)}
is smooth, it is bounded on this compact. Thus, there exists a constant
M=M(t)
, such that

\displaystyle\left|\left|f^{(n)}(t)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq M(t)\int_{G>t}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV\leq M\int_{X}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV=M\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Finally, we shall prove that
f:\mathbb{R}\to\text{Aut}(H)
is smooth, its
n
th derivative being given by
f^{(n)}
.

Theorem 2.4.6
.

f:\mathbb{R}\to\text{Aut}(H)
is smooth, with
f^{(n)}
as its
n
th order derivative. That is, more precisely, for
t,\Delta t_{1},\ldots,\Delta t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
u\in H
,

\displaystyle f^{(n)}(t)(\Delta t_{1},\ldots,\Delta t_{n})u:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Delta t_{j}f^{(n)}(t)u,

where we of course view
f^{(n)}(t)
as a multilinear continuous map.

Proof.

We may as in the previous proof assume
t<0
. The proof will be by induction on
n
, and we begin with the case that
n=1
. By definition of the derivative, what we need to show is that

(2.4.62)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\left|\left|f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)(h)\right|\right|_{H^{*}}}{\left|h\right|}=0.

By definition of the norm on
H^{*}
, it suffices to show that for all
u\in H\backslash\{0\}
,

(2.4.63)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\left|\left|(f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)(h))(u)\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}}{\left|h\right|^{2}}=0.

Indeed, given any
F\in\text{Aut}(H)
, by definition,

\displaystyle\left|\left|F\right|\right|^{2}_{H^{*}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{u\in H\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\left|\left|F(u)\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}}{\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}},

so taking the supremum in (
2.4.63
) we get (
2.4.62
).
Hence, we first take a closer look at the numerator in (
2.4.63
), namely,
\left|\left|(f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)(h))u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}
. This is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{X}\left|f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h\right|^{2}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV,

where, as before, we write
e^{-\phi}dV_{X}
simply as
dV
. We split the integral over the regions
G\leq t+h
and
G>t+h
, assuming here that
h
is negative. In the case that
h
is positive, we split instead the integral over
G\leq t
and
G>t
. The proof is similar in either case from this point on, and we stick here to the case that
h
is negative (in the case that
h
is positive, we replace everywhere in what follows
G>t+h
with
G>t
). Hence, on
G\leq t+h
, we have
G-t\leq h<0
. That means
f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h=0
here. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\left|\left|(f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\int_{G>t+h}\left|f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h\right|^{2}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV.

We now divide by
\left|h\right|
and get

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\left|(f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}}{\left|h\right|}

\displaystyle=\int_{G>t+h}\left|\frac{f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h}{h}\right|^{2}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV

\displaystyle=\int_{G>t+h}\left|\frac{f(t+h)-f(t)}{h}-f^{\prime}(t)\right|^{2}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV.

The idea now is to let
h\to 0
, and we will then need to show that
\frac{f(t+h)-f(t)}{h}\to f^{\prime}(t)
uniformly for all
x\in\{G>t+h\}
. That is, writing
G=G(x)
,
x\in X
,

(2.4.64)

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\chi(G(x)-t-h)}-e^{-\chi(G(x)-t)}}{h}\to\chi^{\prime}(G(x)-t)e^{-\chi(G(x)-t)}=(-1)(e^{-\chi(G(x)-t)})^{\prime}

uniformly in
x
. Let
g:=g(r):=e^{-\chi(r)}
. It suffices to show that given any
\epsilon>0
we can find a
\delta=\delta(\epsilon)
such that whenever
|s-r|<\delta
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{g(r)-g(s)}{r-s}-g^{\prime}(s)\right|<\epsilon.

Indeed, then we can take
r:=G(x)-t-h
and
s:=G(x)-t
, so that
r-s=-h
. Now, by the mean-value theorem, there is
p\in(s,r)
such that

(2.4.65)

\displaystyle\frac{g(r)-g(s)}{r-s}

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}(p).

We can write
p=\eta s+(1-\eta)r
for some
\eta\in(0,1)
. We can write
r=(r-s)+s
. Substituting this we find

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\eta s+(1-\eta)((r-s)+s)=\eta s+(1-\eta)s+(1-\eta)(r-s)=s+(1-\eta)(r-s).

We now get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{g(r)-g(s)}{r-s}-g^{\prime}(s)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|g^{\prime}(p)-g^{\prime}(s)\right|.

Now comes the crux of the argument. Since
g^{\prime}
is smooth, in particular continuous, it is
uniformly continuous
on compact subsets. Recall that we are considering
G=G(x)
for
x
in the set
\{G>t+h\}
. That is
-t>G-t>h
, so there is a compact, say
K\Subset\mathbb{R}
, such that we are considering
g^{\prime}
on (a subset of)
K
. Hence give
\epsilon>0
we can find
\delta=\delta(\epsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\left|p-s\right|<\delta\implies\left|g^{\prime}(p)-g^{\prime}(s)\right|<\epsilon.

Now,
\left|p-s\right|=(1-\eta)\left|r-s\right|
, and so if we let
\delta^{\prime}:=\frac{\delta}{1-\eta}
, it follows that for
\left|r-s\right|<\delta^{\prime}
, we have that
\left|\frac{g(r)-g(s)}{r-s}-g^{\prime}(s)\right|<\epsilon
. It follows that given
\epsilon>0
, we can choose sufficiently small such that

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\left|(f(t+h)-f(t)-f^{\prime}(t)h)u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi}}{\left|h\right|}
