\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\int_{G>t+h}\left|u\right|^{2}_{\omega}dV\leq\epsilon\left|\left|u\right|\right|^{2}_{\phi},

This suffices to show that
f^{\prime}(t)
is the derivative of
f=f(t)
as a map
U\ni t\mapsto f(t)\in\text{Aut}(H)
, and proves the case that
n=1
. For general case of
n
, the exact same argument applies, except that we replace
f
above with
f^{(n-1)}
; as before we may focus on integrating over
\{G>t+h\}
for
h
small. The same ”trick” with the mean-value theorem and uniform continuity applies for the conclusion.
∎

2.4.5.
Plurisubharmonicity properties of minimal solutions associated with quasi-complete Kähler manifolds and (semi)positive line bundles: Proof of Theorem 1.9

We have now come quite far, and it remains only to prove Theorems
2.1.9
and Theorem
2.1.10
. We shall begin, as is natural, with the proof of Theorem
2.1.9
. This is analogous to the proof of Theorem
2.1.5
, except that we use Theorem
2.1.8
instead of Theorem
2.1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1.9
.

We use notation similar to before. Let
[u]
be a holomorphic (local) section of
Q:=H/H_{0}
such that
u
is a (smooth) representative of
[u]
. Then
u_{m}=m_{l}[u]
, where
m_{l}
denotes the minimal lifting operator with respect to
h
. Thus, we want to prove that
-\tilde{f}(\left|\left|[u]\right|\right|^{2}_{h^{Q}})
is plurisubharmonic,
h^{Q}
being the hermitian metric on
Q
induced by
h
, and may therefore use Theorem
2.1.8
. Let, as in the proof of Theorem
2.1.5
,
A_{x}:=\partial_{z}\overline{\partial}_{z}\theta(\cdot,x)
, and
M_{x}:=[\Theta_{j\bar{k}}](\cdot,x)
, for each
x\in X
. Let
\xi:=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and
\epsilon>0
, be given. We put
V:=\overline{\partial}_{z}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{j}\xi_{j}u_{m}\right)
, write
u=u_{m}+u_{0}
, where
u_{0}\in H
, and find, since
\delta_{j}=\partial_{t_{j}}-\partial_{t_{j}}\theta\wedge
, that

(2.4.66)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{j}\overline{\partial}_{z}\left(-\partial_{t_{j}}\theta\wedge u_{m}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{j}\left(-\overline{\partial}_{z}\partial_{t_{j}}\theta\wedge u_{m}-\partial_{t_{j}}\wedge v\right).

By our assumption,
\partial_{t}\theta=0
on the support of
v
, so it follows that

(2.4.67)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\partial}_{z}\partial_{t_{j}}\theta\wedge(\xi_{j}u_{m}).

Let
\mathbf{b}_{s}
be the vector (field) identified with
b_{s}:=\overline{\partial}_{z}\partial_{t_{s}}\theta
, and let
\mathbf{F}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left|\xi_{j}u_{m}\right|_{\omega}\mathbf{b}_{j}
. Let, for each
x\in X
,
\mathbf{F}_{x}:=\mathbf{F}(\cdot,x)
, and
B_{x}
have
j
th column equal to
\mathbf{b}_{j}(\cdot,x)
. Considering
u_{j}:=\xi_{j}u_{m}
, the proof of Theorem
2.1.5
lets us apply Theorem
2.4.3
to find a
U\in H
such that
\{U,\{\mathbf{F}_{x}\},\{A_{x}\}\}
satisfies the Hörmander
h
-estimate for the
\overline{\partial}_{z}(\cdot)=V
equation. Also the plurisubharmonicity of
\theta
gives that at each
x\in X
,

\displaystyle(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(M_{x}+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}B_{x}^{*}\geq 0.

Hence the assertion follows by Theorem
2.1.8
.
∎

2.4.6.
Plurisubharmonicity properties of minimal solutions associated with quasi-complete Kähler manifolds and pseudoeffective line bundles: Proof of Theorem 1.10

Finally, we prove Theorem
2.1.10
. Thanks to our discussion in section
2.4.4
, the proof of Theorem
2.1.9
., and Theorem
2.1.7
, the proof is going to be very short.

Proof of Theorem
2.1.10
.

By our discussion in section
2.4.4
,
y\mapsto e^{-\chi(G-t)}
is a valid smooth weight for
h^{0}
of
h
.
The remaining proof is analogous to that of Theorem
2.1.9
: We use Theorem
2.1.8
. The condition that
(A_{x}+\epsilon I_{n})-B_{x}(M_{x}+\epsilon I_{m})^{-1}B_{x}^{*}\geq 0
follows as before from
\theta
being plurisubharmonicity, and the Hörmander
h
-estimate part follows as in the proof of Theorem
2.1.9
, except that we use Theorem
2.1.7
instead of Theorem
2.4.3
.
∎

2.5.
End and preview

We end by commenting briefly on the theme of the sequel to this paper (
[
17
]
). In that paper we turn to applications in
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension of Ohsawa-Takegosghi type and (strong) openness. The paper will be joint work with Xu Wang, and among our main results in there are a sharp Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem on quasi-complete Kähler manifolds, and a new, complex Brunn-Minkowski theoretical, proof of the strong openness conjecture.

Chapter 3
Paper 3

A HILBERT BUNDLES APPROACH TO COMPLEX BRUNN-MINKOWSKI THEORY, III

TAI TERJE HUU NGUYEN AND XU WANG

3.1.
Introduction

In
[
13
]
, Berndtsson and Lempert gave a ”complex Brunn-Minkowski proof” of the sharp Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem
[
19
,
47
]
for pseudoconvex domains in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and introduced the so-called
Berndtsson-Lempert method
for
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension. The reader may refer to
[
37
]
for the original Ohsawa-Takegoshi theorem, and
[
23
]
for a simple proof. The main observation of Berndtsson and Lempert is that the sharp effective
L^{2}
estimate, in the Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem, can be seen to follow from a certain decreasingness property of minimal extensions. Moreover, this monotonicity property is a direct consequence (see
[
11
, Corollary 4.3]
) of the convexity theorem of Berndtsson (see
[
11
, Lemma 4.2]
and
[
2
,
3
]
), which is a fundamental result in complex Brunn-Minkowski theory. In this paper, which is a sequel to
[
15
]
, we shall generalize the Berndtsson-Lempert method from the local setting of domains in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
to the global setting of complex manifolds. We also discuss applications to (the) strong openness (conjecture of Demailly) and singularity theory of plurisubharmonic functions. More specifically, we shall prove a general monotonicity theorem, and use this to obtain the results related to
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension and strong openness. The general monotonicity theorem is thus our main result, and the other results are applications of it (and its proof). Related results to, and applications of, the main theorem (and its proof), include the following: a Hörmander proof (see Theorem
3.3.1
) of the Demailly-Kollár semi-continuity theorem
[
28
]
; a Donnelly-Fefferman proof (see Theorem
3.3.4
) of Guan-Zhou’s strong openness theorem
[
48
]
; a decreasing theorem approach (see Theorem
3.3.5
and Theorem
3.3.6
) to Guan’s sharp strong openness theorem
[
46
]
; a decreasing theorem approach (see Theorem
3.4.9
) to Zhou-Zhu’s sharp Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem
[
43
]
.

We next state our main result. To set the stage, let
(X,\omega)
be an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kahler manifold, and let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a pseudoeffective holomorphic line bundle over
X
. Let
H
be the trivial Hilbert bundle over
\mathbb{C}
consisting of
L
-valued
(n,q)
-forms
u
such that

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle||u||^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle:=\int_{X}|u|^{2}_{\omega}e^{-\phi}<\infty,

equipped with the inner product that induces the norm
||\cdot||_{\phi}
. We denote this inner product by
h^{0}
, and view it as a hermitian metric of zero variation on
H
. We let
H_{0}
be the subbundle over
H
consisting of elements in
H
which vanishes under the action of the
\overline{\partial}
-operator; the
\overline{\partial}
-operator here being the one on
X
(the one on
\mathbb{C}
will have no play in our discussion). We write a generic point in
\mathbb{C}
as
y
, and the real part of
y
as
t
. Suppose that
G\leq 0
is a function on
X
such that

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle\phi+\lambda G

is plurisubharmonic for some
\lambda>0
, and consider the smooth weight
w
for
h^{0}
given by
y\mapsto e^{-\lambda\max\{G-t\}}
; this is a limiting case of the weights in Theorems 1.6 and 1.10 in
[
16
]
. We denote the smooth weighted hermitian metric induced by
h^{0}
with weight
w
by
h
. Thus, explicitly, if
u,v\in H
, we have, for all
y\in\mathbb{C}
,

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle h_{y}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}(u,v)_{\omega}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t\}}.

Let
||\cdot||_{t}
denote the norm induced by
h_{y}
; it is clear that it only depends on
t
.
Let
0\leq\alpha<\lambda
, and let
S_{\alpha}
consists of the elements of
H_{0}
which are locally
L^{2}
-integrable against
e^{-\phi-\alpha G}
. Our main result is the following theorem:

Theorem 3.1.1

(Main theorem)
.

With the above notation and set-up, let
u\in H_{0}
. Then,

(3.1.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto e^{-\alpha t}\inf\left\{||v||^{2}_{t}:(v-u)\in S_{\alpha}\right\}

is decreasing.

For the proof of Theorem
3.1.1
, we shall use Theorem 4.15 in
[
15
]
and Theorem 1.6 in
[
16
]
, together with the following Hormander type of theorem:

Theorem 3.1.2
.

With the above notation and set-up, replace
\lambda\max\{G-t\}
with
\chi(G-t)
, where
\chi
is a smooth convex function on
\mathbb{R}
which vanishes identically on
(-\infty,0]
, and which satisfies that
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
. Fix
t<0
and
\alpha
(as above), and let
F\in H_{0}
. Then there exists
\tilde{F}\in H_{0}
such that

(3.1.5)

\displaystyle||F-\tilde{F}||^{2}_{\phi+\alpha G}\leq\frac{9\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||^{2}_{t}+2e^{-\alpha t}||F||^{2}_{\phi},

and

(3.1.6)

\displaystyle||\tilde{F}||^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||^{2}_{t},

where
||\cdot||_{\phi+\alpha G}
is defined like
||\cdot||_{\phi}
, except that we replace
\phi
with
\phi+\alpha G
.

The proof of Theorem
3.1.2
is based on the same idea for that of Theorem 1.7 in
[
16
]
. For the benefit of the reader, we give the proof in the next section. This concludes our short introduction. In the remainder of the paper we give the proof of the main theorem, and discuss related results and applications as mentioned above.

3.2.
Proof of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2

In this section we prove our main theorem, that is, Theorem
3.1.1
, and Theorem
3.1.2
. Since the proof of the former uses the latter, we begin with the latter.

3.2.1.
A Hormander type of theorem: Proof of Theorem 1.2

The proof follows much of that of Theorem 1.7 in
[
16
]
, and we shall use the same notation, referring also to
[
16
]
. The main difference is that instead of solving the
\overline{\partial}
-Laplace equation with the weight
\theta^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0}
, we solve it with a weight of the form

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle\Theta^{j}_{\nu}

\displaystyle:=\phi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0}+\alpha G_{\nu}^{j}-c\ln(1-f(G_{\nu}^{j})),

for some constant
c>0
and some sufficiently smooth real-valued function
f
; both to be further specified. As in the proof of Theorem 1.7 in
[
16
]
, we have

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}(\phi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0}),i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi_{j}^{\nu}+\phi_{0})

\displaystyle\geq-\epsilon_{j}\omega.

A direct computation gives, where
F
is also a sufficiently smooth real-valued function,

\displaystyle\partial\overline{\partial}F(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu}))

\displaystyle=(F^{\prime\prime}(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu}))|f^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})|^{2}-F^{\prime}(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu}))f^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu}))\partial G^{j}_{\nu}\wedge\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle-F^{\prime}(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu}))f^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})\partial\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}.

Hence, taking
F:=-c\ln(\cdot)
, we get

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}(-\ln(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})))

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{c|f^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})|^{2}}{(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu}))^{2}}+\frac{cf^{\prime\prime}(G{j}_{\nu})}{1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})}\right)i\partial G^{j}_{\nu}\wedge\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}+\frac{cf^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})}{1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})}i\partial\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}.

Assume that
f(G^{j}_{\nu})\leq 0
, and that
f^{\prime}(G_{\nu}^{j})\geq 0
. If
i\partial\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}\geq 0
, then

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}\Phi^{j}_{\nu}

\displaystyle\geq-\epsilon_{j}\omega+\frac{cf^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})}{1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})}i\partial G^{j}_{\nu}\wedge\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}.

On the other hand, if
i\partial\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}\leq 0
, then

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}\Phi^{j}_{\nu}

\displaystyle\geq-\epsilon_{j}\omega+\frac{cf^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})}{1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})}i\partial G^{j}_{\nu}\wedge\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}+\left(\alpha+cf^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu}\right)i\partial\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}.

Assume that
0\leq f^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})\leq 1
, and consider
c:=\lambda-\alpha
. Recall that
G^{j}_{\nu}=\frac{(\psi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})-(\phi^{j}_{\nu}+\phi_{0})}{\lambda}
. Hence, in either case, whether
i\partial\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}\geq 0
, or not, we get, choosing
c:=\lambda-\alpha
as above,

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}\Phi^{j}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{j}\omega+\frac{(\lambda-\alpha)f^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})}{1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})}i\partial G^{j}_{\nu}\wedge\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}.

Consider
c^{j}_{\nu}:=\overline{\partial}\left((1-f^{\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu}))F\right)=-f^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})\overline{\partial}G^{j}_{\nu}\wedge F
, and let, using the same notation as in the proof of Theorem 1.7 in
[
16
]
,
v_{j,\nu,k}
satisfy

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle\left(\square_{j,\nu,k}+(q+1)\epsilon_{j}\right)v_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle=c_{j,\nu}

and

(3.2.9)

\displaystyle||\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}||^{2}_{j,\nu,k}+||\overline{\partial}^{*}v_{j,\nu,k}||^{2}_{j,\nu,k}+\epsilon_{j}(q+1)||v_{j,\nu,k}||^{2}_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\sqrt{\frac{(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})|f^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})|}{\lambda-\alpha}}F\right|\right|^{2}_{j,\nu,k}.

The expression in the square root in the right-hand side above is found in a similar way as how (4.43) in the proof of Theorem 1.7 in
[
16
]
is found, using Corollary 2.4 in
[
16
]
. The idea now is to choose
f
such that the
t
-norm can be introduced from the
(j,\nu,k)
-norm above. Note that in the latter there is a term
e^{-\alpha G^{j}_{\nu}}
, and this can be replaced bounded by
e^{-\alpha t}
on
\{G^{j}_{\nu}>t\}
. Note also that since
f^{\prime}\geq 0
,
f
is increasing, so on
\{G^{j}_{\nu}>t\}
, we have
1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})\leq 1-t
. To introduce the
t
-norm we can therefore consider
f
such that
f^{\prime\prime}(s)=\chi^{\prime}(s-t)e^{-\chi(s-t)}=-\frac{d}{ds}\left(e^{-\chi(s-t)}\right)
. Then the factor
f^{\prime\prime}(G^{j}_{\nu})
in the above
(j,\nu,k)
-norm will introduce the factor
e^{-\chi(G^{j}_{\nu}-t)}
appearing in the (approximated)
t
-norm, and also reduce the domain of integration to over where
\{G^{j}_{\nu}>t\}
. Integrating

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{ds}(-e^{-\chi(s-t)}),

we get

(3.2.11)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=C-e^{-\chi(s-t)}

for some constant
C
. We want
0\leq f^{\prime}\leq 1
, so we consider taking
C:=1
. Integrating once more, we finally get

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle f(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}1-e^{-\chi(x-t)}\;dx.

There is another factor in the
(j,\nu,k)
-norm which also does not appear in the
t
-norm, namely,

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle e^{(\lambda-\alpha)\ln\left(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})\right)}=\left(1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})\right)^{\lambda-\alpha},

but this can, similar to the term
1-f(G^{j}_{\nu})
above, be bounded above by
(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha}
on
\{G^{j}_{\nu}>t\}
. Choosing
f
as above, we therefore get (recall also that
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
)

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle||\overline{\partial}v_{j,\nu,k}||^{2}_{j,\nu,k}+||\overline{\partial}^{*}v_{j,\nu,k}||^{2}_{j,\nu,k}+\epsilon_{j}(q+1)||v_{j,\nu,k}||^{2}_{j,\nu,k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}}{\lambda-\alpha}e^{-\alpha t}||F||^{2}_{t}.

As in the proof of Theorem 1.7 in
[
16
]
, we now let
k\to\infty
first, and then
\nu,j\to\infty
, considering as usual weak limits. Analogous to in there, we then get a solution
u
satisfying

(3.2.15)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}u

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}((1-f(G))F)

on
X
and

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle||u||_{\phi+\alpha G}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq||u||^{2}_{\phi+\alpha G-(\lambda-\alpha)\ln(1-f(G)))}\leq\frac{\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}}{\lambda-\alpha}e^{-\alpha t}||F||^{2}_{t},

the middle norm being defined similarly as the norm furthest to the left,except that we replace
\phi
with
\phi-(\lambda-\alpha)\ln(1-f(G))
. Finally, let
\tilde{F}:=(1-f^{\prime}(G))F-u
. Then
\tilde{F}
fits our needs. Indeed, the estimate on
u
immediately gives

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle||\tilde{F}-(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F||^{2}_{\phi+\alpha G}\leq\frac{\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||_{t}^{2}.

Since
G\leq 0
, the above inequality gives

\displaystyle||\tilde{F}||^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq 2||\tilde{F}-(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F||^{2}_{\phi}+2||(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F||^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||_{t}^{2}+2||(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F||^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||_{t}^{2}+2\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}e^{-\phi-2\chi(G-t)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}+2\right)||F||_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||_{t}^{2},

which gives (
3.1.6
). To prove (
3.1.5
), note that (
3.2.17
) gives

||\tilde{F}-F||^{2}_{\phi+\alpha G,G<t}\leq\frac{\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||_{t}^{2}.

Hence together with

||\tilde{F}-F||^{2}_{\phi+\alpha G,G\geq t}\leq e^{-\alpha t}||\tilde{F}-F||^{2}_{\phi}\leq 2e^{-\alpha t}\left(||\tilde{F}||^{2}_{\phi}+||F||^{2}_{\phi}\right),

we see that (
3.1.6
) gives (
3.1.5
).

3.2.2.
A general decreasingness theorem: Proof of Theorem 1.1

Next, we give the proof of our main theorem, Theorem
3.1.1
. It follows as a special case of the following general monotonicity theorem by letting
\chi(x)
converge to
\lambda\max\{s,0\}
:

Theorem 3.2.1
.

With the same assumptions and notation in Theorem
3.2.1
, let instead
h_{y}
be defined by

(3.2.18)

\displaystyle h_{y}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\int_{X}(u,v)_{\omega}e^{-\phi-\chi(G-t)},

writing
t
for the real part of
y
(as usual), where
\chi
is a smooth convex function on
\mathbb{R}
vanishing identically on
(-\infty,0]
, and satisfying that
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
. Then the same conclusion as in Theorem
3.2.1
holds. That is, given
u\in H_{0}
,

(3.2.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto e^{-\alpha t}\inf\{||v||^{2}_{t}:(u-v)\in S_{\alpha}\}

is decreasing, where now
||\cdot||_{t}
is the norm induced by
h_{y}
above.

Proof.

Let
\Theta_{0}
denote the Chern curvature of the Chern connection of
H_{0}
with respect to
h
. By Theorem 1.6 in
[
16
]
, it follows that
i\Theta_{0}\geq_{N}0
since
\theta
is plurisubharmonic under our assumptions. Let
\Theta_{0}^{\vee}
denote the Chern curvature of the dual bundle
(H_{0})^{*}:=H_{0}^{*}
with respect to the dual hermitian metric
h^{\vee}
of
h
. Then
i\Theta^{\vee}\leq 0
, so it follows by Theorem 4.15 in
[
15
]
, that

(3.2.20)

\displaystyle t\mapsto\ln(||f||^{2}_{t}),

is convex for all holomorphic (local) sections of
H_{0}^{*}
, where the
t
-norm here is the norm induced by
h^{\vee}_{y}
. Consider
Q_{\alpha}:=H_{0}/S_{\alpha}
, and let
h^{Q_{\alpha}}
denote the hermitian metric on
Q_{\alpha}
induced by
h
. By the quotient bundle curvature formula from
[
15
]
, it follows that for all holomorphic (local) sections
\hat{f}
of
(Q_{\alpha})^{*}
,

(3.2.21)

\displaystyle t\mapsto\ln(||\hat{f}||^{2}_{t})

is convex, where the
t
-norm here denotes the norm induced by the dual hermitian metric of
h^{Q_{\alpha}}
. The point of all this is of course that
\inf\{||v||^{2}_{t}:(v-u)\in S_{\alpha}\}
is precisely equal to the norm of
[u]
with respect to
h_{y}^{Q_{\alpha}}
, where
[u]
denotes any holomorphic (local) section of
Q_{\alpha}
with
u
as a (smooth) representative. What we are trying to prove amounts therefore to certain decreasingness properties of norms of holomorphic (local) sections of
Q_{\alpha}
. That is, turning to the dual, certain increasingness properties of the norms of continuous linear functionals on
Q_{\alpha}
. Now, with this in mind, fix
t<0
, let
F\in H_{0}
be such that
||[u]||^{2}_{t}=||F||^{2}_{t}
, and let
\tilde{F}
be given as in Theorem
3.1.2
. That is,
(\tilde{F}-F)\in S_{\alpha}
, and

(3.2.22)

\displaystyle||\tilde{F}||^{2}_{0}=||\tilde{F}||^{2}_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\lambda(1-\lambda)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||F||^{2}_{t}=\frac{4\lambda(1-\lambda)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||[u]||^{2}_{t}.

That
(\tilde{F}-F)\in S_{\alpha}
means that
[\tilde{F}]=[u]
. Hence, by definition of quotient norms, we have shown that

(3.2.23)

\displaystyle||[u]||^{2}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\lambda(1-\lambda)^{\lambda-\alpha+1}e^{-\alpha t}}{\lambda-\alpha}||[u]||^{2}_{t}.

Hence for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
C=C(\epsilon,\lambda,\alpha)>0
, such that

(3.2.24)

\displaystyle||[u]||

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-(\alpha+\epsilon)t}||[u]||^{2}_{t}.

Turning to the dual we get that for all
\hat{f}\in(Q_{\alpha})^{*}
, where the norm notation is as above,

(3.2.25)

\displaystyle||\hat{f}||^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C}e^{-(\alpha+\epsilon)t}||\hat{f}||^{2}_{0}.

The left-hand side is by our discussion above logarithmically convex in
t
. Then

(3.2.26)

\displaystyle\ln(||\hat{f}||^{2}_{t})+(\alpha+\epsilon)t

is convex in
t
, and
(
3.2.25
) shows that it is bounded above as
t\to-\infty
.
Hence it must be increasing as a function of
t
. Hence, taking the exponential,
e^{(\alpha+\epsilon)t}||\hat{f}||^{2}_{t}
is increasing. Thus, turning back to
Q_{\alpha}
,
e^{-(\alpha+\epsilon)t}||[u]||^{2}_{t}
is decreasing. Letting
\epsilon\to 0
, we finally get that recall that if
t\geq 9
, then
\theta_{y}
is independent of
t
, so the same is true for all the
t
-norms)

(3.2.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto e^{-\alpha t}||[u]||^{2}_{t}

is decreasing, which completes the proof.

∎

Note that the previous proof also gives the following general convexity theorem:

Theorem 3.2.2
.

With the same notation and assumptions as in Theorem
3.2.1
, let
f
be any holomorphic (local) section of
H_{0}^{*}
(or
(Q_{\alpha})^{*}
) . Then

(3.2.28)

\displaystyle t\mapsto\ln(||f||^{2}_{t})

is convex.

Similar to the case of Theorem
3.1.1
, Theorem
3.2.2
also holds with
\theta^{y}=\phi+\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}
, by letting
\chi(s)
tend to
\lambda\max\{s,0\}
.

This finishes the proofs of the theorem from the introduction. In the remainder of the paper we turn to related results and applications.

3.3.
Singularity theory of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions

In this section we discuss applications to singularity theory of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions. In particular, we shall give a (semi) new proof, using Theorem
3.1.2
, of a semi-continuity result of Demailly-Kollar (
[
28
]
), and a complex Brunn-Minkowski proof, using Theorem
3.1.1
, of strong openness.

3.3.1.
Multiplier ideal sheaf and Demailly-Kollár theory

We begin by recalling the notions of
quasi-plurisubharmonic
metrics, and
multiplier ideal sheaves
:

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
L
be a holomorphic line bundle over a complex manifold
X
. A metric
e^{-\phi}
on
L
is said to be
quasi-plurisubharmonic
(quasi psh) if its local potential
\phi
is quasi-psh, i.e.
\phi
can be locally written as the sum of a psh function and a smooth function. If
(L,e^{-\phi})
is a line bundle with quasi-psh metric, then the sheaf
\mathcal{I}(\phi)
(of germs of holomorphic functions
f
such that
|f|^{2}e^{-\phi}
is locally integrable) is called the
multiplier ideal sheaf
associated to
\phi
.

The notion of multiplier ideal sheaf comes from Nadel’s paper
[
34
]
. Nadel proved that
\mathcal{I}(\phi)
is a coherent ideal sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
(see
[
25
, Proposition 5.7]
for the details), and obtained a nice criterion for the existence of Kähler-Einstein metrics on certain Fano manifolds using
\mathcal{I}(\phi)
. Later, Demailly-Kollár
[
28
]
found a simpler proof of Nadel’s criterion using the following semi-continuity result (see
[
28
, Theorem 0.2]
); we use the abbreviation
psh
for plurisubharmonic:

Theorem 3.3.1
.

Let
\phi
be a psh function on the unit ball
\mathbb{B}
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and let
G,G_{j}\leq 0
such that

(3.3.1)

\phi+\lambda G_{j}
and
\phi+\lambda G

are psh on
\mathbb{B}
for some constant
\lambda>1
. Assume that (the Lebesgue measure is omitted)

(3.3.2)

\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\beta G}<\infty,\ \ \lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|G_{j}-G|=0,

for some
1<\beta<\lambda
. Then

(3.3.3)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{|z|<r}|e^{-\phi-G_{j}}-e^{-\phi-G}|=0,\ \ \ \forall\ 0<r<1.

Proof.

For the convenience of the reader, we shall include a proof here. Our proof depends only on the Hörmander estimate, but the idea is already implicitly included in
[
28
]
. We shall use

(3.3.4)

\int|e^{-\phi-G_{j}}-e^{-\phi-G}|\leq\int_{G_{j}\geq t}|e^{-\phi-G_{j}}-e^{-\phi-G}|+\int_{G_{j}<t}e^{-\phi-G}+\int_{G_{j}<t}e^{-\phi-G_{j}}

By our assumption, the psh functions
\phi+G_{j}
converge to the psh function
\phi+G
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{B})
. Since for psh functions,
L^{1}
convergence implies almost everywhere convergence, we know that
G_{j}
converges to
G
almost everywhere. Thus the Lebesgue bounded convergence theorem implies

(3.3.5)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\left(\int_{G_{j}\geq t}|e^{-\phi-G_{j}}-e^{-\phi-G}|+\int_{G_{j}<t}e^{-\phi-G}\right)=\int_{G<t}e^{-\phi-G}.

for every fixed
t<0
. The main difficulty is to control the last term
\int_{G_{j}<t}e^{-\phi-G_{j}}
in (
3.3.4
), and the idea is to use Theorem
3.1.2
with
F=1
and
\alpha=1
. Letting
\chi(s)
converge to
\lambda\max\{s,0\}
we get holomorphic functions
F_{j,t}
on
\mathbb{B}
with (see (
3.1.6
))

(3.3.6)

\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F_{j,t}|^{2}e^{-\phi}\leq\frac{4\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda}e^{-t}}{\lambda-1}\,\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G_{j}-t,0\}},

and (by (
3.2.17
))

(3.3.7)

\int_{G_{j}<t}|F_{j,t}-1|^{2}e^{-\phi-G_{j}}\leq\frac{\lambda(1-t)^{\lambda}e^{-t}}{\lambda-1}\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G_{j}-t,0\}}.

By the Lebesgue bounded convergence theorem we have

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G_{j}-t,0\}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}:=A(t).

Hence for every fixed
t\leq-1
, we can choose
j(t)
such that

\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G_{j}-t,0\}}\leq 2A(t),\ \ \forall\ j\geq j(t).

Lemma
3.3.2
below further gives

e^{-\beta t}\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G_{j}-t,0\}}\leq\frac{4\lambda}{\lambda-\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\beta G},\ \ \forall\ j\geq j(t),\ t\leq-1.

Hence (
3.3.6
) implies that there exists
t_{0}<-1
such that

\sup_{|z|<r}|F_{j,t}(z)|\leq\frac{1}{2},\ \ \forall\ j\geq j(t),\ t\leq t_{0}.

Thus by (
3.3.7
) (when
t_{0}
is smooth enough)

\int_{G_{j}<t,|z|<r}e^{-\phi-G_{j}}\leq e^{(\beta-1)t/2},\ \ \forall\ j\geq j(t),\ t\leq t_{0}.

By (
3.3.5
), we can choose
j(t)
such that

(3.3.8)

\int_{G_{j}\geq t,|z|<r}|e^{-\phi-G_{j}}-e^{-\phi-G}|+\int_{G_{j}<t,|z|<r}e^{-\phi-G}\leq 2\int_{G<t,|z|<r}e^{-\phi-G},\ \ \forall\ j\geq j(t),

so (
3.3.4
) gives

\int_{|z|<r}|e^{-\phi-G_{j}}-e^{-\phi-G}|\leq e^{(\beta-1)t/2}+2\int_{G<t,|z|<r}e^{-\phi-G},\ \ \forall\ j\geq j(t),\ t\leq t_{0}.

Note that the right hand side goes to zero when
t\to-\infty
. Hence, (
3.3.3
) follows.
∎

In the previous proof we used the following lemma:

Lemma 3.3.2
.

For every
t\leq-1
, we have

(3.3.9)

\displaystyle A(t)e^{-\beta t}\leq\frac{2\lambda}{\lambda-\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\beta G}.

Proof.

Note that

(3.3.10)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-\lambda\max\{s-t,0\}}e^{-\beta t}\,dt=\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda-\beta)\beta}e^{-\beta s}-\frac{1}{\beta},\ \ \forall\ \lambda>\beta,

gives

(3.3.11)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}A(t)e^{-\beta t}\,dt\leq\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda-\beta)\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}e^{-\phi-\beta G}<\infty.

Since
A(t)
is increasing in
t
, we have

\int_{-\infty}^{0}A(s)e^{-\beta s}\,ds\geq\int_{t}^{0}A(s)e^{-\beta s}\,ds\geq A(t)\int_{t}^{0}e^{-\beta s}\,ds\geq\frac{A(t)e^{-\beta t}}{2\beta}

for every
t\leq-1
. Hence the lemma follows from (
3.3.11
).
∎

By Berndtsson’s solution of the openness conjecture (see
[
6
]
, and also the next section for further developments), we know that Theorem
3.3.1
is also true in case that
\beta=1
.

3.3.2.
The strong openness theorem (the strong openness conjecture)

In this section, we shall show how to use the Donelly-Fefferman estimate to prove the strong openness theorem of Guan-Zhou (
[
48
]
), which used to be known as the strong openness conjecture of Demailly
[
24
, Remark 15.2.2]
. The reader may also see
[
32
,
11
,
6
,
29
,
30
,
33
]
and references therein for related results. We start by giving the needed details on the Donelly-Fefferman estimates; for the proof it might be helpful to also look back at the proof of Theorem
3.1.2
:

The Donnelly-Fefferman estimate

Theorem 3.3.3
.

Let
F
be a holomorphic function on the unit ball
\mathbb{B}
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and let
G\leq 0
be a smooth plurisubharmonic function on

\mathbb{B}
. Let
\chi\geq 0
be a smooth convex increasing function on
\mathbb{R}
vanishing identically on
(-\infty,0]
, and satisfying
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq\lambda
for some
\lambda>0
(by approximation, we may consider in the limit
\chi(s)=\lambda\max\{s,0\}
). Then for every
t<0
and
\alpha\geq 0
, we can find another holomorphic function
\tilde{F}
on
\mathbb{B}
such that

(3.3.12)

\int_{G<t}|\tilde{F}-F|^{2}e^{-\alpha G}\leq-4\lambda te^{-\alpha t}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)}

and

(3.3.13)

\int_{\mathbb{B}}|\tilde{F}|^{2}\leq(2-8\lambda te^{-\alpha t})\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)}.

Proof.

Put

\sigma(s):=\int_{0}^{s}1-e^{-\chi(x-t)}\,dx.

We know that

\sigma(0)=0,\ \ \sigma^{\prime}(s)=1-e^{-\chi(s-t)}\geq 0.

Hence
0\leq\sigma^{\prime}\leq 1
and
\sigma^{\prime}(s)=0
when
s\leq t
. Thus we have

(3.3.14)

t\leq\sigma(t)\leq\sigma(G)\leq 0,\ \ \sigma^{\prime\prime}(G)=\chi^{\prime}(G-t)e^{-\chi(G-t)}\geq 0.

Now we know that

\psi:=-\log(-\sigma(G))

satisfies

i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\geq i\partial\psi\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi.

By the Donnelly-Fefferman estimate, we can solve
\overline{\partial}u=\overline{\partial}((1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F)
with

\int_{\mathbb{B}}|u|^{2}e^{-\alpha G}\leq 4\int_{\mathbb{B}}|\sigma^{\prime\prime}(G)\overline{\partial}G|^{2}_{i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi}|F|^{2}e^{-\alpha G}.

Notice that

i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\geq\frac{i\partial\overline{\partial}(\sigma(G))}{-\sigma(G)}\geq\frac{\sigma^{\prime\prime}(G)i\partial G\wedge\overline{\partial}G}{-\sigma(G)}

gives

|\sigma^{\prime\prime}(G)\overline{\partial}G|^{2}_{i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi}\leq\sigma^{\prime\prime}(G)|\sigma(G)|\leq-\lambda t\,e^{-\chi(G-t)}.

Hence we have

\int_{\mathbb{B}}|u|^{2}e^{-\alpha G}\leq-4\lambda t\int_{G\geq t}|F|^{2}e^{-\alpha G}e^{-\chi(G-t)}\leq-4\lambda te^{-\alpha t}\int_{G\geq t}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)}.

Take
\tilde{F}=(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F-u
, we get

\int_{\mathbb{B}}|\tilde{F}-(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F|^{2}e^{-\alpha G}\leq-4\lambda te^{-\alpha t}\int_{G\geq t}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)},

which gives (
3.3.12
). Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}}|\tilde{F}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{B}}|\tilde{F}-(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F|^{2}+2\int_{\mathbb{B}}|(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-8\lambda te^{-\alpha t}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)}+2\int_{\mathbb{B}}|(1-\sigma^{\prime}(G))F|^{2}

\displaystyle=-8\lambda te^{-\alpha t}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)}+2\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-2\chi(G-t)}

\displaystyle\leq(2-8\lambda te^{-\alpha t})\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-\chi(G-t)},

gives (
3.3.13
).
∎

The strong openness theorem of Guan-Zhou (
[
48
]
) is the following; as mentioned, we shall prove it using the above Donelly-Fefferman estimate:

Theorem 3.3.4
.

Let
F
be a holomorphic function on the unit ball
\mathbb{B}
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Let
G\leq 0
be a psh function on

\mathbb{B}
. If
\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\,e^{-\beta G}<\infty
for some
\beta>0
then there exists
\alpha>\beta
such that
|F|^{2}\,e^{-\alpha G}
is integrable near the origin of
\mathbb{B}
.

Proof.

Put

(3.3.15)

\alpha_{0}:=\sup\{\alpha\geq 0:F_{0}\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha G)_{0}\}.

It suffices to show that
\alpha_{0}>\beta
. Fix
\lambda>\beta
and put

||F||_{t}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}e^{-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}},\ \ t\leq 0.

If
\alpha_{0}<\infty
then the Donnelly-Fefferman Lemma below gives

C:=\inf_{t<-1}\{(-t)e^{-\alpha_{0}t}||F||_{t}^{2}\}>0.

But since
\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\,e^{-\beta G}<\infty
, we know (
3.3.10
) gives

\infty>\int_{-\infty}^{0}||F||_{t}^{2}e^{-\beta t}\,dt\geq\int_{-\infty}^{-1}\frac{C}{(-t)e^{-\alpha_{0}t}}e^{-\beta t}\,dt=C\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{e^{(\beta-\alpha_{0})s}}{s}\,ds.

Thus we must have
\alpha_{0}>\beta
(otherwise we would get
\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s}<\infty
).
∎

In the previous proof we used the following Donelly-Fefferman lemma:

Donnelly-Fefferman Lemma.

If
\alpha_{0}<\infty
then
\inf_{t<-1}\{(-t)e^{-\alpha_{0}t}||F||_{t}^{2}\}>0
.

Proof.

Put

I_{0}:=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathbb{B}}|\tilde{F}|^{2}:(\tilde{F}-F)_{0}\in\bigcup_{\alpha>\alpha_{0}}\mathcal{I}(\alpha G)_{0}\right\}.

If
\alpha_{0}<\infty
then the strong Noetherian property of coherent ideal sheaves gives
I_{0}>0
(this is a well-known result, see
[
40
, Lemma 2.1]
). On the other hand, the Donnelly-Fefferman estimate, Theorem
3.3.3
, gives

I_{0}\leq(2-8\lambda te^{-\alpha_{0}t})||F||_{t}^{2},\ \ \forall\ t<0,

from which the lemma follows.
∎

The optimal estimate

(3.3.16)

\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{0}-\beta}\geq\frac{I_{0}}{\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\,e^{-\beta G}}

was first proved by Guan
[
46
]
using
[
49
, Lemma 2.1]
; an effective but non-optimal estimate is also given in
[
49
]
. Another proof of Guan’s optimal estimate can be given using our main theorem, Theorem
3.1.1
. In fact, Theorem
3.2.1
directly implies (see the next section for details)

I_{0}\leq e^{-\alpha_{0}t}||F||_{t}^{2},

which gives

\frac{I_{0}}{\alpha_{0}-\beta}\geq\int_{-\infty}^{0}||F||_{t}^{2}e^{-\beta t}\,dt=\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda-\beta)\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\,e^{-\beta G}-\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}.

Letting
\lambda\to\infty
, we get

\frac{I_{0}}{\alpha_{0}-\beta}\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\,e^{-\beta G}-\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{B}}|F|^{2}\,e^{-\beta G}-\frac{1}{\beta}I_{0},

from which (
3.3.16
) follows.

3.3.3.
A generalization of Guan’s sharp strong openness theorem

In this section, we shall show how to use the main theorem, Theorem
3.1.1
, to prove the following generalization of Guan’s sharp estimate (
3.3.16
) for the strong openness theorem; below
q=0
, for which we write
H_{0}
as
H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L)
:

Theorem 3.3.5
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a pseudoeffective line bundle on an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kähler manifold
X
. Let
G\leq 0
on
X
such that
\phi+\lambda G
is psh for some constant
\lambda>1
. Fix
F\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L)
. Assume that

||F||^{2}_{\phi+\beta G}:=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi-\beta G}<\infty

for some constant
0<\beta<\lambda
. For every compact subset
K
of
X
, put

(3.3.17)

\alpha_{K}:=\sup\left\{0\leq\alpha\leq\lambda:F_{x}\in\mathcal{I}(\phi+\alpha G),\ \forall\,x\in K\right\}.

Assume that
\alpha_{K}<\lambda
. Put

(3.3.18)

I_{K}:=\inf\left\{||\tilde{F}||_{\phi}^{2}:\tilde{F}\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L),\,\tilde{F}_{x}-F_{x}\in\bigcup_{\alpha>\alpha_{K}}\mathcal{I}(\phi+\alpha G)_{x},\ \forall\,x\in K\right\},

then

(3.3.19)

\alpha_{K}-\beta\geq\frac{I_{K}}{\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda-\beta)\beta}||F||^{2}_{\phi+\beta G}-\frac{1}{\beta}||F||^{2}_{\phi}}>0.

Proof.

Since
\alpha_{K}<\lambda
, the proof of
[
40
, Lemma 2.1]
gives
I_{K}>0
. Thus it suffices to prove the estimate in (
3.3.19
). For
\alpha_{K}<\alpha<\lambda
, put

||[F]||_{t}^{2}:=\inf\left\{||\tilde{F}||_{t}^{2}:\tilde{F}-F\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi+\alpha G))\right\},

where
||\tilde{F}||_{t}^{2}:=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}\tilde{F}\wedge\overline{\tilde{F}}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}
,
we have

(3.3.20)

||[F]||_{0}^{2}\geq I_{K}.

By our main theorem, Theorem
3.1.1
, we know that
e^{-\alpha t}||[F]||_{t}^{2}
is decreasing in
t
. Hence

\int_{-\infty}^{0}||[F]||_{t}^{2}\,e^{-t\beta}\,dt\geq\int_{-\infty}^{0}||[F]||_{0}^{2}\,e^{t(\alpha-\beta)}dt

and (
3.3.20
) gives

\int_{-\infty}^{0}||[F]||_{t}^{2}\,e^{-t\beta}\,dt\geq\frac{I_{K}}{\alpha-\beta}.

Integrating

||[F]||_{t}^{2}\geq\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}

and applying (
3.3.10
) we have

\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda-\beta)\beta}||F||^{2}_{\phi+\beta G}-\frac{1}{\beta}||F||^{2}_{\phi}\geq\frac{I_{K}}{\alpha-\beta},\ \ \forall\ \alpha_{K}<\alpha<\lambda.

Letting
\alpha
go to
\alpha_{K}
, the estimate in (
3.3.19
) follows.
∎

In the case that
\beta=0
, (
3.3.10
) degenerates to

(3.3.21)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-\lambda\max\{s-t,0\}}\,dt=\frac{1}{\lambda}-s,\ \ \forall\ s\leq 0

and we have

(3.3.22)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}||F||_{t}^{2}\,dt=\frac{1}{\lambda}||F||^{2}_{\phi}+\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}(-G)\,F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi},

thus a similar proof gives the following result:

Theorem 3.3.6
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a pseudoeffective line bundle on an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kähler manifold
X
. Let
G\leq 0
on
X
such that
\phi+\lambda G
is psh for some constant
\lambda>1
. Fix
F\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L)
. Assume that

||F||^{2}_{\phi}:=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}<\infty,\ \ \int_{X}i^{n^{2}}(-G)\,F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}<\infty.

For every compact subset
K
of
X
, put

(3.3.23)

\alpha_{K}:=\sup\left\{0\leq\alpha\leq\lambda:F_{x}\in\mathcal{I}(\phi+\alpha G),\ \forall\,x\in K\right\}.

Assume that
\alpha_{K}<\lambda
. Put

(3.3.24)

I_{K}:=\inf\left\{||\tilde{F}||_{\phi}^{2}:\tilde{F}\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L),\,\tilde{F}_{x}-F_{x}\in\bigcup_{\alpha>\alpha_{K}}\mathcal{I}(\phi+\alpha G)_{x},\ \forall\,x\in K\right\},

then

(3.3.25)

\alpha_{K}\geq\frac{I_{K}}{\frac{1}{\lambda}||F||^{2}_{\phi}+\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}(-G)\,F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}}>0.

3.4.
The Berndtsson-Lempert approach for the Ohsawa-Takegoshi theory

3.4.1.
Vanishing theorems and extension of holomorphic sections

In this section we discuss further applications, now to the Ohsawa-Takegoshi theory and sharp effective estimates in the
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension theorem. The starting point of the story is the following Nadel vanishing theorem (see the proof of Theorem 5.11 and Theorem 6.25 in
[
25
]
):

Theorem 3.4.1
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a holomorphic line bundle with smooth metric (i.e.
\phi
is smooth) on a weakly
pseudoconvex Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
. Assume that there exists an upper semi-continuous function
G
on
X
such that

i\partial\overline{\partial}(\phi+G)\geq\epsilon\,\omega

for some continuous positive function
\varepsilon
on X. Then

(3.4.1)

H^{q}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))=0,\ \ \forall\ q\geq 1.

It is known that the short exact sequence
0\to I_{0}\to I_{1}\to I_{2}\to 0
, where

I_{0}:=\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi+G),\ \ I_{1}:=\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{J},\ \ I_{2}:=\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{J}/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G),

and
\mathcal{J}\supset\mathcal{I}(\phi+G)
is a coherent ideal sheaf of
\mathcal{O}(X)
,
induces a long exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(I_{0})\to H^{0}(I_{1})\to H^{0}(I_{2})\to H^{1}(I_{0})\to H^{1}(I_{1})\to\cdots.

Hence (
3.4.1
) implies the following extension theorem:

Theorem 3.4.2
.

With the notation above,

H^{q}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{J})\to H^{q}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{J}/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))

is surjective for every
q\geq 0
.

In general,
H^{1}(I_{0})=0
is strictly stronger than the surjectivity of
H^{0}(I_{1})\to H^{0}(I_{2})
. Hence one may expect a weaker conditions (without assuming that
\phi+G
is strictly psh) for surjectivity. Such a result is first obtained by Demailly
[
27
]
in the compact case, and later generalizations include Cao-Demailly-Matsumura
[
20
]
(when
G
has neat analytic singularity in Theorem
3.4.3
) and Zhou-Zhu
[
43
]
. The last-mentioned theorem is the following:

Theorem 3.4.3
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a holomorphic line bundle with quasi-psh metric (i.e.
\phi
is quasi-psh) on a Kähler manifold
X
that possesses a proper holomorphic mapping to
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Let
G
be locally bounded above on
X
such that

\phi+G
and
\phi+\lambda G
are psh

for some positive constant
\lambda>1
. Then

H^{q}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))\to H^{q}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))

is surjective for every
q\geq 0
.

Remark.

In the case that
q=0
, Theorem
3.4.3
implies that every holomorphic section of
\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)
over the support scheme of
\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G)
extends to
X
. The theory on the effective
L^{2}
estimate of those extensions is usually known as the Ohsawa-Takegoshi theory
[
37
,
36
]
.

3.4.2.
The Ohsawa-Takegoshi theory

In this section, we discuss the Ohsawa-Takegoshi theory, and prove a sharp version of the
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension theorem using our main theorem. The first part of the story concerns a certain
L^{2}
-norm on
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))
. The idea is to use the following isomorphism

(3.4.2)

H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))\simeq\frac{\{u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi}^{n,0}:\overline{\partial}u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,1}\}}{\{u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,0}:\overline{\partial}u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,1}\}},

where
\mathcal{A}_{\psi}^{n,k}
denotes the space of smooth
L
-valued
(n,k)
-forms on
X
whose coefficients are locally square integrable with respect to
e^{-\psi}
. The above isomorphism allows us to write an element in
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))
as an equivalence class
\{F\}
, where

F\in\{u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi}^{n,0}:\overline{\partial}u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,1}\}.

The surjectivity of

H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))\to H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))

is equivalent to the existence of

\tilde{F}\in\{u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi}^{n,0}:\overline{\partial}u=0\}=H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))

such that

\tilde{F}-F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,0}.

That is, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 3.4.4
.

The surjectivity of

H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))\to H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))

is equivalent to that for every
F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi}^{n,0}
with
\overline{\partial}F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,1}
, we can find

\tilde{F}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))

such that
\tilde{F}-F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,0}.

The previous lemma suggests to use Hörmander
L^{2}
estimate for
\overline{\partial}
to study the effective extension theorem. The following general theorem is proved by Zhou-Zhu in
[
43
]
(see also
[
14
,
21
,
22
]
for some recent related results):

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a holomorphic line bundle, with
\phi
quasi-psh, over an
n
-dimensional weakly
pseudoconvex Kähler manifold
X
. Let
G\leq 0
on
X
such that

\phi+G
and
\phi+\lambda G
are psh

for some constant
\lambda>1
on
X
. Then for every
F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi}^{n,0}
with
\overline{\partial}F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,1}
and

(3.4.3)

||F||_{G}^{2}:=\sup_{K\ \text{compact in}\ X}\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\int_{\{t<G<t+1\}\cap K}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\overline{F}\,e^{-(\phi+G)}<\infty,

there exists
\tilde{F}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))
such that
\tilde{F}-F\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,0}
and

(3.4.4)

\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}\tilde{F}\wedge\overline{\tilde{F}}\,e^{-\phi}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}||F||^{2}_{G}.

Remark
.

Ohsawa
[
36
]
further observed that one may use (
3.4.2
) to define a norm for
\{F\}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))
. In fact, for
F_{1},F_{2}\in\{u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi}^{n,0}:\overline{\partial}u\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,1}\}
with

F_{1}-F_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{\phi+G}^{n,0},

we have

(3.4.5)

\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\int_{\{t<G<t+1\}\cap K}i^{n^{2}}F_{1}\wedge\overline{F_{1}}\,e^{-(\phi+G)}=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\int_{\{t<G<t+1\}\cap K}i^{n^{2}}F_{2}\wedge\overline{F_{2}}\,e^{-(\phi+G)}

for every compact set
K
in
X
, which implies that

||F_{1}||^{2}_{G}=||F_{2}||^{2}_{G},\ \ \text{if}\ \{F_{1}\}=\{F_{2}\}.

The previous remark shows thus that

(3.4.6)

||\{F\}||^{2}:=||F||^{2}_{G}

is well-defined. We may therefore rephrase Theorem
3.4.5
as follows (see
[
43
, Theorem 1.2,
R(t)=e^{-t}
]
):

Theorem 3.4.6
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a holomorphic line bundle, with
\phi
quasi-psh, over an
n
-dimensional weakly
pseudoconvex Kähler manifold
X
. Let
G\leq 0
on
X
such that

\phi+G
and
\phi+\lambda G
are psh

for some constant
\lambda>1
on
X
. Then every
\{F\}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))
with
||\{F\}||^{2}<\infty
has an extension
\tilde{F}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))
with

(3.4.7)

\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}\tilde{F}\wedge\overline{\tilde{F}}\,e^{-\phi}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}||\{F\}||^{2}.

3.4.3.
The Berndtsson-Lempert method for
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension

A direct corollary of our main theorem, Theorem
3.1.1
, is the following theorem on; for relation with what we have discussed above,
q=0
below, but the theorem immediately generalizes to also the case that
q\geq 0
:

Theorem 3.4.7
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a pseudoeffective line bundle on an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
. Let
G\leq 0
be a function on
X
such that

\phi+\lambda G
is psh for some constant
\lambda>1
.
Fix

F\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L)
with

\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}<\infty
. Then there exists

\tilde{F}\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L)

such that

\tilde{F}-F\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))

and

\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}\tilde{F}\wedge\overline{\tilde{F}}\,e^{-\phi}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1.1
,

t\mapsto A(t):=e^{-t}\inf\left\{\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}\tilde{F}\wedge\overline{\tilde{F}}\,e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}:\tilde{F}-F\in S_{1}\right\}

is decreasing in
t\in\mathbb{R}
. It suffices to show that

(3.4.8)

A(0)\leq\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}.

But notice that

A(t)\leq e^{-t}\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\overline{F}\,e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}.

Hence, the decreasingness property of
A(t)
implies

A(0)\leq\lim_{t\to-\infty}A(t)\leq\liminf_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\overline{F}\,e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}},

and the calculus lemma below gives (
3.4.8
).
∎

In the previous proof we used the following calculus lemma:

Lemma 3.4.8
.

Put
||F||^{2}_{t}:=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}.
Then

\limsup_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}||F||_{t}^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}.

Proof.

Consider a positive Borel measure
\mu
on
X
defined by

d\mu:=i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}.

Then

\mu(X)=\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi},\ \ \ \mu(G<s)=\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi},

and we have

||F||_{t}^{2}=\int_{X}e^{-\lambda\max\{G-t,0\}}\,d\mu=e^{\lambda t}\mu(X)-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mu(G<s)\,d\,e^{-\lambda\max\{s-t,0\}},

Since
\mu(X)
is finite and
\lambda>1
, we have

\lim_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}e^{\lambda t}\mu(X)=0,

hence

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}||F||_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\left(-e^{-t}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mu(G<s)\,d\,e^{-\lambda\max\{s-t,0\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\left(-e^{-t}\int_{t}^{0}\mu(G<s)\,d\,e^{-\lambda(s-t)}\right)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\left(\lambda\int_{t}^{0}\mu(G<s)e^{-s}e^{-(\lambda-1)(s-t)}\,ds\right)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\left(\lambda\int_{0}^{-t}\mu(G<x+t)e^{-(x+t)}e^{-(\lambda-1)x}\,dx\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\lambda-1)x}\,dx\right)\limsup_{s\to-\infty}\left(\mu(G<s)e^{-s}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi},

gives the lemma.
∎

Finally, we may also rephrase the Theorem
3.4.7
as a sharp Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem:

Theorem 3.4.9
.

Let
(L,e^{-\phi})
be a pseudoeffective line bundle on an
n
-dimensional quasi-complete Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
. Let
G\leq 0
be a function on
X
such that

\phi+\lambda G
is psh for some constant
\lambda>1
.
Fix
\{F\}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi)/\mathcal{I}(\phi+G))
. If
\{F\}
has an extension
F_{1}\in H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}(K_{X}+L)\otimes\mathcal{I}(\phi))
with

\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F\wedge\bar{F}\,e^{-\phi}<\infty
then we can choose an extension
F_{2}
such that

\int_{X}i^{n^{2}}F_{2}\wedge\overline{F_{2}}\,e^{-\phi}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-1}\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F_{1}\wedge\overline{F_{1}}\,e^{-\phi}.

We end our paper with the following remark:

Remark
.

Assume that

(3.4.9)

\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{\{G<s\}\cap K}i^{n^{2}}F_{1}\wedge\overline{F_{1}}\,e^{-\phi}=\liminf_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{\{G<s\}\cap K}i^{n^{2}}F_{1}\wedge\overline{F_{1}}\,e^{-\phi}

for every compact set
K
in
X
, then we have

||F_{1}||_{G}^{2}=\sup_{K\ \text{compact in}\ X}\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{\{G<s\}\cap K}i^{n^{2}}F_{1}\wedge\overline{F_{1}}\,e^{-\phi}.

Hence

(3.4.10)

||F_{1}||_{G}^{2}\leq\limsup_{s\to-\infty}e^{-s}\int_{G<s}i^{n^{2}}F_{1}\wedge\overline{F_{1}}\,e^{-\phi}.

Thus if
X
is weakly pseudoconvex and (
3.4.9
) holds then the above theorem follows from Theorem
3.4.5
.
But even in that special case the proofs are quite different. The main idea in our proof comes from
[
13
, page 5]
(
\lambda
is denoted by
p
there). The Berndtsson-Lempert approach in
[
13
]
is to first let
\lambda
go to infinity, then generalize Theorem
3.2.2
to the non-product family
\{G<t\}
. Thus the have to assume that
\phi+\lambda G
is psh for all positive constant
\lambda
. This means that both
\phi
and
G
are psh. Hence the method in
[
13
]
does not directly apply to the compact Kähler case (since there is no non-trivial psh
G
there). In order to generalize the approach in
[
13
]
to the compact Kähler case, we rephrased the proof of
[
13
, Theorem 3.8]
in
[
41
, Section 10]
. Eventually we realized that (see
[
41
, Section 10.4]
) our new formulation of
[
13
, Theorem 3.8]
is equivalent to Theorem
3.2.2
for a
fixed

\lambda>1
. This observation finally leads to the above generalization of the Berndtsson–Lempert theorem; see also
[
31
,
18
,
39
]
for other recent applications of the Berndtsson–Lempert approach.

Chapter 4
Paper 4

ON A REMARK BY OHSAWA RELATED TO THE BERNDTSSON-LEMPERT METHOD FOR
L^{2}
-HOLOMORPHIC EXTENSION

TAI TERJE HUU NGUYEN AND XU WANG

4.1.
Introduction

In
[
35
]
Ohsawa gave a new proof of two theorems of Guan-Zhou (see
[
47
,
54
,
50
]
for further details and related results), Theorem
4.1.1
and
4.1.2
below, and asked in a remark (remark 4.1 in
[
35
]
) whether a proof using the Berndtsson-Lempert method in
[
13
]
can be had.

Theorem 4.1.1

(Theorem 4.1 in
[
35
]
and Corollary 1.8 in
[
51
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a pseudoconvex domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\Omega^{\prime}:=\{(z^{\prime},z_{n})\in\Omega:z_{n}=0\}
,
\phi
a plurisubharmonic function on
\Omega
,
f\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{\prime})
a holomorphic function on
\Omega^{\prime}
, and let
\alpha>0
. Then there exists
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
a holomorphic function on
\Omega
such that
\tilde{f}|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=f
and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi-\alpha|z_{n}|^{2}}\leq\frac{\pi}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi},

where integration is with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

Theorem 4.1.2

(Theorem 0.1 in
[
35
]
)
.

With the same assumptions and notation as in Theorem
4.1.1
, there exists
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
such that
\tilde{f}|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=f
and satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi}}{(1+|z_{n}|^{2})^{1+\alpha}}\leq\frac{\pi}{\alpha}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi}.

In this paper we answer Ohsawa’s question affirmatively by proving an extension theorem with estimate using the Berndtsson-Lempert method. This is Theorem
4.1.3
below and the main theorem of this paper. A key ingredient in our proof is the construction of a weight function
\theta
using the Legendre-Fenchel transform and weak geodesics for plurisubharmonic functions (see the appendix in Section
4.5
) to which we can apply the Berndtsson-Lempert technique.

Below we use the following terminology. Let
\sigma:U\to\mathbb{R},\;x\mapsto\sigma(x)
be a real-valued function where
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is some subset and we write
x:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. We will say that
\sigma
is increasing if
\sigma
is separately increasing in each argument. That is, more precisely, if for each
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, the one-variable function

t\mapsto\sigma(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1},t,x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{n})
is increasing for all
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1},t,x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{n})\in U
.

Theorem 4.1.3

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
\Omega
be a pseudoconvex domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
1\leq k\leq n
an integer,

\Omega^{\prime}:=\{(z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime})\in\Omega:z^{\prime\prime}=0\},\ \ \ z^{\prime\prime}:=(z_{n-k+1},\cdots,z_{n}),

\phi
a plurisubharmonic function on
\Omega
,
f\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{\prime})
a holomorphic function on
\Omega^{\prime}
, and
\sigma
a convex increasing function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then there exists
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
a holomorphic function on
\Omega
such that
\tilde{f}|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=f
and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi-\sigma(\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|)}\leq\int_{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}e^{-\sigma(\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|)}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi},

where we write
\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|:=(\ln|z_{n-k+1}|,\cdots,\ln|z_{n}|)
,
\Omega^{\prime\prime}:=\{z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{k}:\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}\}
,
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the convex hull of
\{\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|:z\in\Omega\}
, and integration is with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

In the case that
\sigma
depends only on
|z^{\prime\prime}|^{2}
, Theorem
4.1.3
reduces to
[
54
, Theorem 1.7]
. It is also very likely that the main theorem in
[
47
]
implies Theorem
4.1.3
. So our contribution is not the theorem itself, but rather a method of proof using the Berndtsson-Lempert method.

4.2.
The Berndtsson-Lempert method in a simple setting

We start by recalling quickly the Berndtsson-Lempert method for
L^{2}
-holomorphic extension (
[
13
]
) in the simple setting of domains in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
as it will apply to us. Specifically, we consider the following set-up. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
be a pseudoconvex domain and let
\phi
be a plurisubharmonic function on
\Omega
. For
z\in\Omega
, we write
z=(z^{\prime},z_{n})
. Let
\Omega^{\prime}:=\{z\in\Omega:z_{n}=0\}
, suppose that
f\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{\prime})
is a given holomorphic function on
\Omega^{\prime}
, and suppose that
|z_{n}|<1
on
\Omega
. We are interested in finding a holomorphic extension
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
of
f
defined on all of
\Omega
for which we have a good weighted
L^{2}
estimate of the form (
[
37
]
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi}\leq C\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi},

where integration is with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and
C
is some universal constant. The Berndtsson-Lempert method demonstrated in
[
13
]
is the following. Fix a positive constant
j>1
and consider the following plurisubharmonic function

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle\theta:\mathbb{C}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\quad(\tau,z)\mapsto\theta(\tau,z):=\theta^{{\rm Re}\,\tau}(z):=\phi+j\max\{\ln|z_{n}|^{2}-{\rm Re}\,\tau,0\}.

For each
t:={\rm Re}\,\tau\leq 0
,
\theta^{t}
provides a weight function for a weighted
L^{2}
inner product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{t}
and the corresponding induced weighted
L^{2}
norm
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}
, given by

\displaystyle\left\langle u,v\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Omega}u\bar{v}e^{-\theta^{t}},\quad||u||^{2}_{t}:=\left\langle u,u\right\rangle_{t}.

Since
|z_{n}|<1
on
\Omega
, we have
\theta^{0}=\phi
. Let
f_{t}
denote the holomorphic extension of
f
with minimal
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}
-norm. Then we can write
[
13
, section 3]

\displaystyle\left|\left|f_{t}\right|\right|^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sup_{g\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}\frac{|\xi_{g}(F)|^{2}}{\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|^{2}_{t}},\quad\xi_{g}(F):=\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}F\bar{g}e^{-\phi},

where
F
denotes
any
holomorphic extension of
f
, and where
\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}
denotes the (squared) dual norm of the continuous linear functional
\xi_{g}
, given by

\displaystyle\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left|\left|F\right|\right|_{t}=1}|\xi_{g}(F)|^{2}.

The crux of the argument is that by complex Brunn-Minkowski theory (see e.g.
[
7
,
4
,
2
,
3
]
), under the assumption that
\theta
is plurisubharmonic, we have that
t\mapsto\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}
is log-convex. Hence
t\mapsto e^{t}\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}
is log-convex. Suppose that this is also bounded as
t\to-\infty
. Then
t\mapsto t+\ln(\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2})
is convex and bounded as
t\to-\infty
, and hence increasing. Taking the exponential, we infer that
t\mapsto e^{t}\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}
is increasing, which implies that
t\mapsto e^{-t}\left|\left|f_{t}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}
is decreasing. Thus, we get the inequality

\displaystyle\left|\left|f_{0}\right|\right|^{2}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}\left|\left|f_{t}\right|\right|^{2}_{t}.

Since
f_{t}
is the extension of
f
with
minimal

\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}
-norm, the right-hand side limit is bounded by the limit of
e^{-t}\left|\left|F\right|\right|_{t}^{2}
as
t\to-\infty
with
F

any
extension as above. Writing
f_{0}:=\tilde{f}
and recalling that we are assuming
\theta^{0}=\phi
, we therefore find

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}\left|\left|F\right|\right|_{t}^{2}=\liminf_{t\to-\infty}e^{-t}\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}e^{-\theta^{t}}.

By
[
13
, Lemma 3.2, Lemma 3.3]
, as
j\to\infty
, the right-hand side limit converges to
\pi\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi}
. Thus, we get an extension
\tilde{f}
with the following estimate:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\pi\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi}.

This estimate is known to be optimal (we have equality in the case
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\{|z_{n}|<1\}
and
\phi
does not depend on
z_{n}
) and was first proved in
[
19
,
47
]
.

4.3.
A solution to Ohsawa’s question

In this section we apply the Berndtsson-Lempert method in Section
4.2
to a weight function
\theta
that is different from the one in (
4.2.1
) to prove Theorem
4.3.1
below.
Notice that taking

\sigma=\alpha e^{2x}
or
\sigma=(1+\alpha)\ln(1+e^{2x})
we get
\mathrm{L}=\pi/\alpha
in Theorem
4.3.1
. Hence Theorem
4.1.1
and
4.1.2
follow. Our construction of
\theta
is based on a Legendre-Fenchel transform approach to weak geodesics for plurisubharmonic functions (see the appendix in Section
4.5
).

Theorem 4.3.1
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
be pseudoconvex and write for
z\in\Omega
,
z=(z^{\prime},z_{n})
. Let
\phi
be a plurisubharmonic function in
\Omega
,
\Omega^{\prime}:=\{z\in\Omega:z_{n}=0\}
, and
f\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{\prime})
a holomorphic function on
\Omega^{\prime}
. Put
x:=\ln|z_{n}|
and let
\sigma=\sigma(x)
be a
convex and increasing function in
x
with the property that
\mathrm{L}:=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\sigma(\ln|w|)}<\infty
. Then there exists
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
a holomorphic function on
\Omega
such that
\tilde{f}|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=f
and

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi-\sigma(\ln|z_{n}|)}\leq\mathrm{L}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi}.

Proof.

Fix
c\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the weight function
\theta
defined by

\theta(\tau,z):=\theta^{{\rm Re}\,\tau}(z):=\phi(z)+\psi^{{\rm Re}\,\tau}(\ln|z_{n}|),\ \ \ t={\rm Re}\,\tau>0,

where

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle\psi^{t}(x):=\psi(t,x)

\displaystyle:=t\sigma\left(\frac{x-c}{t}+c\right).

One may directly verify that (see also the appendix in Section
4.5
for another approach)
\psi
is convex in
(t,x)
and increasing with respect to
x
. Since
\phi
is assumed to be plurisubharmnic, we know that
\theta
defined above is plurisubharmonic in
(\tau,z)
. By the Berndtsson-Lempert method, using the same notation as in Section
4.2
, it follows that

\rho_{c}(t):=\ln||\xi_{g}||^{2}_{t}-2c(t-1)

is convex as a function of
t
. Hence for all
t\in[1/2,1)
,

\frac{\rho_{c}(1)-\rho_{c}(t)}{1-t}\geq\frac{\rho_{c}(1)-\rho_{c}(1/2)}{1-1/2}.

Observe that
\rho_{c}(1)=\ln||\xi_{g}||^{2}_{1}
does not depend on
c
. Thus,

\ln||\xi_{g}||^{2}_{1}\geq\rho_{c}(t)+2(1-t)\left(\ln||\xi_{g}||^{2}_{1}-\rho_{c}(1/2)\right),

for all
t\in[1/2,1)

and
for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Now let

c:=\frac{-1}{(1-t)^{2}},

and let
t\uparrow 1
. Lemma
4.3.2
below implies that

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle\ln||\xi_{g}||^{2}_{1}\geq\limsup_{t\uparrow 1}\rho_{c}(t).

Hence, by (
4.3.3
), we have a holomorphic extension
\tilde{f}
with the following estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi-\sigma(\ln|z_{n}|)}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\uparrow 1}e^{2c(t-1)}\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}e^{-\phi-\psi^{t}(\ln|z_{n}|)}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{t\uparrow 1}e^{2/(1-t)}\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}e^{-\phi-t\sigma\left(\frac{\ln|z_{n}|+\frac{1}{1-t}}{t}\right)},

where
F
is any arbitrary holomorphic extension. From the change of variables

z_{n}=e^{\frac{1}{t-1}}w,\ \ \Omega_{t}:=\{(z^{\prime},w)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:(z^{\prime},e^{\frac{1}{t-1}}w)\in\Omega\},

and the definition of
\mathrm{L}
, we find that letting
t\uparrow 1
,

e^{2/(1-t)}\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}e^{-\phi-t\sigma\left(\frac{\ln|z_{n}|+\frac{1}{1-t}}{t}\right)}=\int_{\Omega_{t}}|F(z^{\prime},e^{\frac{1}{t-1}}w)|^{2}e^{-\phi\left(z^{\prime},e^{\frac{1}{t-1}}w\right)-t\sigma\left(\frac{\ln|w|}{t}\right)}

converges to the right hand side of (
4.3.1
). Hence the theorem follows.
∎

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Let
c:=\frac{-1}{(1-t)^{2}}
. Then
\lim_{t\uparrow 1}(1-t)\rho_{c}(1/2)=0
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\limsup_{c\to-\infty}\rho_{c}(1/2)<\infty
. That is,

\limsup_{c\to\infty}\sup_{F\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}\frac{\left|\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}F\bar{g}e^{-\phi}\right|^{2}}{e^{-c}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}e^{-\phi-\frac{1}{2}\sigma\left(2\ln|z_{n}|-c\right)}}<\infty.

This now follows from the change of variables
z_{n}=e^{c/2}w
and the definition of
\mathrm{L}
.
∎

Remark
: The change of variables argument in the proof of Theorem
4.3.1
also suggests to use another weight function, with

(4.3.4)

\psi^{t}(x)=\sigma(x-t),\ \ t\in\mathbb{R}.

In fact, as we shall see next, the proof of Theorem
4.3.1
is simpler if we use this new weight function. Nevertheless, we still wish to include the proof given above since it is our first example of the use of weak geodesics in the Berndtsson-Lempert method.

4.3.1.
Proof of the main theorem (Theorem
4.1.3
)

Since
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}^{n-k}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, one may assume that
\sigma=\infty
outside
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Also we may assume that the right-hand side in the estimate in the theorem is finite, else there is nothing to prove.
Replace
\psi^{t}
in (
4.3.2
) by

(4.3.5)

\psi^{t}(x)=\sigma(x-t),\ \ t\in\mathbb{R},\ \ x-t:=(x_{1}-t,\cdots,x_{k}-t).

It is clear that
\psi^{t}(x)
in convex in
(t,x)
and increasing in
x
. Hence, the corresponding weight function

\theta(\tau,z)=\phi(z)+\psi^{{\rm Re}\,\tau}(\ln|z_{n}|)

is plurisubharmonic in
(\tau,z)
, and the Berndtsson-Lempert method applies. Thus, we know that
t\mapsto\ln\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}-2kt
is convex. Moreover, the change of variables
z^{\prime\prime}=e^{t}w
gives,

(4.3.6)

\displaystyle e^{2kt}\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}e^{-\phi-\sigma(\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|-t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{(z^{\prime},e^{t}w)\in\Omega\}}|F(z^{\prime},e^{t}w)|^{2}e^{-\phi(z^{\prime},e^{t}w)-\sigma(\ln|w|)},

where
F
, as before, is any arbitrary fixed extension of
f
. From (
4.3.6
) we infer that
\ln\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}-2kt
is also bounded near
t=-\infty
. Hence,
t\mapsto\ln\left|\left|\xi_{g}\right|\right|_{t}^{2}-2kt
is increasing and there exists a holomorphic extension
\tilde{f}
with

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{f}|^{2}e^{-\phi-\sigma(\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|)}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\to-\infty}\int_{\{(z^{\prime},e^{t}w)\in\Omega\}}|F(z^{\prime},e^{t}w)|^{2}e^{-\phi(z^{\prime},e^{t}w)-\sigma(\ln|w|)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}e^{-\sigma(\ln|w|)}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}e^{-\sigma(\ln|w|)}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|^{2}e^{-\phi},

where in the last equality we have used that
\sigma=\infty
outside
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
This completes the proof. ∎

Remark
: From (
4.3.6
), in the case that
\Omega
is horizontally balanced (that is,
(z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime})\in\Omega\Rightarrow(z^{\prime},\tau z^{\prime\prime})\in\Omega
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\tau|<1
), the method above is equivalent to the one associated to the weight function

\theta(\tau,z):=\phi(z^{\prime},\tau z^{\prime\prime})+\sigma(\ln|z^{\prime\prime}|).

This construction has already been used in
[
11
, Section 2]
. The advantage of (
4.3.5
) is that it also applies to general
\Omega
.

4.4.
An application to Bergman kernels asymptotics

As an interesting application of Theorem
4.1.3
, we shall now give a new simple and short proof of a well-known asymptotic lower bound for the Bergman kernel.

Let
\Omega
be a pseudoconvex domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
which contains the origin and let
\phi
be a twice continuously differentiable plurisubharmonic function on
\Omega
such that
i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi(0)>0
. Let
k\geq 1
be an integer and let
K_{k}(0)
denote the Bergman kernel on
\Omega
with weight
e^{-k\phi}
on the diagonal at the origin. The following is a well-known asymptotic lower bound for
K_{k}(0)
:

Theorem 4.4.1
.

With the above notation,

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{K_{k}(0)e^{-k\phi(0)}}{k^{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi)_{n}(0)}{\pi^{n}}.

One possible proof uses Hörmander
L^{2}
-estimates for the
\overline{\partial}
-equation. We shall here give a new, and in our opinion simpler and shorter, proof, which instead uses Theorem
4.1.3
. Of course, both proofs utilize non-trivial results, the first the
L^{2}
-estimates of Hörmander, and the second, an Ohsawa-Takegoshi type of extension theorem. The point is that the proof that we shall discuss is simpler and shorter given both these results. To prove the extension theorem that we shall employ, we used the Berndtsson-Lempert method. Our proof here is therefore complex Brunn-Minkowski theoretical.

4.4.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.4.1

Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
denote the eigenvalues of the complex Hessian of
\phi
at the origin. By a unitary change of variables, we may assume that
i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi=i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j}
. Here, we let
z:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
be generic coordinates on
\Omega
. The key observation is the following simple identity. Let
x:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):=\ln\left|z\right|:=(\ln\left|z_{1}\right|,\ldots,\ln\left|z_{n}\right|)
, and let
\Delta(0,r)
, where
r:=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
be the polydisc centred at the origin with polyradius
r
. Then

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta(0,r)}e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}\left|z_{j}\right|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{n}}{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n}}\left(1-e^{-\lambda_{1}r_{1}^{2}}\right)\cdots\left(1-e^{-\lambda_{n}r_{n}^{2}}\right).

Note that this tends to the inverse of the right-hand side in Theorem
4.4.1
. Let
\epsilon>0
. By continuity of
\partial\overline{\partial}\phi
at the origin, there are
\delta_{j}>0
such that if
\left|z_{j}\right|<\delta_{j}
, for each
j
, we have
i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi(z)-i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j}>-i\epsilon\partial\overline{\partial}\left|z\right|^{2}
. We actually want the left-hand side to be positive, so we replace
\lambda_{j}
with some smaller
\eta_{j}
; the idea is that we can let
\eta_{j}\to\lambda_{j}
in the limit later when we let
\epsilon\to 0
. More precisely, let
\eta_{j}
, for each
j
, be such that
\lambda_{j}-\eta_{j}>2\frac{\epsilon}{n}
. Then it follows that on
\Delta(0,\delta)
, where we write
\delta:=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n})
,

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi(z)-i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j}>0.

It follows that if we define
\sigma_{k}:=k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\left|z_{j}\right|^{2}
on
\Delta(0,\delta)
, then, there,

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle\psi_{k}

\displaystyle:=k\phi-\sigma_{k}

is plurisubharmonic. The idea is to define
\sigma_{k}
outside
\Delta(0,\delta)
such that
\psi_{k}
is plurisubharmonic on all of
\Omega
. This is really the crux of the proof and we do this as follows. We define
\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{k}(\ln\left|z\right|):=f_{k}(x)
, to be a function of
\left|z\right|
, where
x=\ln\left|z\right|
as above. What we need to define is then
f_{k}
on
\{x_{j}\geq\ln\left|\delta_{j}\right|\}
. We will define
f_{k}
to be the tangent space of
x\mapsto k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}e^{2x_{j}}\left(=k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\left|z_{j}\right|^{2}\right)
. That is, we define
f_{k}=f_{k}(x)
in general by

\displaystyle f_{k}(x)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}e^{2x_{j}},&\{x_{j}<\ln\left|\delta_{j}\right|\}\\
k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\delta_{j}^{2}+2k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\delta_{j}^{2}(x_{j}-\ln\left|\delta_{j}\right|),&\{x_{j}\geq\ln\left|\delta_{j}\right|\}.\end{array}\right.

Let
\delta_{k}:=(\delta_{k,1},\ldots,\delta_{k,n})
be such that
\delta_{k,j}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Then for sufficiently large
k
,
\Delta(0,\delta)\subseteq\Delta(0,\delta)
. We choose
k
sufficiently large such that this holds and replace above
\delta
everywhere with
\delta_{k}
. Hence, we define

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}(z)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\left|z_{j}\right|^{2},&z\in\Delta(0,\delta_{k})\\
k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\left(\delta_{k,j}^{2}+2\delta_{k,j}^{2}\left(\ln\left|z_{j}\right|-\ln\left|\delta_{k,j}\right|\right)\right),&z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\Delta(0,\delta_{k}).\end{array}\right.

Finally, we let
\psi_{k}:=k\phi-\sigma_{k}
(defined on
\Omega
as
\phi
is). It is clear that
\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{k}(x)
, as a function of
x
, is increasing and convex. Moreover, by construction,
\psi_{k}
is plurisubharmonic and we have
\psi_{k}(0)=k\phi(0)
. Hence, by Theorem
4.1.3
, there exists a holomorphic function
f\in\mathscrsfs{O}(\Omega)
on
\Omega
such that

(4.4.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|f\right|^{2}e^{-k\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}e^{-\sigma_{k}(\ln\left|z\right|)}\right)\left|f(0)\right|^{2}e^{-k\phi(0)}.

Let the parenthesis above be denoted by
L_{k}
; it is easy to see that
L_{k}<\infty
. By definition of
K_{k}(0)
(or, if one wants, by the extremal property of the Bergman kernel), it follows then that

(4.4.10)

\displaystyle\frac{K_{k}(0)e^{-k\phi(0)}}{k^{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{k^{n}L_{k}}.

A simple computation shows that
L_{k}=L_{k}^{(1)}+L_{k}^{(2)}
, where
L_{k}^{(1)}
is given from (
4.4.2
) by

(4.4.11)

\displaystyle L_{k}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Delta(0,\delta_{k})}e^{-\sigma_{k}(\left|z\right|)}=\pi^{n}\frac{\left(1-e^{\eta_{1}k\delta_{k,1}^{2}}\right)\cdots\left(1-e^{-\eta_{n}k\delta_{k,n}^{2}})\right)}{k^{n}\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n}},

and

\displaystyle L_{k}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\Delta(0,\delta_{k})}e^{-k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}-2k\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}\left(\ln\left|z_{j}\right|-\ln\left|\delta_{k,j}\right|\right)}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{e^{-k\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}{k\left(\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}-\frac{1}{k}\right)},

where we choose that
k\delta_{k,j}^{2}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
(which we can certainly do); this is used above to choose
k
so large that
1-2k\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}<0
. Then for such large
k
,

(4.4.12)

\displaystyle k^{n}L_{k}

\displaystyle=\pi^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1-e^{-k\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}}{\lambda_{j}}+\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{e^{-k\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}{\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}-\frac{1}{k}}=\pi^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1-e^{-k\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}}{\lambda_{j}}+\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{e^{-k\eta_{j}\delta_{k,j}^{2}}}{\eta_{j}-\frac{1}{k\delta_{k,j}^{2}}}.

Letting
k\to\infty
, recalling that
\delta_{k,j}\to 0,k\delta_{k,j}^{2}\to\infty
(consider for example
\delta_{k,j}:=1/k^{1/3}
), we can then let
\epsilon\to 0
and
then

\eta_{j}\to\lambda_{j}
, which together with (
4.4.10
) gives

(4.4.13)

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{K_{k}(0)e^{-k\phi(0)}}{k^{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n}}{\pi^{n}}=\frac{(i\partial\overline{\partial}\phi)_{n}(0)}{\pi^{n}}.

This completes the proof for Theorem
4.4.1
. Note that the
actual proof
(that is, without as many details as provided above) is very short (and simple): Define
\psi_{k}:=k\phi-\sigma_{k}
with
\sigma_{k}
defined as in (
4.4.1
) where
\delta_{k,j}\to 0
and
k\delta_{k,j}^{2}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
, and where
k
is so large that (
4.4.3
) holds on
\Delta(0,\delta_{k})
, which is possible by continuity. Since
\sigma_{k}
is defined using the tangent plane, it is convex as a function of
x
and Theorem
4.4.1
follows by (
4.4.10
) using Theorem
4.1.3
.

We end with a small remark that equality actually holds in Theorem
4.4.1
. The opposite inequality (which is much simpler to establish) can be shown by using the mean-value property of holomorphic functions.

4.5.
Appendix: weak geodesics in the space of toric plurisubharmonic functions

By a toric plurisubharmonic function we mean a function of the following form

\bm{\psi}(z):=\psi(\log|z_{1}|,\cdots,\log|z_{n}|)

on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, where
\psi
is a convex increasing function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 4.5.1
.

We call a family, say
\{\bm{\psi}^{t}\}_{0<t<1}
, of toric plurisubharmonic functions a weak geodesic
1
1

1

For usual weak psh geodesics on compact Kähler manfiolds, see
[
1
, Section 2]
. The weak geodesic is also called generalized geodesic in
[
10
, Section 2.2]
, and maximal psh segment or psh geodesic segment in
[
53
, page 5]
. See
[
52
]
for the background.
if

\bm{\Psi}:(\tau,z)\mapsto\bm{\psi}^{{\rm Re}\,\tau}(z),\ \ \tau\in\mathbb{C}_{(0,1)}:=\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}:0<{\rm Re}\,\tau<1\}

is plurisubharmonic on
\mathbb{C}_{(0,1)}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
(i\partial\overline{\partial}\bm{\Psi})^{n+1}=0
on
\mathbb{C}_{(0,1)}\times(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
.

Remark
: Noticing that
\bm{\Psi}
is locally bounded on
\mathbb{C}_{(0,1)}\times(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
, we know that
(i\partial\overline{\partial}\bm{\Psi})^{n+1}
is well defined. Moreover, by a local change of variables
z_{j}=e^{w_{j}}
, we know that
\{\bm{\psi}^{t}\}_{0<t<1}
is a weak geodesic if and only if

\psi(t,x):=\psi^{t}(x)

is convex on
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

MA(\psi):=\det(D^{2}_{(t,x)}\psi)=0

on
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
D^{2}_{(t,x)}\psi
denotes the real Hessian matrix of
\psi
with respect to
(t,x)
. Hence, by Corollary 2.5 in
[
9
]
,
\{\bm{\psi}^{t}\}_{0<t<1}
is a weak geodesic if and only if the Legendre-Fenchel transform of
\psi(t,x)
with respect to
x
for fixed
t
, say

(\psi^{t})^{*}(\xi):=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}x\cdot\xi-\psi^{t}(x),

is an affine function in
t
. This suggest the following definition.

Definition 4.5.2
.

We call a family, say
\{\bm{\psi}^{t}\}_{0<t<1}
, of toric plurisubharmonic functions, a weak geodesic segment if

(\psi^{t})^{*}=t(\sigma^{1})^{*}+(1-t)(\sigma^{0})^{*}

with
\sigma^{1},\sigma^{0}
convex increasing functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now let us consider the case
n=1
. Let
\sigma^{1}:=\sigma
in Theorem
4.3.1
. For
\sigma^{0}
we take

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}:=\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,c]}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}0,&x\leq c\\
\infty,&x>c.\end{array}\right.

Then we have

(\sigma^{0})^{*}(\xi)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}x\xi-\sigma^{0}(x)=\sup_{x\leq c}x\xi=c\xi+\sup_{x\leq 0}x\xi=\begin{cases}c\xi&\xi\geq 0\\
\infty&\xi<0.\end{cases}

Since
\sigma^{1}=\sigma
is also increasing, we know that
(\sigma^{1})^{*}(\xi)=\sigma^{*}(\xi)=\infty
when
\xi<0
. Hence

\psi^{t}
can be written as

\psi^{t}(x)=\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}x\xi-t\sigma^{*}(\xi)-(1-t)c\xi=t\left(\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{x-(1-t)c}{t}\,\xi-\sigma^{*}(\xi)\right),

and
\sigma^{**}=\sigma
gives
\psi(t,x)=t\sigma\left(\frac{x-c}{t}+c\right)
. This
is precisely (
4.3.2
). Note that (
4.3.4
) also gives a weak geodesic since

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}x\xi-\sigma(x-t)=t\xi+\sigma^{*}(\xi)

is affine in
t
.
